On families of strongly divisible modules of rank 2 2
Seongjae Han, Chol Park

Abstract
. Let p p be an odd prime, and 𝐐 p f \mathbf{Q}_{p^{f}} the unramified extension of 𝐐 p \mathbf{Q}_{p} of degree f f . In this paper, we reduce the problem of constructing strongly divisible modules for 2 2 -dimensional semi-stable non-crystalline representations of Gal ⁡ ( 𝐐 ¯ p / 𝐐 p f ) \Gal(\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}/\mathbf{Q}_{p^{f}}) with Hodge–Tate weights in the Fontaine–Laffaille range to solving systems of linear equations and inequalities. We also determine the Breuil modules corresponding to the mod- p p reduction of the strongly divisible modules. We expect our method to produce at least one Galois-stable lattice in each such representation for general f f . Moreover, when the mod- p p reduction is an extension of distinct characters, we further expect our method to provide the two non-homothetic lattices. As applications, we show that our approach recovers previously known results for f = 1 f=1 and determine the mod- p p reduction of the semi-stable representations with some small Hodge–Tate weights when f = 2 f=2 .



1.
Introduction

The significance of the mod-
p
reduction of geometric Galois representations has been demonstrated in various areas of Number Theory. In particular, one can use the mod-
p
reduction to construct Galois deformation rings. The non-existence of certain potentially crystalline lifts (equivalently, the vanishing of certain potentially crystalline deformation rings) can be used to eliminate Sere weights in the weight part of Serre’s conjecture
[
GLS14
,
GLS15
,
MP
,
LMP
,
LLL
]
. Additionally, understanding the special fibers of certain deformation rings can be used to show the modularity of specific Serre weights
[
LHLM18
,
LLL
,
LLMPQ24
]
. Furthermore, it plays a key role in understanding Breuil–Mézard conjecture
[
BM
,
Kis09
,
Pas15
,
CEGS
,
EG14
,
GK14
,
LHLM23
]
, Gelfand–Kirillov dimension
[
BHHMS
]
, mod-
p
local-global compatibility
[
PQ
,
LLMPQa
,
EL
]
, etc.

There are a few known results on mod-
p
reduction of geometric Galois representations. Breuil–Mézard computed the mod-
p
reduction of
2
-dimensional semi-stable representations of
\Gal(\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}/\mathbf{Q}_{p})
with Hodge–Tate weights
(0,r)
when
r
is odd and
0<r<p-1
by constructing the strongly divisible modules
[
BM
]
. This strategy was extended in
[
GP19
,
LP
]
to the case where
r
is even and
0<r<p-1
. Savitt used the same approach to determine the mod-
p
reduction of
2
-dimensional potentially crystalline representations of
\Gal(\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}/\mathbf{Q}_{p})
with Hodge–Tate weights
(0,1)

[
Sav05
]
. Bergdall–Levin–Liu computed the mod-
p
reduction of
2
-dimensional semi-stable representations of
\Gal(\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}/\mathbf{Q}_{p})
with large
\mathfrak{L}
-invariant, by using Breuil–Kisin modules
[
BLL
]
. Buzzard–Gee employed the compatibility between
p
-adic Langlands correspondence and mod-
p
Langlands correspondence to compute the mod-
p
reduction of
2
-dimensional crystalline representations of
\Gal(\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}/\mathbf{Q}_{p})
of slopes in
(0,1)

[
BG09
,
BG13
]
, and Ghate and his collaborators applied similar strategies for the cases of slopes in
[1,2)

[
BhG
,
BGR
,
GaG
,
GRa
]
. Chitrao–Ghate extended the results of
[
BM
,
GP19
,
LP
]
to the Hodge–Tate weights
(0,r)
for
r=p-1,p
, by leveraging the Iwahori mod-
p
local Langlands correspondence
[
CG
]
. There are some notable results as well for
3
-dimensional representations of
\Gal(\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}/\mathbf{Q}_{p})
. The mod-
p
reduction of
3
-dimensional semi-stable representations of
\Gal(\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}/\mathbf{Q}_{p})
with Hodge–Tate weights
(0,1,2)
was studied in
[
Par17
]
constructing strongly divisible modules. Additionally, Liu treated
3
-dimensional crystalline representations of
\Gal(\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}/\mathbf{Q}_{p})
with Hodge–Tate weights
(0,r,s)
for
2\leq r\leq p-2
and
p+2\leq s\leq r+p-2
using Breuil–Kisin modules
[
Liu
]
. Despite these advances, little is known about the mod-
p
reduction of semi-stable representations of
\Gal(\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}/K)
for finite extensions
K
beyond
\mathbf{Q}_{p}
.

Let
\mathbf{Q}_{p^{f}}
be the unramified extension of
\mathbf{Q}_{p}
of degree
f
. In this paper, we study the mod-
p
reduction of
2
-dimensional semi-stable non-crystalline representations of
\Gal(\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}/\mathbf{Q}_{p^{f}})
with Hodge–Tate weights in the Fontaine–Laffaille range. We use strongly divisible modules as in
[
BM
]
, but our approach is slightly different. We first introduce the notion of pseudo-strongly divisible modules of rank
2
, motivated by the anticipated structure of strongly divisible modules. The isotypic components of strongly divisible modules generated by elements of a particular form serve as canonical examples of pseudo-strongly divisible modules; however, the converse is far from true. These pseudo-strongly divisible modules are parameterized by quadruples
(\Lambda,\Theta,\Omega,x)\in(\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}^{\times})^{3}\times\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}
, which naturally appear as coefficients in their construction. We then formulate explicit conditions—given by a system of simple equations and inequalities—under which such a pseudo-strongly divisible module arises as an isotypic component of a genuine strongly divisible module (see
A
and
B
in §
1.1
or Proposition
5.1.25
).

We expect that solving these equations and inequalities yields at least one strongly divisible module of rank
2
corresponding to a Galois-stable lattice in each
2
-dimensional semi-stable non-crystalline representation of
\Gal(\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}/\mathbf{Q}_{p^{f}})
with Hodge–Tate weights in the Fontaine–Laffaille range, for arbitrary
f
. More optimistically, when the mod-
p
reduction is an extension of two distinct characters, we further expect our method to produce both non-homothetic lattices. To support this framework, we verify that it reproduces known results for
f=1

[
BM
,
GP19
,
LP
]
, and we present examples for
f=2
by explicitly solving the associated systems (see §
6
, in particular Remarks
6.2.2
and
6.3.2
).

1.1.
Main results

In this subsection, we introduce our main results. We start by introducing some notation. Let
E
be a finite extension of
\mathbf{Q}_{p^{f}}
with ring of integers
\mathcal{O}
, uniformizer
\varpi
, and residue field
\mathbf{F}
. We will always consider
\mathbf{Q}_{p^{f}}
as a subfield of
E
, and so if
a\in\mathbf{Q}_{p^{f}}
then
a\in E
. Denote by
\mathcal{J}
the cyclic group
\mathbf{Z}/f\mathbf{Z}
which we identify with the set
\Hom_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}}(\mathbf{Q}_{p^{f}},E)
by associating
1
with the geometric Frobenius. Fix an isomorphism

\mathbf{Z}_{p^{f}}\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}\mathcal{O}\cong\bigoplus_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\mathcal{O}:a\otimes b\mapsto(\varphi^{-j}(a)b)_{j\in\mathcal{J}},

where
\varphi
is the arithmetic Frobenius on
\mathbf{Q}_{p^{f}}
and
\mathbf{Z}_{p^{f}}
is the ring of integers of
\mathbf{Q}_{p^{f}}
. For each
j\in\mathcal{J}
, let
e_{j}
be the idempotent element in
\mathbf{Z}_{p^{f}}\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}\mathcal{O}
corresponding to the element
(0,\cdots,0,1,0,\cdots,0)\in\bigoplus_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\mathcal{O}
, under the isomorphism above, where the non-zero entry appears at the
j
-th position. Additionally, we fix the isomorphism
\mathbf{Q}_{p^{f}}\otimes_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}}E\cong\bigoplus_{j\in\mathcal{J}}E
and
\mathbf{F}_{p^{f}}\otimes_{\mathbf{F}_{p}}\mathbf{F}\cong\bigoplus_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\mathbf{F}
, both of which are compatible with the isomorphism above. By abuse of the notation, we will also write
e_{j}
for the image of
e_{j}
under the natural quotient map
\mathbf{Z}_{p^{f}}\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}\mathcal{O}\rightarrow\mathbf{F}_{p^{f}}\otimes_{\mathbf{F}_{p}}\mathbf{F}
. Note that we have
\varphi(e_{j})=e_{j+1}
and so
\varphi((d_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{J}})=(d_{j-1})_{j\in\mathcal{J}}
. We also note that we will write
v_{p}
for the
p
-adic valuation on
E
normalized as
v_{p}(p)=1
, and write
v_{p}(\vec{y})
for
(v_{p}(y_{j}))_{j\in\mathcal{J}}
if
\vec{y}=(y_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\in\bigoplus_{j\in\mathcal{J}}E(=E^{f})
.

Let
\vec{r}:=(r_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{J}}
be an
f
-tuple of positive integers with
r_{j}<p-1
for all
j\in\mathcal{J}
, and fix
r\in\mathbf{Z}
with
r_{j}\leq r<p-1
for all
j\in\mathcal{J}
. Let
\mathcal{J}_{0}
be a subset of
\mathcal{J}
satisfying

(1.1.1)

\frac{1}{2f}\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}}(r_{j}-1)\geq\frac{1}{f}\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}_{0}}r_{j},

and set
\hat{\mathbf{Q}}:=\mathbf{Q}\cup\{\infty\}
with the usual order. (Note that the condition (
1.1.1
) occurs from the admissibility of filtered
(\phi,N)
-modules.) For
\vec{k}^{\prime}=(k^{\prime}_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{J}}
with

(1.1.2)

k^{\prime}_{j}\in\begin{cases}[\frac{1}{2},r_{j}+\frac{1}{2}]\cap\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{Z}&\mbox{if }j\not\in\mathcal{J}_{0};\\
\{\infty\}&\mbox{if }j\in\mathcal{J}_{0},\end{cases}

we further define a sign function
s_{j}:=(-1)^{2k^{\prime}_{j}}
if
j\not\in\mathcal{J}_{0}
and
s_{j}:=-1
if
j\in\mathcal{J}_{0}
.

We are now ready to introduce the equations and inequalities: for a subset
\mathcal{J}_{0}\subset\mathcal{J}
with (
1.1.1
) and for a tuple
\vec{k}^{\prime}=(k^{\prime}_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{J}}
with (
1.1.2
), we consider the following equations and inequalities in
\vec{T}:=(T_{0},T_{1},\cdots,T_{f-1})\in\hat{\mathbf{Q}}^{f}
and
\vec{t}:=(t_{0},t_{1},\cdots,t_{f-1})\in\hat{\mathbf{Q}}^{f}
:

A
.

for each
j\in\mathcal{J}

T_{j}+s_{j}T_{j+1}=\begin{cases}2k^{\prime}_{j}-r_{j}-\frac{1+s_{j}}{2}&\mbox{if }\frac{1}{2}\leq k^{\prime}_{j}\leq\frac{r_{j}}{2}\,\,\mbox{or}\,\,k^{\prime}_{j}=r_{j}+\frac{1}{2};\\
2k^{\prime}_{j}-r_{j}-\frac{1+s_{j}}{2}+2t_{j}&\mbox{if }\frac{r_{j}+1}{2}\leq k^{\prime}_{j}\leq r_{j};\\
r_{j}+1&\mbox{if }k^{\prime}_{j}=\infty,\end{cases}

B
.

for each
j\in\mathcal{J}

T_{j}\leq 0\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\begin{cases}T_{j}\leq t_{j}&\mbox{if }k^{\prime}_{j}=\frac{1}{2};\\
T_{j}\leq t_{j},\,-1\leq T_{j+1}&\mbox{if }1\leq k^{\prime}_{j}\leq\frac{r_{j}}{2};\\
T_{j}\geq t_{j}<0,\,-1\leq T_{j+1}&\mbox{if }\frac{r_{j}+1}{2}\leq k^{\prime}_{j}\leq r_{j}-\frac{1}{2};\\
t_{j}\leq T_{j}\leq t_{j}+r_{j}&\mbox{if }k^{\prime}_{j}=r_{j};\\
T_{j}\geq t_{j}+r_{j}&\mbox{if }k^{\prime}_{j}=r_{j}+\frac{1}{2};\\
\mbox{none}&\mbox{if }k^{\prime}_{j}=\infty.\end{cases}

For
(\vec{\Theta},\vec{x})=((\Theta_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{J}},(x_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{J}})\in(E^{f})^{2}
with
(\vec{T},\vec{t})=(v_{p}(\vec{\Theta}),v_{p}(\vec{x}))
satisfying the equations in
A
and the inequalities in
B
for some
\mathcal{J}_{0}\subset\mathcal{J}
with (
1.1.1
) and for some
\vec{k}^{\prime}
with (
1.1.2
), one can further choose
\vec{\Lambda}=(\Lambda_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\in E^{f}
satisfying

(1.1.3)

v_{p}(\Lambda_{j})=\frac{r_{j}-1-v_{p}(\Theta_{j})+v_{p}(\Theta_{j+1})}{2}

for all
j\in\mathcal{J}
. Then we give explicit recipes to construct a strongly divisible module and an admissible filtered
(\phi,N)
-module, determined by these data (see §
5.2
as well as §
5.1
for the precise construction):

\circ

the data
(\vec{\Lambda},\vec{x},\vec{r})
determines an admissible filtered
(\phi,N)
-module
D
corresponding to a
2
-dimensional semi-stable non-crystalline representation of
\Gal(\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}/\mathbf{Q}_{p^{f}})
with
j
-labeled Hodge–Tate weights
(0,r_{j})
for all
j\in\mathcal{J}
;

\circ

the data
(\vec{\Lambda},\vec{\Theta},\vec{x},\vec{r},r,\vec{k}^{\prime})
determines a strongly divisible module
\mathfrak{M}
of weight
r
.

Theorem 1.1.4

(Theorem
5.2.9
)
.

\mathfrak{M}
corresponds to a Galois stable lattice in the semi-stable representation corresponding to
D
.

An advantage of this theorem is that it allows the construction of strongly divisible modules corresponding to Galois stable lattices in
2
-dimensional semi-stable non-crystalline representations of
\Gal(\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}/\mathbf{Q}_{p^{f}})
with Hodge–Tate weights in the Fontaine–Laffaille range by solving the system of linear equations in
A
and the inequalities in
B
.

We expect that this method is quite general. More precisely, we propose the following:

Conjecture 1.1.5
.

(i)

This method provides at least one Galois stable lattice in each
2
-dimensional semi-stable non-crystalline representation of
\Gal(\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}/\mathbf{Q}_{p^{f}})
with Hodge–Tate weights in the Fontaine–Laffaille range.

(ii)

If the mod-
p
reduction is an extension of two distinct characters, then this method exhausts the two non-homothetic lattices.

To support our conjecture, we verify that our method recovers the known results when
f=1
(see
[
BM
,
GP19
,
LP
]
) and provide illustrative examples for
f=2
(see §
6
; in particular, see Remarks
6.2.2
and
6.3.2
).

We further point out that we also compute the mod-
p
reductions of pseudo-strongly divisible modules (see §
5.3
). Once
(\vec{\Theta},\vec{x})
satisfies the equations in
A
and the inequalities in
B
for some
\vec{k}^{\prime}
, these mod-
p
reductions determine the Breuil module corresponding to the mod-
p
reduction of the strongly divisible module
\mathfrak{M}
.

In the following example, we use our main results to compute the mod-
p
reductions of the
2
-dimensional semi-stable non-crystalline representations of
\Gal(\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}/\mathbf{Q}_{p^{f}})
with parallel Hodge–Tate weights
(0,1)
.

Example 1.1.6
.

Let
\vec{r}\in\mathbf{Z}^{f}
with
r_{j}=1
for all
j\in\mathcal{J}
, and set
r=1
. It is immediate that
\mathcal{J}_{0}=\emptyset
is the only choice satisfying the condition (
1.1.1
). Thus
k^{\prime}_{j}\neq\infty
for any
j\in\mathcal{J}
, and it turns out that it is enough to consider the case
k^{\prime}_{j}=\frac{1}{2}
for all
j\in\mathcal{J}
.

For this
\vec{k}^{\prime}:=(k^{\prime}_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{J}}
, the system of the equations in
A
and the inequalities in
B
is given by

\circ

T_{j}-T_{j+1}=0
for all
j\in\mathcal{J}
;

\circ

T_{j}\leq t_{j}
and
T_{j}\leq 0
for all
j\in\mathcal{J}
,

which are summarized as

(1.1.7)

v_{p}(\Theta_{0})=v_{p}(\Theta_{1})=\cdots=v_{p}(\Theta_{f-1})\leq\min\{0,v_{p}(x_{0}),\cdots,v_{p}(x_{f-1})\}

for
\vec{\Theta}\in E^{f}
and
\vec{x}\in E^{f}
. Moreover, we need to choose
\vec{\Lambda}\in(\mathcal{O}^{\times})^{f}
by (
1.1.3
). Note that for any
\vec{x}\in E^{f}
there exists
\vec{\Theta}\in E^{f}
satisfying (
1.1.7
).

It turns out that
\vec{x}
determines the
\mathfrak{L}
-invariant
\vec{\mathfrak{L}}\in[\mathbb{P}^{1}(E)]^{f}
(see (
5.1.2
), (
5.2.2
), and (
5.1.12
)), and in this case we have

x_{j}=\frac{p\mathfrak{L}_{j}-\mathfrak{L}_{j-1}}{p}\in E

for all
j\in\mathcal{J}
. Moreover, one can choose
\lambda\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}
satisfying
\lambda^{f}=\prod_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\Lambda_{j}
(see (
5.1.19
)), and these data
(\lambda,\vec{\mathfrak{L}})
together with
\vec{r}
determine an admissible filtered
(\phi,N)
-module, denoted by
D(\lambda,\vec{\mathfrak{L}})
, in Example
2.1.3
, which does not depend on the choice of
\lambda
(see Lemma
2.1.5
).

The data
(\vec{\Lambda},\vec{\Theta},\vec{x},\vec{r},r,\vec{k}^{\prime})
also determine a strongly divisible module, but in this example we only describe the Breuil module, denoted by
\mathcal{M}
, corresponding to the mod-
p
reduction of the strongly divisible module, as the structure of the strongly divisible module is too complicated for the purpose of the introduction. For each
j\in\mathcal{J}
, we describe the isotypic component

(\mathcal{M}e_{j},\mathrm{Fil}^{1}\mathcal{M}e_{j},\phi_{1}:\mathrm{Fil}^{1}\mathcal{M}e_{j}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}e_{j+1},N:\mathcal{M}e_{j}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}e_{j})

following the matrix notation in §
1.3.4
: by Proposition
5.3.6
there exists a basis
\underline{E}:=(\bar{E}_{1},\bar{E}_{2})
for
\mathcal{M}
and a system of generators
\underline{F}:=(\bar{F}_{1},\bar{F}_{2})
for
\mathrm{Fil}^{1}\mathcal{M}
such that for each
j\in\mathcal{J}

\circ

\mathrm{Mat}_{\underline{E}e_{j},\underline{F}e_{j}}(\mathrm{Fil}^{1}\mathcal{M}e_{j})=\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
\frac{x_{j}}{\Theta_{j}}&u\end{bmatrix}
;

\circ

\mathrm{Mat}_{\underline{E}e_{j+1},\underline{F}e_{j}}(\phi_{1})=\begin{bmatrix}\Lambda_{j}&0\\
0&-\tfrac{\Lambda_{j}\Theta_{j}}{\Theta_{j+1}}\end{bmatrix}
;

\circ

\mathrm{Mat}_{\underline{E}e_{j},\underline{E}e_{j}}(N)=\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
\frac{1}{\Theta_{j}}&0\end{bmatrix}
.

Hence, one can conclude the following results by Caruso
[
Car06
]
: if we let
\overline{\rho}:=\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{st}}^{\ast}(\mathcal{M})
, where
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{st}}^{\ast}
is defined at (
2.3.2
), then

\overline{\rho}\simeq\begin{bmatrix}\omega&\ast\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}\otimes U_{\lambda^{f}}

where
U_{\lambda^{f}}
is the unramified character sending
\varphi^{-f}
to
\lambda^{f}\in\mathbf{F}^{\times}
and
\omega
is the fundamental character of neveau
1
. Moreover, one can describe the extension class
\ast
by observing the Breuil module above:

\circ

Assume that
v_{p}(x_{j})\geq 0
for all
j\in\mathcal{J}
. If we choose
\vec{\Theta}
with
v_{p}(\Theta_{0})=\cdots=v_{p}(\Theta_{f-1})=0
then
\overline{\rho}_{0}
is très ramifiée, and otherwise it is split;

\circ

Assume that
v_{p}(x_{j})<0
for some
j\in\mathcal{J}
. Then
\overline{\rho}
is peu ramifiée, and it is split if and only if the inequality in (
1.1.7
) is strict.

We note that this example recovers the main result of Cheon in his master’s thesis
[
Ch
]
.

In the following subsection, we outline our approach for obtaining the equations and inequalities. We also briefly explain how a common solution
(\vec{\Lambda},\vec{\Theta},\vec{x})
to the equations in
A
and the inequalities in
B
for some
\vec{k}^{\prime}
determines an admissible filtered
(\phi,N)
-module and a strongly divisible module.

1.2.
Strategy and overview

Our basic strategy to compute mod-
p
reduction follows
[
BM
]
. The first step is to classify admissible filtered
(\phi,N)
-modules of rank
2
with coefficients in
\mathbf{Q}_{p^{f}}\otimes_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}}E
, as there is an equivalence between the category of semi-stable representations and the category of admissible filtered
(\phi,N)
-modules
[
CF
]
. The isomorphism classes of admissible filtered
(\phi,N)
-modules of rank
2
have already been classified in
[
Dous
]
. These classes are parameterized by the Frobenius eigenvalue
\lambda\in\mathcal{O}
and the
\mathfrak{L}
-invariants
\vec{\mathfrak{L}}:=(\mathfrak{L}_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\in[\mathbb{P}^{1}(E)]^{f}
, and are denoted by
D(\lambda,\vec{\mathfrak{L}})
, when we fix the
j
-labeled Hodge–Tate weights
(0,r_{j})
for
r_{j}>0
for all
j\in\mathcal{J}
(see Example
2.1.3
). Since the absolute Galois group is compact, every semi-stable representation admits Galois stable lattices. Thanks to
[
Bre99
,
Liu
]
, the corresponding admissible filtered
(\phi,N)
-module also has lattice structures, called strongly divisible modules, which correspond to these Galois stable lattices, if we further assume
r_{j}<p-1
for all
j\in\mathcal{J}
. As the mod-
p
reduction of Galois stable lattices is naturally defined, there exist Breuil modules associated with the mod-
p
reduction of strongly divisible modules. By studying the structure of these Breuil modules, one can determine the mod-
p
reduction of the semi-stable representations, and thanks to
[
Car06
]
our understanding for simple Breuil modules is relatively good.

The most difficult part in
[
BM
,
GP19
]
is constructing the strongly divisible modules, and it is the same here. But our approach to construct strongly divisible modules differs slightly from that of
[
BM
]
. To explain our approach, we introduce the Breuil ring
S
. Let
S
be the
p
-adic completion of the ring
\mathbf{Z}_{p^{f}}[\frac{u^{i}}{i!}\mid i\in\mathbf{N}]
, and fix
v:=u-p
. The ring
S
can be explicitly described as follows:

S=\left\{\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a_{k}\frac{v^{k}}{k!}\mid a_{k}\in\mathbf{Z}_{p^{f}}\mbox{ for all }k\in\mathbf{N}\cup\{0\}\mbox{ and }a_{k}\mapsto 0\mbox{ $p$-adically}\right\}.

This ring
S
has additional structures: the filtration of ideals
\mathrm{Fil}^{i}S
of
S
for all
i\in\mathbf{N}\cup\{0\}
, the Frobenius map
\phi
on
S
, and the monodromy operator
N
on
S
. We further define
S_{\mathcal{O}}:=S\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}\mathcal{O}
, and extend the definitions of
\phi
,
N
, and
\mathrm{Fil}^{i}S_{\mathcal{O}}

\mathcal{O}
-linearly. Note that one can decompose
S_{\mathcal{O}}\cong\bigoplus_{j\in\mathcal{J}}S_{\mathcal{O}}e_{j}
, and we often consider an
S_{\mathcal{O}}
-module
\mathfrak{M}
as an
\bigoplus_{j\in\mathcal{J}}S_{\mathcal{O}}e_{j}
-module
\mathfrak{M}=\bigoplus_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\mathfrak{M}e_{j}
via the isomorphism above. Each isotypic component
\mathfrak{M}e_{j}
is an
S_{\mathcal{O}}e_{j}
-module. To define a strongly divisible module, we first need to extend the coefficient of the admissible filtered
(\phi,N)
-module
D(\lambda,\vec{\mathfrak{L}})
to
S_{E}:=S\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}E
, by letting
\mathcal{D}(\lambda,\vec{\mathfrak{L}}):=S\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}_{p^{f}}}D(\lambda,\vec{\mathfrak{L}})
. Note that
\mathcal{D}(\lambda,\vec{\mathfrak{L}})
is naturally endowed with additional structures: the filtration
\{\mathrm{Fil}^{i}\mathcal{D}\}_{i\in\mathbf{N}\cup\{0\}}
(resp.
\phi
,
N
) induced from the filtration of
S
(resp.
\phi
on
S
,
N
on
S
) and the filtration of
D
(resp.
\phi
on
D
,
N
on
D
) (see §
2.2
for the precise definition). Then we fix
r\in\mathbf{N}
with
r_{j}\leq r<p-1
for all
j\in\mathcal{J}
, and a strongly divisible module of weight
r
is, roughly speaking, a lattice structure in
\mathcal{D}(\lambda,\vec{\mathfrak{L}})
(see Definition
2.2.3
for its definition).

We first introduce the notion of pseudo-strongly divisible modules. Let
S^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}}
(resp.
S^{\prime}_{E}
) denote the ring
S_{\mathcal{O}}e_{j}
(resp.
S_{E}e_{j}
) for a fixed (and thus every)
j\in\mathcal{J}
. Let
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
be a free
S^{\prime}_{E}
-module of rank
2
, with basis denoted by
E_{1}^{\prime}
and
E_{2}^{\prime}
. Let
(r,r^{\prime})
be a pair of positive integers satisfying
r^{\prime}\leq r<p-1
. For each
k\in[0,r^{\prime}]\cup\{\infty\}
, the pseudo-strongly divisible modules in
\mathbf{Case}~(k)
consist of a quadruple

(\mathfrak{M}^{\prime},\,\mathrm{Fil}^{r;r^{\prime}}_{k}\mathfrak{M}^{\prime},\,\phi^{\prime}:\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{D}^{\prime},\,N^{\prime}:\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{D}^{\prime}),

where:

\circ

\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
is the free
S^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}}
-submodule of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
generated by
E_{1}^{\prime}
and
E_{2}^{\prime}
;

\circ

\mathrm{Fil}^{r;r^{\prime}}_{k}\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
is an
S^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}}
-submodule of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
;

\circ

\phi^{\prime}
is a continuous
\phi
-semilinear map on
\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
;

\circ

N^{\prime}
is a continuous
\mathcal{O}
-linear derivation.

Our definition of pseudo-strongly divisible modules is explicit (see Definition
4.1.2
for the precise statement) and is inspired by the expected structure of strongly divisible modules. The positive integer
r
indicates the weight of the strongly divisible module, and
r^{\prime}
corresponds to a Hodge–Tate weight. The filtration
\mathrm{Fil}^{r;r^{\prime}}_{k}\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
is generated by a set of explicit elements in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
depending on
k
. In particular, when
k=\infty
, we have

(1.2.1)

\mathrm{Fil}^{r;r^{\prime}}_{\infty}\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}=S^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}}(v^{r}E_{1}^{\prime},v^{r-r^{\prime}}E_{2}^{\prime})+\mathrm{Fil}^{p}S^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}}\mathfrak{M}^{\prime},

which is intended to arise as the isotypic component at
j
of a strongly divisible module when
\mathfrak{L}_{j}=\infty
. If the triple
(r,r^{\prime},k)
is fixed, then these pseudo-strongly divisible modules are parameterized by the data
(\Lambda,\Theta,\Omega,x)\in(E^{\times})^{3}\times E
and
\Delta(T),\tilde{\Delta}(T)\in(E(T)\cap(E+\mathcal{O}[[T+1]]))
. These data
(\Lambda,\Theta,\Omega,x)
and
\Delta(\gamma-1),\tilde{\Delta}(\gamma-1)\in S^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}}
, where
\gamma:=\frac{(u-p)^{p}}{p}\in S^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}}
, naturally appears in the coefficients of the definition.

We wish that the pseudo-strongly divisible modules become the isotypic components of the strongly divisible modules. To achieve this, it suffices to impose the following conditions:

(i)

\phi^{\prime}(\mathfrak{M}^{\prime})\subseteq\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
and
N^{\prime}(\mathfrak{M}^{\prime})\subseteq\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
;

(ii)

\mathrm{Fil}^{r;r^{\prime}}_{k}\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
is a
S^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}}
-submodule of
\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
;

(iii)

\phi^{\prime}_{r}(\mathrm{Fil}^{r;r^{\prime}}_{k}\mathfrak{M}^{\prime})
is contained in
\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
and generates
\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
over
S^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}}
, where
\phi^{\prime}_{r}:=\tfrac{1}{p^{r}}\phi^{\prime}
.

Condition (i) can be readily described in terms of inequalities involving the quadruple
(v_{p}(\Lambda),v_{p}(\Theta),v_{p}(\Omega),v_{p}(x))
(see Lemma
4.1.7
). We also derive inequalities on the quadruple that are nearly equivalent to condition (ii) for each
k
(see Theorem
4.1.10
). Moreover, we show that for each
k

\mathrm{Fil}^{r;r^{\prime}}_{k}\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
can be written as

\mathrm{Fil}^{r;r^{\prime}}_{k}\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}=S^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}}(G^{\prime}_{1},G^{\prime}_{2})+\mathrm{Fil}^{p}S^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}}\mathfrak{M}^{\prime},

where
G^{\prime}_{1},G^{\prime}_{2}
are explicitly defined for each
k
(see (
4.1.11
)). For example, the filtration described in (
1.2.1
) corresponds to the case
k=\infty
. To check the condition (iii), it is necessary for
\phi^{\prime}
to satisfy

(1.2.2)

\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}=S^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}}\left(\phi^{\prime}_{r}(G^{\prime}_{1}),\phi^{\prime}_{r}(G^{\prime}_{2})\right)

for each
\mathbf{Case}~(k)
, and so we need to check the square matrix appeared in the following matrix multiplication is invertible:

(\phi^{\prime}_{r}(G^{\prime}_{1}),\phi^{\prime}_{r}(G^{\prime}_{2}))=(E^{\prime}_{1},E^{\prime}_{2})\cdot\begin{bmatrix}a_{k}&b_{k}\\
c_{k}&d_{k}\end{bmatrix}.

We will consider the following two possibilities
a_{k}\in(S_{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime})^{\times}
or
b_{k}\in(S_{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime})^{\times}
, and it turns out that these two possibilities are mutually exclusive (depending the choice of
\Delta(T)
). Under the setting of
\mathbf{Case}~(k)
, we say that the pseudo-strongly divisible module
\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}

satisfies the strong divisibility of type
\mathbf{Case}_{\phi}(k)
(resp.
of type
\mathbf{Case}_{\phi}(k+\tfrac{1}{2})
) if (
1.2.2
) holds and
b_{k}\in(S_{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime})^{\times}
(resp.
a_{k}\in(S_{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime})^{\times}
). Hence, we consider
\mathbf{Case}_{\phi}(k^{\prime})
for
k^{\prime}\in([0,r^{\prime}+\tfrac{1}{2}]\cap\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{Z})\cup\{\infty,\infty+\tfrac{1}{2}\}
. But it turns out that
\mathbf{Case}_{\phi}(0)
and
\mathbf{Case}_{\phi}(\infty)
are empty, and so we consider
\mathbf{Case}_{\phi}(k^{\prime})
only for
k^{\prime}\in([\tfrac{1}{2},r^{\prime}+\tfrac{1}{2}]\cap\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{Z})\cup\{\infty\}
, after renaming
\mathbf{Case}_{\phi}(\infty+\tfrac{1}{2})
to
\mathbf{Case}_{\phi}(\infty)
. For
k^{\prime}\in([\tfrac{1}{2},r^{\prime}+\tfrac{1}{2}]\cap\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{Z})\cup\{\infty\}
, we give enough conditions on the quadruple
(v_{p}(\Lambda),v_{p}(\Theta),v_{p}(\Omega),v_{p}(x))
to guarantee that
\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
satisfies the strong divisibility of type
\mathbf{Case}_{\phi}(k^{\prime})
, and these conditions involve the equations as well as the inequalities on the quadruple. All the equations and inequalities obtained for (i), (ii), and (iii) are summarized in Table
1
. Moreover, for each
k^{\prime}
we determine
\Delta(T)
to ensure the strong divisibility of type
\mathbf{Case}_{\phi}(k^{\prime})
, and it is worth noting that
\tilde{\Delta}(T)
is not relevant to the strong divisibility (see Lemma
4.1.14
). Note that §
4
is devoted to deriving these conditions, and that we heavily use the frameworks developed in §
3
for the proofs.

The next step is applying the conditions obtained from the step in the previous paragraph to construct the strongly divisible modules. We begin by determining the form of the generators of strongly divisible modules in
\mathcal{D}(\lambda,\vec{\mathfrak{L}})
, which we expect to be as follow: if we write
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}
for the basis of
D(\lambda,\vec{\mathfrak{L}})
as in Example
2.1.3
then

(1.2.3)

E_{1}=\vec{\theta}_{1}\bigg(\eta_{1}+\frac{\varphi(\vec{\varrho})+\vec{\Delta}^{\varphi}(\gamma-1)}{p}\eta_{2}\bigg)\qquad\&\qquad E_{2}=\vec{\theta}_{2}\eta_{2}

for some
\vec{\theta}_{1},\vec{\theta}_{2}\in(E^{\times})^{f}
,
\vec{\varrho}\in E^{f}
, and
\vec{\Delta}(T)\in(E(T)\cap(E+\mathcal{O}[[T+1]]))^{f}
, where
\gamma:=\frac{(u-p)^{p}}{p}\in S_{\mathcal{O}}
. (Here, by
\vec{\Delta}^{\varphi}(T)
we mean
(\Delta^{(j-1)}(T))_{j\in\mathcal{J}}
if we write
\vec{\Delta}(T)=(\Delta^{(j)}(T))_{j\in\mathcal{J}}
.) We further require that
\vec{\varrho}
satisfies

\varrho_{j}=\mathfrak{L}_{j}
if
\mathfrak{L}_{j}\neq\infty
, a condition that also arises from the definition of strongly divisible modules (see Remark
5.1.3
). We will write
\mathfrak{M}^{\diamond}
for the
S_{\mathcal{O}}
-submodule of
\mathcal{D}(\lambda,\vec{\mathfrak{L}})
generated by
E_{1},E_{2}
. Moreover, we further have
\mathrm{Fil}^{r}\mathfrak{M}^{\diamond}:=\mathfrak{M}^{\diamond}\cap\mathrm{Fil}^{r}\mathcal{D}
as well as
\phi^{\diamond},N^{\diamond}
on
\mathfrak{M}^{\diamond}
induced from
\phi,N
on
\mathcal{D}(\lambda,\vec{\mathfrak{L}})
, respectively, by restriction. This leads to the quadruple

(1.2.4)

(\mathfrak{M}^{\diamond},\,\mathrm{Fil}^{r}\mathfrak{M}^{\diamond},\,\phi^{\diamond}:\mathfrak{M}^{\diamond}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}(\lambda,\vec{\mathfrak{L}}),\,N^{\diamond}:\mathfrak{M}^{\diamond}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}(\lambda,\vec{\mathfrak{L}})),

which is parameterized by the data
(\vec{\Lambda},\vec{\Theta},\vec{x})\in((E^{\times})^{f})^{2}\times E^{f}
along with
\vec{\Delta}(T)
. These parameters satisfy the relations

(1.2.5)

\vec{\Lambda}=\frac{\lambda\vec{\theta}_{1}}{\varphi^{-1}(\vec{\theta}_{1})},\quad\vec{\Theta}=\frac{\vec{\theta}_{2}}{\vec{\theta}_{1}},\quad\mbox{and}\quad\vec{x}=\frac{p\vec{\varrho}-\varphi(\vec{\varrho})-\vec{\Delta}^{\varphi}(-1)}{p}.

For each
j\in\mathcal{J}
, we consider the isotypic component of
\mathfrak{M}^{\diamond}
at
j\in\mathcal{J}

\big(\mathfrak{M}^{\diamond}e_{j},\mathrm{Fil}^{r}\mathfrak{M}^{\diamond}e_{j},\phi^{\diamond,(j)}:\mathfrak{M}^{\diamond}e_{j}\to\mathcal{D}(\lambda,\vec{\mathfrak{L}})e_{j+1},N^{\diamond,(j)}:\mathfrak{M}^{\diamond}e_{j}\to\mathcal{D}(\lambda,\vec{\mathfrak{L}})e_{j}\big),

which are parameterized by
(\Lambda_{j},\Theta_{j},\Theta_{j+1},x_{j})\in(E^{\times})^{3}\times E
and
(\Delta^{(j)}(T),\Delta^{(j-1)}(T))
. If we define the natural isomorphism

\psi^{(j)}:\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}(\lambda,\vec{\mathfrak{L}})e_{j}\,:\,\,E^{\prime}_{1}\mapsto E_{1}e_{j}\,\,\&\,\,E^{\prime}_{2}\mapsto E_{2}e_{j},

then by identifying
(r^{\prime},\Lambda,\Theta,\Omega,x,\Delta,\tilde{\Delta})
with
(r_{j},\Lambda_{j},\Theta_{j},\Theta_{j+1},x_{j},\Delta^{(j)},\Delta^{(j-1)})
, we have the following commutative diagram

\textstyle{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi^{(j)}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{N^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\phi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\psi^{(j)}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi^{(j+1)}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Fil}^{r;r^{\prime}}_{k}\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{r}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\psi^{(j)}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}(\lambda,\vec{\mathfrak{L}})e_{j}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{M}^{\diamond}e_{j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{N^{\diamond,(j)}}
\scriptstyle{\phi^{\diamond,(j)}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}(\lambda,\vec{\mathfrak{L}})e_{j+1}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Fil}^{r}\mathfrak{M}^{\diamond}e_{j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{r}^{\diamond,(j)}}

where
\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
is a pseudo-strongly divisible module in
\mathbf{Case}~(k)
, under the assumption that
\mathrm{Fil}^{r;r^{\prime}}_{k}\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
is a
S^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}}
-submodule of
\mathfrak{M}
together with a parallel assumption on
\mathrm{Fil}^{r}\mathfrak{M}^{\diamond}e_{j}
(see Proposition
5.1.23
).

We apply the properties of the first row of the diagram above to the second row, identifying
(r^{\prime},\Lambda,\Theta,\Omega,x,\Delta,\tilde{\Delta},k^{\prime})
with
(r_{j},\Lambda_{j},\Theta_{j},\Theta_{j+1},x_{j},\Delta^{(j)},\Delta^{(j-1)},k^{\prime}_{j})
for each
j\in\mathcal{J}
. The resulting conditions on
(v_{p}(\vec{\Lambda}),v_{p}(\vec{\Theta}),v_{p}(\vec{x}))
are summarized in
A
,
B
, and (
1.1.3
). In other words, if
(v_{p}(\vec{\Theta}),v_{p}(\vec{x}))
satisfies the corresponding equations and inequalities for some
\vec{k}^{\prime}=(k^{\prime}_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{J}}
, and if we choose
\vec{\Lambda}\in E^{f}
according to (
1.1.3
), then the quadruple in (
1.2.4
) induced by (
1.2.3
), where
\vec{\theta}_{1},\vec{\theta}_{2},\vec{\varrho}
are determined by (
1.2.5
) and
\vec{\Delta}(T)
is determined by
\vec{k}^{\prime}
, forms a strongly divisible module. Note that §
5
is devoted to deriving the main results.

As stated in Conjecture
1.1.5
, we believe that our method is quite general. To support this conjecture, we verify that it recovers known results in the case
f=1
(see
[
BM
,
GP19
,
LP
]
) and provide illustrative examples for
\vec{r}=(2,2)
and
\vec{r}=(1,5)
when
f=2
(see §
6
).

1.3.
Notation

Much of the notation introduced in this subsection will also be introduced in the text, but we try to collect various definitions here for ease of reading.

As usual, we write
\mathbf{N}
(resp.
\mathbf{Z}
, resp.
\mathbf{Q}
, resp.
\mathbf{R}
) for the set of natural numbers (resp. the ring of integers, resp. the field of rational numbers, resp. the field of real numbers). We further set
\mathbf{N}_{0}:=\mathbf{N}\cup\{0\}
.

1.3.1.
Galois groups

Let
p
be a prime, and
f
be a positive integer. We let
\mathbf{Q}_{p^{f}}
be the finite unramified extension of
\mathbf{Q}_{p}
of degree
f
with ring of integers
\mathbf{Z}_{p^{f}}
(=
W(\mathbf{F}_{p^{f}})
, the ring of Witt vectors over
\mathbf{F}_{p^{f}}
). We write
G_{\mathbf{Q}_{p^{f}}}
for the absolute Galois group of
\mathbf{Q}_{p^{f}}
, and
I_{\mathbf{Q}_{p^{f}}}
for the inertial subgroup of
G_{\mathbf{Q}_{p^{f}}}
. The cyclotomic character is denoted by
\varepsilon:G_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}}\rightarrow\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{\times}
, and the fundamental character of neveau
f
is denoted by
\omega_{f}:G_{\mathbf{Q}_{p^{f}}}\rightarrow\mathbf{F}_{p^{f}}^{\times}
. We will naturally identify
\Gal(\mathbf{Q}_{p^{f}}/\mathbf{Q}_{p})
with
\Gal(\mathbf{F}_{p^{f}}/\mathbf{F}_{p})
via the natural surjection
\mathbf{Z}_{p^{f}}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{F}_{p^{f}}
, and will consider the arithmetic Frobenius
\varphi\in\Gal(\mathbf{F}_{p^{f}}/\mathbf{F}_{p})
as an element in
\Gal(\mathbf{Q}_{p^{f}}/\mathbf{Q}_{p})
via the identification. Since
\varphi
is the generator of the Galois group, the association
\varphi^{-1}\leftrightarrow 1
gives rise to an isomorphism
\Gal(\mathbf{Q}_{p^{f}}/\mathbf{Q}_{p})\cong\mathbf{Z}/f\mathbf{Z}
, and we will fix this isomorphism throughout this paper.

1.3.2.
Coefficients

Let
E
be a finite extension of
\mathbf{Q}_{p^{f}}
with ring of integers
\mathcal{O}
, maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}
, uniformizer
\varpi
, and residue field
\mathbf{F}
. We will always consider
\mathbf{Q}_{p^{f}}
as a subfield of
E
, and so if
a\in\mathbf{Q}_{p^{f}}
then
a\in E
. We may naturally consider the characters
\varepsilon
and
\omega_{f}
are defined over
\mathcal{O}
and
\mathbf{F}
, respectively. Let
\mathcal{J}:=\Hom_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}}(\mathbf{Q}_{p^{f}},\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p})
, and we will naturally identify the following sets

\mathcal{J}=\Hom_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}}(\mathbf{Q}_{p^{f}},E)=\Hom_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}(\mathbf{Z}_{p^{f}},\mathcal{O})=\Hom_{\mathbf{F}_{p}}(\mathbf{F}_{p^{f}},\mathbf{F})=\Gal(\mathbf{F}_{p^{f}}/\mathbf{F}_{p})=\mathbf{Z}/f\mathbf{Z}.

Fix a ring isomorphism

(1.3.1)

\mathbf{Z}_{p^{f}}\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}\mathcal{O}\cong\bigoplus_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\mathcal{O}:a\otimes b\mapsto\left(\varphi^{-j}(a)\cdot b\right)_{j\in\mathcal{J}},

and write
e_{j}\in\mathbf{Z}_{p^{f}}\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}\mathcal{O}
for the idempotent element corresponding to the element
(b_{j^{\prime}})_{j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{J}}\in\bigoplus_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\mathcal{O}
with
b_{j^{\prime}}=1
if
j^{\prime}=j
and
b_{j^{\prime}}=0
otherwise. The action of
\varphi
on
\mathbf{Z}_{p^{f}}\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}\mathcal{O}
(acting naturally on
\mathbf{Z}_{p^{f}}
and trivially on
\mathcal{O}
) sends
e_{j}
to
e_{j+1}
due to our choice of the isomorphism above. We will often consider
\varphi
as a map from
\mathcal{O}^{f}
onto itself sending
(a_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{J}}
to
(a_{j-1})_{j\in\mathcal{J}}
. Note that these idempotent elements satisfy

e_{j}\cdot e_{j^{\prime}}=\begin{cases}e_{j}&\mbox{if }j=j^{\prime};\\
0&\mbox{otherwise}\end{cases}\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}}e_{j}=1.

Moreover, they also satisfy that for each
j\in\mathcal{J}
and for all
a\otimes b\in\mathbf{Z}_{p^{f}}\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}\mathcal{O}

(1.3.2)

(a\otimes b)e_{j}=\left(1\otimes\varphi^{-j}(a)\cdot b\right)e_{j}.

The fixed isomorphism above also induces isomorphisms

\mathbf{Q}_{p^{f}}\otimes_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}}E\cong\bigoplus_{j\in\mathcal{J}}E\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\mathbf{F}_{p^{f}}\otimes_{\mathbf{F}_{p}}\mathbf{F}\cong\bigoplus_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\mathbf{F},

and by abuse of the notation we also write
e_{j}
for the corresponding idempotents in each case. We further note that these
e_{j}
in
\mathbf{Q}_{p^{f}}\otimes_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}}E
(resp. in
\mathbf{F}_{p^{f}}\otimes_{\mathbf{F}_{p}}\mathbf{F}
) also satisfy the same properties as described on
\mathbf{Z}_{p^{f}}\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}\mathcal{O}
.

Let
v_{p}
be the
p
-adic valuation on
\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}
normalized as
v_{p}(p)=1
. If
\vec{y}=(y_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\in\bigoplus_{j\in\mathcal{J}}E
, then we write
v_{p}(\vec{y})
for
(v_{p}(y_{j}))_{j\in\mathcal{J}}
.

1.3.3.
Elements of the ring
S

Let
S
be the
p
-adic completion of the ring
\mathbf{Z}_{p^{f}}[\frac{u^{i}}{i!}\mid i\in\mathbf{N}]
, and fix
v:=u-p\in S
. We also let
\phi:S\rightarrow S
be a continuous
\varphi
-semilinear map with
\phi(u)=u^{p}
. We let
c:=\phi(v)/p\in S^{\times}
and
\gamma:=\frac{(u-p)^{p}}{p}\in S
. It is easy to see that
c-(\gamma-1)\equiv pu^{p-1}\pmod{p^{2}S}
and
\phi(\gamma)\in p^{p-1}S
. These two elements naturally appear in our strongly divisible modules. We further let
S_{\mathcal{O}}:=S\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}\mathcal{O}
, and often identify

S_{\mathcal{O}}\cong\bigoplus_{j\in\mathcal{J}}S_{\mathcal{O}}e_{j}.

If we write
S^{\prime}
for the
p
-adic completion of
\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\frac{u^{i}}{i!}\mid i\in\mathbf{N}]
, then we naturally identify
S^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}}:=S^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}\mathcal{O}
with
S_{\mathcal{O}}e_{j}
for all
j\in\mathcal{J}
. If
\mathfrak{M}
is an
S_{\mathcal{O}}
-module, then for each
j\in\mathcal{J}
we will naturally consider the isotypic component
\mathfrak{M}e_{j}
at
j\in\mathcal{J}
as a module of
S^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}}
via the identification. Moreover, if
\vec{\Delta}(u)\in S_{\mathcal{O}}
, then we often consider it as an element of
\bigoplus_{j\in\mathcal{J}}S^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}}
via the identification above, and write it as
\vec{\Delta}(u)=(\Delta^{(j)}(u))_{j\in\mathcal{J}}
. By
\phi(\vec{\Delta}(u))
we mean the usual action of
\phi
on
S_{\mathcal{O}}
, and by
\vec{\Delta}^{\varphi}(u)
we mean the shifting, i.e.,
\vec{\Delta}^{\varphi}(u):=(\Delta^{(j-1)}(u))_{j\in\mathcal{J}}
. Similarly, we write
S^{\prime}_{E}
for
S^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}E
and we have natural isomorphisms
S^{\prime}_{E}\cong S_{E}e_{j}
for all
j\in\mathcal{J}
.

Let
R[[T]]
be the power series ring over a commutative ring
R
with one variable
T
, and let
n
be positive integer. We write
R[T]^{(n)}
for the subgroup of
R[T]
consisting of the polynomials of degree less than equal to
n
. For
F(T)=\sum_{k\geq 0}a_{k}T^{k}\in R[[T]]
, we set
[F(T)]_{T}^{(n)}:=\sum_{k=0}^{n}a_{k}T^{k}
. For instance, we set
f_{n}(T):=0
if
n=1
, and

(1.3.3)

f_{n}(T):=\bigg[\frac{1}{T}\log(1+T)\bigg]_{T}^{(n-2)}=\sum_{k=0}^{n-2}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k+1}T^{k}\in\mathbf{Z}_{(p)}[T]

if
n\geq 2
. We also write
\dot{F}(T)
for the formal derivative of
F(T)=\sum_{k\geq 0}a_{k}T^{k}
, i.e.,
\dot{F}(T)=\sum_{k\geq 1}ka_{k}T^{k-1}\in R[[T]]
. Note that if
F(T)\in\mathcal{O}[[T+1]]
, then
F(\gamma-1)
naturally belongs to
S^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}}
.

1.3.4.
Miscellaneous

Let
R
be a commutative ring. We write
\mathrm{M}_{m\times n}(R)
for the ring of matrices of size
(m\times n)
over
R
, and
I_{n}\in\mathrm{M}_{n\times n}(R)
(resp.
0_{m,n}\in\mathrm{M}_{m\times n}(R)
) for the identity matrix (resp. for the zero matrix). If
M
is a free module over
R
with a basis
\underline{E}:=(E_{1},E_{2})
, and
A\in M
then by
[A]_{E_{i}}
we mean the coefficients of
E_{i}
in
A
. If
N
is an
R
-module generated by
\underline{F}:=(F_{1},F_{2})
, and
f:N\rightarrow M
is a (semi)-linear map, then
f(F_{j})=A_{1,j}E_{1}+A_{2,j}E_{2}
for all
j=1,2
if and only if

\mathrm{Mat}_{\underline{E},\underline{F}}(f)=\begin{bmatrix}A_{1,1}&A_{1,2}\\
A_{2,1}&A_{2,2}\end{bmatrix}.

Furthermore, if
N
is a submodule of
M
, then
[F_{j}]_{E_{i}}=B_{i,j}\in R
for all
i,j\in\{1,2\}
if and only if

\mathrm{Mat}_{\underline{E},\underline{F}}(N)=\begin{bmatrix}B_{1,1}&B_{1,2}\\
B_{2,1}&B_{2,2}\end{bmatrix}.

If
M
is a non-empty set, we often write
M^{\mathcal{J}}
for
M^{f}
. If
\vec{A}\in M^{\mathcal{J}}
, by
A_{j}
we often mean the entry of
\vec{R}
at the
j
-th position, i.e.,
\vec{A}=(A_{0},\cdots,A_{f-1})
. If
\mathcal{J}_{0}\subset\mathcal{J}
then we will naturally identify
M^{\mathcal{J}}
with
M^{\mathcal{J}_{0}}\times M^{\mathcal{J}\setminus\mathcal{J}_{0}}
respecting the coordinates. For instance, we write
\hat{\mathbf{R}}
for
\mathbf{R}\cup\{\infty\}
with order topology, and we will naturally identify
\hat{\mathbf{R}}^{\mathcal{J}}=\hat{\mathbf{R}}^{\mathcal{J}_{0}}\times\hat{\mathbf{R}}^{\mathcal{J}\setminus\mathcal{J}_{0}}
.

Finally, for a positive integer
n\in\mathbf{N}
, we write
H_{n}\in\mathbf{Q}
for the harmonic number
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{i}
.

1.4.
Acknowledgment

The authors sincerely thank Christophe Breuil and Florian Herzig for numerous helpful comments and suggestions. The authors also thank Eknath Ghate, Yongquan Hu, Wansu Kim, Stefano Morra, Zicheng Qian, and David Savitt for a plenty of helpful discussions.

This work was supported by Samsung Science and Technology Foundation under Project Number SSTF-BA2001-02.

2.
Preliminary

In this section, we quickly review (integral)
p
-adic Hodge theory and fix some notation. We note that the materials in this section are already well-known or easy generalization of known results. Moreover, the exposition in this section is quite close to
[
GP19
,
Par17
]
, but we decided to have a quick review of integral
p
-adic Hodge theory for the completeness and for the ease of reading.

Throughout this section, we write
E
for a finite extension of
\mathbf{Q}_{p}
containing
\mathbf{Q}_{p^{f}}
with the ring of integers
\mathcal{O}
, the maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}
, a uniformizer
\varpi
, and the residue field
\mathbf{F}
. We also let
K
be a unramified extension of
\mathbf{Q}_{p}
of degree
f
, i.e.,
K=\mathbf{Q}_{p^{f}}
. All the results in this section still hold for arbitrary finite extension of
\mathbf{Q}_{p}
, but for simpler notation, we restrict our attention to the unramified extension of
\mathbf{Q}_{p}
, which is our context. We write
\mathcal{O}_{K}
,
\mathfrak{m}_{K}
, and
k
for the ring of integers, the maximal ideal, and the residue field, respectively, of
K
.

2.1.
Filtered
(\phi,N)
-modules

In this subsection, we introduce weakly admissible filtered
(\phi,N)
-modules and their connection to semi-stable representations.

We fix a prime
p
, so that we fix the inclusion
\mathbf{B}_{\mathrm{st}}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{B}_{\mathrm{dR}}
. (For detail, see
[
Fon94
]
.)

Definition 2.1.1
.

A
filtered
(\phi,N,K,E)
-module
(or simply, a
filtered
(\phi,N)
-module
) is a free
K\otimes_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}}E
-module
D
of finite rank together with a triple
\left(\phi,N,\{\mathrm{Fil}^{i}D\}_{i\in\mathbf{Z}}\right)
where

\circ

the
Frobenius map

\phi:D\rightarrow D
is a
\varphi
-semilinear and
E
-linear automorphism;

\circ

the
monodromy operator

N:D\rightarrow D
is a (nilpotent)
K\otimes_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}}E
-linear endomorphism such that
N\phi=p\phi N
;

\circ

the
Hodge filtration

\{\mathrm{Fil}^{i}D\}_{i\in\mathbf{Z}}
is a decreasing filtration on
D
such that a
K\otimes_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}}E
-submodule
\mathrm{Fil}^{i}D
is

D
if
i\ll 0
and
0
if
i\gg 0
.

The morphisms of filtered
(\phi,N)
-modules are
K\otimes_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}}E
-module homomorphisms that commute with
\phi
and
N
and that preserve the filtration. We also say that a filtered
(\phi,N)
-module
D
is
weakly admissible
if it is in the sense of
[
BM
]
.

Let
V
be a finite-dimensional
E
-vector space equipped with continuous
E
-linear action of
G_{K}
, and define

\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{st}}(V):=(\mathbf{B}_{\mathrm{st}}\otimes_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}}V)^{G_{K}}.

Then
\dim_{K}\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{st}}(V)\leq\dim_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}}V
. If the equality holds, then we say that
V
is
semi-stable
; in that case
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{st}}(V)
inherits from
\mathbf{B}_{\mathrm{st}}
the structure of a weakly admissible filtered
(\phi,N)
-module. We say that
V
is
crystalline
if
V
is semi-stable and the monodromy operator
N
on
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{st}}(V)
is
0
.

Theorem 2.1.2

(
[
CF
]
)
.

The functor
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{st}}
provides an equivalence between the category of semi-stable
E
-representations of
G_{K}
and the category of weakly admissible filtered
(\phi,N,K,E)
-modules.

The functor
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{st}}
does depend on the choice of an element
\pi
in
\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}
with
v_{p}(\pi)>0
. More precisely, the filtration of the filtered
(\phi,N)
-module
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{st}}(V)
depend on the choice of the embedding
\mathbf{B}_{\mathrm{st}}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{B}_{\mathrm{dR}}
which is determined by
\pi
. To avoid this issue, we choose
p\in\mathbf{Q}_{p}
. Note that the functor
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{st}}
restricted to the category of crystalline representations does not depend on
\pi
.

If we write
V^{*}
for the dual representation of
V
, then
V
is semi-stable (resp. crystalline) if and only if so is
V^{*}
. If we denote
\mathrm{D_{st}^{*}}(V):=\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{st}}(V^{*})
, then the functor
\mathrm{D_{st}^{*}}
gives rise to an anti-equivalence between the category of semi-stable
E
-representations of
G_{K}
and the category of weakly admissible filtered
(\phi,N,K,E)
-modules. The quasi-inverse to
\mathrm{D^{*}_{st}}
is given by

\mathrm{V^{*}_{st}}(D):=\Hom_{\phi,N}(D,\mathbf{B}_{\mathrm{st}})\cap\Hom_{\mathrm{Fil}^{*}}(D,\mathbf{B}_{\mathrm{st}}).

(that is, the homomorphisms of
K\otimes_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}}E
-modules that commute with
\phi
and
N
and that preserve the filtration.)

\mathrm{V^{*}_{st}}(D)
inherits an
E
-module structure from the
E
-module structure on
D
and an action of
G_{K}
from the action of
G_{K}
on
\mathbf{B}_{\mathrm{st}}
.

Let
D
be a filtered
(\phi,N,K,E)
-module of rank
n
. Then the isomorphism
\mathbf{Q}_{p^{f}}\otimes_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}}E\cong\bigoplus_{j\in\mathcal{J}}E
induces an isomorphism

D\cong\bigoplus_{j\in\mathcal{J}}D^{(j)}:\,d\mapsto(de_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{J}}

where
D^{(j)}:=De_{j}
and
e_{j}
is the idempotent elements described in the notation section. For each
j\in\mathcal{J}
the isotypic component
D^{(j)}
at
j
is an
n
-dimensional vector space over
E
by (
1.3.2
), and the Frobenius map
\phi
(resp. the monodromy operator
N
) on
D
inherits an
E
-linear map

\phi^{(j)}:D^{(j)}\rightarrow D^{(j+1)}\qquad(\mbox{resp.}\quad N^{(j)}:D^{(j)}\rightarrow D^{(j)})

for all
j\in\mathcal{J}
. Moreover, for each
j\in\mathcal{J}

\mathrm{Fil}^{i}D^{(j)}:=(\mathrm{Fil}^{i}D)e_{j}
is an
E
-linear subspace of
D^{(j)}
, and we may write
\mathrm{Fil}^{i}D=\bigoplus_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\mathrm{Fil}^{i}D^{(j)}
. The
j
-labeled Hodge–Tate weights
of the filtered
(\phi,N)
-module
D
are defined to be the integers
h
such that
\mathrm{Fil}^{h}D^{(j)}\neq\mathrm{Fil}^{h+1}D^{(j)}
, each counted with multiplicity
\dim_{E}(\mathrm{Fil}^{h}D^{(j)}/\mathrm{Fil}^{h+1}D^{(j)})
. We say that
h
is a
Hodge–Tate weight of
D
if it is a
j
-labeled Hodge–Tate weight for some
j\in\mathcal{J}
. When a filtered
(\phi,N)
-module
D
of rank
n
has
n
distinct
j
-labeled Hodge–Tate weights for all
j\in\mathcal{J}
, we say that
D
is
regular
(or that it has regular Hodge–Tate weights). We say that a filtered
(\phi,N)
-module is
positive
if the lowest
j
-labeled Hodge–Tate weight is greater than or equal to
0
for each
j\in\mathcal{J}
. We also say a filtered
(\phi,N)
-module is
parallel
if the
j
-labeled Hodge–Tate weights does not depend on
j
.

If
V
is semi-stable, then by
j
-labeled Hodge–Tate weights of
V
(resp.
Hodge–Tate weights of
V
), we mean those of
\mathrm{D_{st}^{*}}(V)
, and we write
\mathrm{HT}(V)_{j}
for the set of
j
-labeled Hodge–Tate weights of
V
. Our normalization implies that the cyclotomic character
\varepsilon:G_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}}\rightarrow E^{\times}
has Hodge–Tate weight
1
. After a twist of a suitable Lubin–Tate character, we are free to assume that the lowest
j
-labeled Hodge–Tate weight is
0
for all
j\in\mathcal{J}
. Similarly, we say that
V
is
regular
(resp.
positive
, resp.
parallel
) if
\mathrm{D_{st}^{*}}(V)
is. In particular, if
V
is parallel then we have
\mathrm{HT}(V)_{j}=\mathrm{HT}(V)_{j^{\prime}}
for all
j,j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{J}
.

The following example comes from
[
Dous
]
.

Example 2.1.3
.

Let
D
be a free
K\otimes_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}}E
-module of rank
2
with basis
\underline{\eta}:=(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})
, i.e.,
D=K\otimes_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}}E(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})
. We endow
D
with a filtered
(\phi,N,K,E)
-module structure, denoted by
D(\lambda,\{[\xi_{j}:\nu_{j}]\}_{j\in\mathcal{J}})
:

\circ

\Mat_{\underline{\eta}}(N)=\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
1&0\end{bmatrix}
and

\Mat_{\underline{\eta}}(\phi)=\begin{bmatrix}p\lambda&0\\
0&\lambda\end{bmatrix}
for
\lambda\in E
;

\circ

\mathrm{Fil}^{i}D=\bigoplus_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\mathrm{Fil}^{i}D^{(j)}
where for each
j\in\mathcal{J}

\mathrm{Fil}^{i}D^{(j)}=\begin{cases}D^{(j)}&\mbox{if }i\leq 0;\\
E(\xi_{j}\eta_{1}+\nu_{j}\eta_{2})e_{j}&\mbox{if }0<i\leq r_{j};\\
0&\mbox{if }r_{j}<i\end{cases}

for
[\xi_{j}:\nu_{j}]\in\mathbb{P}^{1}(E)
.

It turns out that
D(\lambda,\{[\xi_{j}:\nu_{j}]\}_{j\in\mathcal{J}})
is weakly admissible if and only if

(2.1.4)

v_{p}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{2f}\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}}(r_{j}-1)\quad\mbox{ and }\quad v_{p}(\lambda)\geq\frac{1}{f}\sum_{j\in I_{D}}r_{j},

where we set

I_{D}:=\{j\in\mathcal{J}\mid\xi_{j}=0\}.

Moreover, it is also known that
D(\lambda,\{[\xi_{j}:\nu_{j}]\}_{j\in\mathcal{J}})
exhaust all the
2
-dimensional semi-stable non-crystalline representations
V
of
G_{K}
with
\mathrm{HT}(V)_{j}=\{0,r_{j}\}
for all
j\in\mathcal{J}
.

We often write
\mathfrak{L}_{j}:=[\xi_{j}:\nu_{j}]=\frac{\nu_{j}}{\xi_{j}}\in\mathbb{P}^{1}(E)
and
\vec{\mathfrak{L}}:=(\mathfrak{L}_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{J}}
. In particular, we write
\mathfrak{L}_{j}=\infty
if
\xi_{j}=0
. Note that the data
\left(\lambda,(\mathfrak{L}_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\right)
determines a weakly admissible filtered
(\phi,N)
-module
D(\lambda,(\mathfrak{L}_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{J}})
if
\lambda
satisfies (
2.1.4
).

By easy linear algebra, one can describe the isomorphism classes of
D(\lambda,(\mathfrak{L}_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{J}})
.

Lemma 2.1.5
.

D(\lambda,(\mathfrak{L}_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{J}})
is isomorphic to
D(\lambda^{\prime},(\mathfrak{L}^{\prime}_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{J}})
if and only if
\lambda^{f}=(\lambda^{\prime})^{f}
and
\mathfrak{L}_{j}=\mathfrak{L}^{\prime}_{j}
for all
j\in\mathcal{J}
.

Proof.

Let
f:D(\lambda,(\mathfrak{L}_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{J}})\rightarrow D(\lambda^{\prime},(\mathfrak{L}^{\prime}_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{J}})
be an isomorphism of filtered
(\phi,N)
-modules. From the commutativity with
\phi,N
, for
i=1,2
and
j\in\mathcal{J}
we have
f(\eta_{i}e_{j})=a_{j}\eta_{i}e_{j}
for some
a_{j}\in E^{\times}
with
a_{j+1}\lambda=a_{j}\lambda^{\prime}
. Since
\mathcal{J}
is cyclic, we conclude
\lambda^{f}=(\lambda^{\prime})^{f}
. Moreover, we further conclude that
\mathfrak{L}_{j}=\mathfrak{L}_{j}^{\prime}
for all
j\in\mathcal{J}
, as it should preserve the filtration. For the converse, first observe
\lambda^{\prime}=\zeta\cdot\lambda
for some
f
-th root of unity
\zeta\in E
. If we let
f:D(\lambda,(\mathfrak{L}_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{J}})\rightarrow D(\lambda^{\prime},(\mathfrak{L}^{\prime}_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{J}})
be a
K\otimes_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}}E
-linear map given by
f(\eta_{i}e_{j})=\zeta^{j}\eta_{i}e_{j}
for all
j\in\mathcal{J}
and
i=1,2
, then it is tedious to see that it is an isomorphism between the two filtered
(\phi,N)
-modules.
∎

2.2.
Strongly divisible modules

In this subsection, we introduce strongly divisible modules and their connection to Galois stable lattices of semi-stable representations.

Let
W(k)
be the ring of Witt vectors over
k
so that
K=W(k)[\frac{1}{p}]
. Let
v:=u-p\in W(k)[u]
, and let
S
be the
p
-adic completion of
W(k)[\frac{u^{i}}{i!}]_{i\in\mathbf{N}}
. We endow
S
with the following structure:

\circ

a continuous
\varphi
-semilinear map
\phi:S\rightarrow S
with
\phi(u)=u^{p}
, called the
Frobenius map
on
S
;

\circ

a continuous
W(k)
-linear derivation
N:S\rightarrow S
with
N(u^{i}/i!)=-iu^{i}/i!
, called the
monodromy map
on
S
;

\circ

a decreasing filtration
\{\mathrm{Fil}^{i}S\}_{i\in\mathbf{N}_{0}}
where
\mathrm{Fil}^{i}S
is the
p
-adic completion of
\sum_{j\geq i}\frac{v^{j}}{j!}S
.

Note that
N\phi=p\phi N
and
\phi(\mathrm{Fil}^{i}S)\subset p^{i}S
for
0\leq i\leq p-1
.

Let
S_{\mathcal{O}}:=S\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}\mathcal{O}
and
S_{E}:=S_{\mathcal{O}}\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}\mathbf{Q}_{p}
, and extend the definitions of
\mathrm{Fil}^{*}
,
\phi
, and
N
to
S_{\mathcal{O}}
and
S_{E}

\mathcal{O}
-linearly and
E
-linearly, respectively. Let
\mathcal{MF}(\phi,N,K,E)
be the category whose objects are finite free
S_{E}
-modules
\mathcal{D}
with

\circ

a
\phi
-semilinear and
E
-linear morphism
\phi:\mathcal{D}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
such that the determinant of
\phi
with respect to some choice of
S_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}}
-basis is invertible in
S_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}}
(which does not depend on the choice of basis);

\circ

a decreasing filtration of
\mathcal{D}
by
S_{E}
-submodules
\mathrm{Fil}^{i}\mathcal{D}
,
i\in\mathbf{Z}
, with
\mathrm{Fil}^{i}\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}
for
i\leq 0
and
\mathrm{Fil}^{i}S_{E}\cdot\mathrm{Fil}^{j}\mathcal{D}\subset\mathrm{Fil}^{i+j}\mathcal{D}
for all
j
and all
i\geq 0
;

\circ

a
K_{0}\otimes E
-linear map
N:\mathcal{D}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
such that

–

N(sx)=N(s)x+sN(x)
for all
s\in S_{E}
and
x\in\mathcal{D}
,

–

N\phi=p\phi N
,

–

N(\mathrm{Fil}^{i}\mathcal{D})\subset\mathrm{Fil}^{i-1}\mathcal{D}
for all
i
.

For a filtered
(\phi,N)
-module
D
with positive Hodge–Tate weights, one can associate an object
\mathcal{D}\in\mathcal{MF}(\phi,N,K,E)
by the following:

\circ

\mathcal{D}:=S\otimes_{W(k)}D
;

\circ

\phi:=\phi\otimes\phi:\mathcal{D}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
;

\circ

N:=N\otimes\mathrm{Id}+\mathrm{Id}\otimes N:\mathcal{D}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
;

\circ

\mathrm{Fil}^{0}\mathcal{D}:=\mathcal{D}
and, by induction,

\mathrm{Fil}^{i+1}\mathcal{D}:=\{x\in\mathcal{D}\mid N(x)\in\mathrm{Fil}^{i}\mathcal{D}\mbox{ and }f_{p}(x)\in\mathrm{Fil}^{i+1}D\}

where
f_{p}:\mathcal{D}\twoheadrightarrow D
is defined by
s(u)\otimes x\mapsto s(p)x
.

By
[
Bre97
]
, we see that the functor
\mathcal{D}:D\mapsto S\otimes_{W(k)}D
gives rise to an equivalence between the category of positive filtered
(\phi,N,K,E)
-modules and the category
\mathcal{MF}(\phi,N,K,E)
.

Example 2.2.1
.

Let
D
be the weakly admissible filtered
(\phi,N)
-module in Example
2.1.3
, and fix a positive integer
r
with
r_{j}\leq r<p-1
. If we let
\mathcal{D}:=\mathcal{D}(D)
then we may write

\mathcal{D}=\bigoplus_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\mathcal{D}^{(j)}\supset\mathrm{Fil}^{r}\mathcal{D}=\bigoplus_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\mathrm{Fil}^{r}\mathcal{D}^{(j)}

where
\mathcal{D}^{(j)}:=\mathcal{D}e_{j}
,
\mathrm{Fil}^{r}\mathcal{D}^{(j)}:=(\mathrm{Fil}^{r}\mathcal{D})e_{j}
, and
e_{j}
is the idempotents defined in (
1.3.1
). Then, it is easy to see that for each
j\in\mathcal{J}

\mathrm{Fil}^{r}\mathcal{D}^{(j)}=\mathrm{Fil}^{r-r_{j}}S_{E}\left(\xi_{j}\eta_{1}+\nu_{j}\eta_{2}+\xi_{j}\frac{v}{p}f_{r_{j}}\big(\frac{v}{p}\big)\eta_{2}\right)e_{j}+(\mathrm{Fil}^{r}S_{E}\mathcal{D})e_{j}

where
f_{r_{j}}
is defined in (
1.3.3
), as a
S_{E}e_{j}
-submodule of the isotypic component
\mathcal{D}^{(j)}
.

Definition 2.2.2
.

Fix a positive integer
r<p-1
. A
strongly divisible module of weight
r
is defined to be a free
S_{\mathcal{O}}
-modules
\mathfrak{M}
of finite rank with an
S_{\mathcal{O}}
-submodule
\mathrm{Fil}^{r}\mathfrak{M}
and additive maps
\phi,N:\mathfrak{M}\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}
such that the following properties hold:

\circ

\mathrm{Fil}^{r}S_{\mathcal{O}}\cdot\mathfrak{M}\subset\mathrm{Fil}^{r}\mathfrak{M}
;

\circ

\mathrm{Fil}^{r}\mathfrak{M}\cap I\mathfrak{M}=I\mathrm{Fil}^{r}\mathfrak{M}
for all ideals
I
in
\mathcal{O}
;

\circ

\phi(sx)=\phi(s)\phi(x)
for all
s\in S_{\mathcal{O}}
and for all
x\in\mathfrak{M}
;

\circ

\phi_{r}(\mathrm{Fil}^{r}\mathfrak{M})
is contained in
\mathfrak{M}
and generates it over
S_{\mathcal{O}}
, where
\phi_{r}:=\frac{1}{p^{r}}\phi
;

\circ

N(sx)=N(s)x+sN(x)
for all
s\in S_{\mathcal{O}}
and for all
x\in\mathfrak{M}
;

\circ

N\phi=p\phi N
;

\circ

vN(\mathrm{Fil}^{r}\mathfrak{M})\subset\mathrm{Fil}^{r}\mathfrak{M}
.

The morphisms are
S_{\mathcal{O}}
-linear maps that preserve
\mathrm{Fil}^{r}
and commute with
\phi
and
N
. The category of strongly divisible modules of weight
r
is denoted by
\mathfrak{MD}^{r}_{\mathcal{O}}
.

For a strongly divisible module
\mathfrak{M}
of weight
r
, there exists a unique weakly admissible filtered
(\phi,N)
-module
D
with Hodge–Tate weights lying in
[0,r]
such that
\mathfrak{M}[\frac{1}{p}]\simeq\mathcal{D}(D)
. More precisely, one can construct a free
S_{E}
-module
\mathcal{D}:=\mathfrak{M}\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}\mathbf{Q}_{p}
, and extend
\phi
and
N
on
\mathcal{D}
. The filtration on
\mathcal{D}
is described as follows:
\mathrm{Fil}^{r}\mathcal{D}=\mathrm{Fil}^{r}\mathfrak{M}[\frac{1}{p}]
and

\mathrm{Fil}^{i}\mathcal{D}:=\begin{cases}\mathcal{D}&\mbox{if }i\leq 0;\\
\{x\in\mathcal{D}\mid v^{r-i}x\in\mathrm{Fil}^{r}\mathcal{D}\}&\mbox{if }0\leq i\leq r;\\
\sum_{j=0}^{i-1}(\mathrm{Fil}^{i-j}S_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}})\mathrm{Fil}^{j}\mathcal{D}&\mbox{if }i>r,\mbox{ inductively.}\end{cases}

Let
s_{0}:S_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}}\rightarrow K
and
s_{p}:S_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}}\rightarrow K
be defined by
u\mapsto 0
and
u\mapsto p
, respectively, and let
D:=\mathcal{D}\otimes_{S_{\mathbf{Q}_{p}},s_{0}}K
. The map
s_{0}
induces
\phi
and
N
on
D
, and the map
s_{p}
induces the filtration on
D
by taking
\mathrm{Fil}^{i}D:=s_{p}(\mathrm{Fil}^{i}\mathcal{D})
. Then it turns out that
D
is a weakly admissible filtered
(\phi,N,K,E)
-module with
\mathrm{Fil}^{0}D=D
and
\mathrm{Fil}^{r+1}D=0
.

Hence, one has the following equivalent definition of strongly divisible modules.

Definition 2.2.3
.

For a weakly admissible filtered
(\phi,N)
-module
D
such that
\mathrm{Fil}^{0}D=D
and
\mathrm{Fil}^{r+1}D=0
, a
strongly divisible module
in
\mathcal{D}:=\mathcal{D}(D)
is defined as an
S_{\mathcal{O}}
-submodule
\mathfrak{M}
of
\mathcal{D}
such that

\circ

\mathfrak{M}
is a free
S_{\mathcal{O}}
-module of finite rank such that
\mathfrak{M}[\frac{1}{p}]\simeq\mathcal{D}
;

\circ

\mathfrak{M}
is stable under
\phi
and
N
;

\circ

\phi(\mathrm{Fil}^{r}\mathfrak{M})\subset p^{r}\mathfrak{M}
where
\mathrm{Fil}^{r}\mathfrak{M}:=\mathfrak{M}\cap\mathrm{Fil}^{r}\mathcal{D}
.

For a strongly divisible module
\mathfrak{M}
, an
\mathcal{O}[G_{K}]
-module
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{st}}^{\ast}(\mathfrak{M})
is defined as follows:

\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{st}}^{\ast}(\mathfrak{M}):=\Hom_{S,\mathrm{Fil}^{r},\phi,N}(\mathfrak{M},\widehat{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathrm{st}}).

(see
[
Bre99
]
for detail.)
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{st}}^{\ast}(\mathfrak{M})
inherits an
\mathcal{O}
-module structure from the
\mathcal{O}
-module structure on
\mathfrak{M}
and an action of
G_{K}
from the action of
G_{K}
on
\widehat{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathrm{st}}
.

Theorem 2.2.4

(
[
Bre99
,
Liu08
,
EGH15
]
)
.

Assume that
0<r<p-1
. The functor
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{st}}^{\ast}
provides an anti-equivalence of categories between the category
\mathfrak{MD}^{r}_{\mathcal{O}}
of strongly divisible modules of weight
r
and the category of
G_{K}
-stable
\mathcal{O}
-lattices in semi-stable
E
-representations of
G_{K}
with Hodge–Tate weights lying in
[0,r]
, .

There is also a compatibility: if
\mathfrak{M}
is a strongly divisible module in
\mathcal{D}:=\mathcal{D}(D)
for a weakly admissible filtered
(\phi,N)
-module
D
, then
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{st}}^{\ast}(\mathfrak{M})
is a Galois stable
\mathcal{O}
-lattice in the semi-stable representation
\mathrm{V^{*}_{st}}(D)
.

For a strongly divisible module
\mathfrak{M}
of weight
r
, we often write

\mathfrak{M}=\bigoplus_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\mathfrak{M}^{(j)}\supset\mathrm{Fil}^{r}\mathfrak{M}=\bigoplus_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\mathrm{Fil}^{r}\mathfrak{M}^{(j)}

where
\mathrm{Fil}^{r}\mathfrak{M}^{(j)}:=(\mathrm{Fil}^{r}\mathfrak{M})e_{j}
and
\mathfrak{M}^{(j)}:=\mathfrak{M}e_{j}
. Note that
\mathrm{Fil}^{r}\mathfrak{M}^{(j)}
is a
S_{\mathcal{O}}e_{j}
-submodule of the isotypic component
\mathfrak{M}^{(j)}
. Moreover, the map
\phi
(resp.
N
) on
\mathfrak{M}
inherits the maps

\phi^{(j)}:\mathfrak{M}^{(j)}\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}^{(j+1)}\qquad(\mbox{resp.}\quad N^{(j)}:\mathfrak{M}^{(j)}\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}^{(j)})

for all
j\in\mathcal{J}
. If we write
S^{\prime}
for the
p
-adic completion of
\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\frac{u^{i}}{i!}]_{i\in\mathbf{N}}
and
\phi^{\prime}
is the Frobenius map on
S^{\prime}
, then by identifying
S^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}}:=S^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}\mathcal{O}
with
S_{\mathcal{O}}e_{j}
we may regard
\phi^{(j)}
as a
\phi^{\prime}
-semilinear and
\mathcal{O}
-linear map. The map
\phi^{(j)}
also induces

\phi_{r}^{(j)}:=\frac{1}{p^{r}}\phi^{(j)}:\mathrm{Fil}^{r}\mathfrak{M}^{(j)}\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}^{(j+1)}.

Moreover, if we write
N^{\prime}
for the monodromy map on
S^{\prime}
, then
N^{(j)}
satisfies
N^{(j)}(sx)=N^{\prime}(s)x+sN^{(j)}(x)
for all
s\in S^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}}
and
x\in\mathfrak{M}^{(j)}
.

2.3.
Breuil modules

In this subsection, we introduce Breuil modules and their connection to the mod-
p
reduction of semi-stable representations. Fix again a positive integer
r<p-1
, and let
\overline{S}_{\mathbf{F}}:=S_{\mathcal{O}}/(\varpi,\mathrm{Fil}^{p}S_{\mathcal{O}})=(k\otimes_{\mathbf{F}_{p}}\mathbf{F})[u]/u^{p}
.

Definition 2.3.1
.

A
Breuil modules of weight
r
consists of quadruples
(\mathcal{M},\mathrm{Fil}^{r}\mathcal{M},\phi_{r},N)
where

\circ

\mathcal{M}
is a finitely generated
\overline{S}_{\mathbf{F}}
-module, free over
k[u]/u^{p}
, (which implies that
\mathcal{M}
is in fact a free
\overline{S}_{\mathbf{F}}
-module of finite rank);

\circ

\mathrm{Fil}^{r}\mathcal{M}
is a
\overline{S}_{\mathbf{F}}
-submodule of
\mathcal{M}
containing
u^{r}\mathcal{M}
;

\circ

\phi_{r}:\mathrm{Fil}^{r}\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}
is
\mathbf{F}
-linear and
\phi
-semilinear (where
\phi:k[u]/u^{p}\rightarrow k[u]/u^{p}
is the
p
-th power map) with image generating
\mathcal{M}
as
\overline{S}_{\mathbf{F}}
-module;

\circ

N:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}
is
k\otimes_{\mathbf{F}_{p}}\mathbf{F}
-linear and satisfies

–

N(ux)=uN(x)-ux
for all
x\in\mathcal{M}
,

–

uN(\mathrm{Fil}^{r}\mathcal{M})\subset\mathrm{Fil}^{r}\mathcal{M}
, and

–

\phi_{r}(uN(x))=cN(\phi_{r}(x))
for all
x\in\mathrm{Fil}^{r}\mathcal{M}
, where
c\in(k[u]/u^{p})^{\times}
is the image of
\frac{1}{p}\phi(v)
under the natural map
S\rightarrow k[u]/u^{p}
.

The morphisms are
\overline{S}_{\mathbf{F}}
-module homomorphisms that preserve
\mathrm{Fil}^{r}\mathcal{M}
and commute with
\phi_{r}
and
N
. The category of Breuil modules of weight
r
is denoted by
\mathrm{BrMod}^{r}_{\mathbf{F}}
.

If
\mathfrak{M}
is an object of
\mathfrak{MD}^{r}_{\mathcal{O}}
, then

\mathcal{M}:=\mathfrak{M}/(\varpi,\mathrm{Fil}^{p}S_{\mathcal{O}})\mathfrak{M}

is naturally an object of
\mathrm{BrMod}^{r}_{\mathbf{F}}
. More precisely,

\phi
,
N
, and
\mathrm{Fil}^{r}\mathcal{M}
are induced as follow:

\circ

\mathrm{Fil}^{r}\mathcal{M}
is the image of
\mathrm{Fil}^{r}\mathfrak{M}
in
\mathcal{M}
;

\circ

the map
\phi_{r}
is induced by
\frac{1}{p^{r}}\phi|_{\mathrm{Fil}^{r}\mathfrak{M}}
;

\circ

N
is induced by the one on
\mathfrak{M}
.

Note that this association gives rise to a functor from the category
\mathfrak{MD}^{r}_{\mathcal{O}}
to
\mathrm{BrMod}^{r}_{\mathbf{F}}
.

For a Breuil module
\mathcal{M}
, we define a
\mathbf{F}[G_{K}]
-module
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{st}}^{\ast}(\mathcal{M})
as follows:

(2.3.2)

\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{st}}^{\ast}(\mathcal{M}):=\Hom_{k[u]/u^{p},\mathrm{Fil}^{r},\phi_{r},N}(\mathcal{M},\widehat{\mathbf{A}}).

(See
[
EGH15
]
for detail.)
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{st}}^{\ast}(\mathcal{M})
inherits an
\mathbf{F}
-module structure from the
\mathbf{F}
-module structure on
\mathcal{M}
and an action of
G_{K}
from the action of
G_{K}
on
\widehat{\mathbf{A}}
.

Theorem 2.3.3

(
[
Car06
]
)
.

The functor
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{st}}^{\ast}
gives rise to a fully faithful contravariant functor from the category
\mathrm{BrMod}^{r}_{\mathbf{F}}
to the category of finite-dimensional
\mathbf{F}
-representations of
G_{K}
with
\dim_{\mathbf{F}}\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{st}}^{\ast}(\mathcal{M})=\mathrm{rank}_{(k\otimes_{\mathbf{F}_{p}}\mathbf{F})[u]/u^{p}}\mathcal{M}
.

In general, the functor
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{st}}^{\ast}
is only faithful. More precisely, if
K
is an arbitrary finite extension with the absolute ramification index
e
, it is full when
er<p-1
. In our case, it is fully faithful as we restrict our attention to a finite unramified extension
K
and assume
r\leq p-2
.

There is also a compatibility: if
\mathfrak{M}\in\mathfrak{MD}^{r}_{\mathcal{O}}
and
\mathcal{M}:=\mathfrak{M}/(\varpi,\mathrm{Fil}^{p}S)\mathfrak{M}
denotes the Breuil module corresponding to the reduction of
\mathfrak{M}
, then
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{st}}^{\ast}(\mathfrak{M})\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}\mathbf{F}\simeq\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{st}}^{\ast}(\mathcal{M})
.

For a Breuil module
\mathcal{M}
of weight
r
, we often write

\mathcal{M}=\bigoplus_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\mathcal{M}^{(j)}\supset\mathrm{Fil}^{r}\mathcal{M}=\bigoplus_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\mathrm{Fil}^{r}\mathcal{M}^{(j)}

where
\mathrm{Fil}^{r}\mathcal{M}^{(j)}:=(\mathrm{Fil}^{r}\mathcal{M})e_{j}
,
\mathcal{M}^{(j)}:=\mathcal{M}e_{j}
, and
e_{j}
is the idempotents defined in (
1.3.1
). Note that
\mathrm{Fil}^{r}\mathcal{M}^{(j)}
is a
e_{j}\overline{S}_{\mathbf{F}}(\cong\overline{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{F}})
-submodule of the isotypic component
\mathcal{M}^{(j)}
, where
\overline{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{F}}:=S^{\prime}/(\varpi,\mathrm{Fil}^{p}S^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}})=\mathbf{F}[u]/u^{p}
. Moreover, the map
\phi_{r}
(resp.
N
) on
\mathcal{M}
inherits the maps

\phi_{r}^{(j)}:\mathrm{Fil}^{r}\mathcal{M}^{(j)}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}^{(j+1)}\qquad(\mbox{resp.}\quad N^{(j)}:\mathcal{M}^{(j)}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}^{(j)})

for all
j\in\mathcal{J}
. The map
\phi_{r}^{(j)}
is a
\mathbf{F}
-linear and
\phi^{\prime}
-semilinear map where
\phi^{\prime}:\mathbf{F}_{p}[u]/u^{p}\rightarrow\mathbf{F}_{p}[u]/u^{p}
is the
p
-th power map, and
N^{(j)}
is a
\mathbf{F}
-linear map satisfying
N^{(j)}(ux)=uN^{(j)}(x)-ux
for all
x\in\mathfrak{M}^{(j)}
. Finally, we note that if
\mathcal{M}=\mathfrak{M}/(\varpi,\mathrm{Fil}^{p}S_{\mathcal{O}})
for a strongly divisible module
\mathfrak{M}
of weight
r
, then for each
j\in\mathcal{J}
, the maps
\phi_{r}^{(j)}
,
N^{(j)}
and the filtration
\mathrm{Fil}^{r}\mathcal{M}^{(j)}
on
\mathcal{M}
are compatible with the mod-
p
reduction of the maps
\phi_{r}^{(j)}
,
N^{(j)}
, and the filtration
\mathrm{Fil}^{r}\mathfrak{M}^{(j)}
on
\mathfrak{M}
, respectively.

We close this subsection by giving some examples of Breuil modules for
f=2
, which will be used later. We first introduce a Breuil module of rank
1
.

Example 2.3.4
.

Let
a,b\in[0,r]\cap\mathbf{Z}
and
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbf{F}^{\times}
. Denote by
\mathcal{M}(a,b;\alpha,\beta)
the free
\overline{S}_{\mathbf{F}}
-module
\mathcal{M}
of rank
1
with additional structures:

\circ

\mathcal{M}
is a free
\overline{S}_{\mathbf{F}}
-module of rank
1
generated by
\overline{E}
, i.e.,
\mathcal{M}=\overline{S}_{\mathbf{F}}(\overline{E})
;

\circ

\mathrm{Fil}^{r}\mathcal{M}=\overline{S}_{\mathbf{F}}(u^{a}\overline{E}^{(0)}+u^{b}\overline{E}^{(1)})
, where
\overline{E}^{(j)}:=\overline{E}e_{j}
for all
j\in\mathcal{J}=\mathbf{Z}/2\mathbf{Z}
;

\circ

\phi_{r}(u^{a}\overline{E}^{(0)})=\alpha\overline{E}^{(1)}
and
\phi_{r}(u^{b}\overline{E}^{(1)})=\beta\overline{E}^{(0)}
;

\circ

N(\overline{E}^{(0)})=N(\overline{E}^{(1)})=0
.

Then
\mathcal{M}(a,b;\alpha,\beta)
forms a rank
1
Breuil module of weight
r
.

We now introduce a Breuil module of rank
2
.

Example 2.3.5
.

Fix
i\in\mathcal{J}=\mathbf{Z}/2\mathbf{Z}
, and let

\circ

\vec{a}=(a_{0},a_{1})
and
\vec{b}=(b_{0},b_{1})
with
a_{j},b_{j}\in[0,r]\cap\mathbf{Z}
for all
j\in\mathcal{J}
and
\vec{a}\neq\vec{b}
;

\circ

\vec{\alpha}=(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})
and
\vec{\beta}=(\beta_{0},\beta_{1})
with
\alpha_{j},\beta_{j}\in\mathbf{F}^{\times}
for all
j\in\mathcal{J}
.

Denote by
\mathcal{M}_{i}(\vec{a},\vec{b};\vec{\alpha},\vec{\beta})
the free
\overline{S}_{\mathbf{F}}
-module
\mathcal{M}
of rank
2
with additional structures:

\circ

\mathcal{M}
is a free
\overline{S}_{\mathbf{F}}
-module of rank
2
generated by
(\overline{E}_{1},\overline{E}_{2})
, i.e.,
\mathcal{M}=\overline{S}_{\mathbf{F}}(\overline{E}_{1},\overline{E}_{2})
;

\circ

\mathrm{Fil}^{r}\mathcal{M}
is a
\overline{S}_{\mathbf{F}}
-submodule of
\mathcal{M}
generated by
(\overline{F}_{1},\overline{F}_{2})
;

\circ

\Mat_{\underline{E}^{(j)},\underline{E}^{(j)}}(N^{(j)})=0_{2,2}
for
j\in\mathcal{J}
, where
\underline{E}^{(j)}:=(\overline{E}_{1}e_{j},\overline{E}_{2}e_{j})
;

\circ

\Mat_{\underline{E}^{(j)},\underline{F}^{(j)}}(\mathrm{Fil}^{r}\mathcal{M}^{(j)})=\begin{bmatrix}u^{a_{j}}&0\\
0&u^{b_{j}}\end{bmatrix}
for
j\in\mathcal{J}
, where
\underline{F}^{(j)}:=(\overline{F}_{1}e_{j},\overline{F}_{2}e_{j})
;

\circ

\Mat_{\underline{E}^{(i+1)},\underline{F}^{(i)}}(\phi_{r}^{(i)})=\begin{bmatrix}0&\alpha_{i}\\
\beta_{i}&0\end{bmatrix}
and

\Mat_{\underline{E}^{(i)},\underline{F}^{(i+1)}}(\phi_{r}^{(i+1)})=\begin{bmatrix}\alpha_{i+1}&0\\
0&\beta_{i+1}\end{bmatrix}
.

Then
\mathcal{M}_{i}(\vec{a},\vec{b};\vec{\alpha},\vec{\beta})
forms a rank
2
Breuil module of weight
r
.

Remark 2.3.6
.

There are isomorphisms between
\mathcal{M}_{i}(\vec{a},\vec{b};\vec{\alpha},\vec{\beta})
for different choices of data
(\vec{a},\vec{b};\vec{\alpha},\vec{\beta})
. Let
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}_{i}(\vec{a},\vec{b};\vec{\alpha},\vec{\beta})
, and let
\vec{a}^{\prime}=(a_{0},b_{1})
,
\vec{b}^{\prime}=(b_{0},a_{1})
,
\vec{\alpha}^{\prime}=(\alpha_{0},\beta_{1})
, and
\vec{\beta}^{\prime}=(\beta_{0},\alpha_{1})
. Then we have

(i)

\mathcal{M}\cong\mathcal{M}_{i}(\vec{a},\vec{b};(1,1),(1,\alpha_{0}\alpha_{1}\beta_{0}\beta_{1}))
, after suitable scaling on each bases;

(ii)

\mathcal{M}\cong\mathcal{M}_{i+1}(\vec{a}^{\prime},\vec{b}^{\prime};\vec{\beta}^{\prime},\vec{\alpha}^{\prime})
, via swapping the order in
\underline{E}^{(1)}
and
\underline{F}^{(1)}
;

(iii)

\mathcal{M}\cong\mathcal{M}_{i+1}(\vec{b}^{\prime},\vec{a}^{\prime};\vec{\alpha}^{\prime},\vec{\beta}^{\prime})
, via swapping the order in
\underline{E}^{(0)}
and
\underline{F}^{(0)}
.

We now compute the mod-
p
representations corresponding the Breuil modules above.

Lemma 2.3.7
.

(i)

If we write
\mathcal{M}
for the Breuil module
\mathcal{M}(a,b;\alpha,\beta)
in Example
2.3.4
, then

\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{st}}^{\ast}(\mathcal{M})|_{I_{\mathbf{Q}_{p^{2}}}}\cong\omega_{2}^{(r-a)+(r-b)p}.

(ii)

If we write
\mathcal{M}_{i}
for the Breuil module
\mathcal{M}_{i}(\vec{a},\vec{b};\vec{\alpha},\vec{\beta})
in Example
2.3.5
, then

\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{st}}^{\ast}(\mathcal{M}_{0})|_{I_{\mathbf{Q}_{p^{2}}}}=\omega_{4}^{(r-a_{0})+(r-a_{1})p+(r-b_{0})p^{2}+(r-b_{1})p^{3}}\oplus\omega_{4}^{(r-b_{0})+(r-b_{1})p+(r-a_{0})p^{2}+(r-a_{1})p^{3}}

and

\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{st}}^{\ast}(\mathcal{M}_{1})|_{I_{\mathbf{Q}_{p^{2}}}}=\omega_{4}^{(r-a_{0})+(r-b_{1})p+(r-b_{0})p^{2}+(r-a_{1})p^{3}}\oplus\omega_{4}^{(r-b_{0})+(r-a_{1})p+(r-a_{0})p^{2}+(r-b_{1})p^{3}}.

Proof.

The proofs for both (i) and (ii) are similar to
[
Par17
]
Proposition 2.5, which highly rely on
[
Car06
]
, Theorem 5.2.2.
∎

3.
Frameworks

Fix a positive integer
r^{\prime}
with
1\leq r^{\prime}<p-1
, and let
m:=\lfloor r^{\prime}/2\rfloor
. We also fix
x\in E
and
\Theta\in E^{\times}
. In this section, we deduce various properties of the polynomials
B(T)
and
C(T)
in
\mathcal{O}[T]^{(r^{\prime}-1)}
satisfying the following equation

(3.0.1)

B(T)=\bigg[C(T)\bigg(x+\frac{T}{p}f_{r^{\prime}}\Big(\frac{T}{p}\Big)\bigg)\bigg]_{T}^{(r^{\prime}-1)}

together with
\Theta C(T)\in\mathcal{O}[T]^{(r^{\prime}-1)}
. This equation naturally appears in the filtration of our pseudo-strongly divisible modules, as we will see later.

3.1.
Linear expressions of
B(pT)
and
C(pT)

Let
\mathbb{V}
be a
r^{\prime}
-dimensional vector space over
E
generated by the free variables
C_{0},C_{1},\cdots,C_{r^{\prime}-1}
, and if we let
B(T):=\sum_{i=0}^{r^{\prime}-1}B_{i}T^{i}
and
C(T):=\sum_{i=0}^{r^{\prime}-1}C_{i}T^{i}
are polynomials in
T
satisfying (
3.0.1
) then it is clear that
B_{0},B_{1},\cdots,B_{r^{\prime}-1}
belong to
\mathbb{V}
. Let
\mathbb{M}
be the
\mathcal{O}
-submodule of
\mathbb{V}
generated by

\Theta C_{0},\cdots,\Theta C_{r^{\prime}-1},B_{0},\cdots,B_{r^{\prime}-1}.

As
\mathcal{O}
is a principal ideal domain, we see that
\mathbb{M}
is a free
\mathcal{O}
-module of rank
r^{\prime}
, and set
D_{0},\cdots,D_{r^{\prime}-1}
to be the generators of
\mathbb{M}
, depending on the
p
-adic valuations of
x
and
\Theta
.

In this subsection, for a given choice of the generators
D_{0},D_{1},\cdots,D_{r^{\prime}-1}
of
\mathbb{M}
, we describe
C(pT)
and
B(pT)
as linear combinations of
D_{i}
’s and introduce various properties of the coefficients of the linear combinations. We consider
(r^{\prime}+1)
choices of the generators
D_{0},D_{1},\cdots,D_{r^{\prime}-1}
of
\mathbb{M}
described as follow: for a given integer
k
with
0\leq k\leq r^{\prime}
,
D_{0},\cdots,D_{r^{\prime}-1}
are determined by

(3.1.1)

\begin{cases}D_{0}=B_{r^{\prime}-k}\\
\vdots\\
D_{k-1}=B_{r^{\prime}-1}\\
D_{k}=\Theta C_{k}\\
\vdots\\
D_{r^{\prime}-1}=\Theta C_{r^{\prime}-1}.\end{cases}

By
\mathbf{Case}~(k)
, we mean that we choose the generators
D_{0},D_{1},\cdots,D_{r^{\prime}-1}
as displayed in (
3.1.1
). For instances, by
\mathbf{Case}~(0)
we mean
D_{i}=\Theta C_{i}
for all
0\leq i\leq r^{\prime}-1
, and by
\mathbf{Case}~(r^{\prime})
we mean that
D_{i}=B_{i}
for all
0\leq i\leq r^{\prime}-1
. This choice of the generators will be illustrated in terms of
v_{p}(x)
and
v_{p}(\Theta)
(see Theorem
4.1.10
). The reason why we consider these
(r^{\prime}+1)
cases is illustrated in the following.

Remark 3.1.2
.

We choose the generators
D_{0},\cdots,D_{r^{\prime}-1}
which have the highest possible
p
-powers, which can be readily seen in the matrix equation (
3.1.3
) induced from (
3.0.1
). Hence, it is very likely to imply the rest of the variables to be integral under weaker conditions on
v_{p}(x)
and
v_{p}(\Theta)
. In §
4.2
for each choice of generators
D_{0},\cdots,D_{r^{\prime}-1}
we will determine the generators of our pseudo-strongly divisible modules and give the precise conditions on
v_{p}(x)
and
v_{p}(\Theta)
in each case.

For the rest of this subsection, we will consider
x
as well as
C_{0},C_{1},\cdots,C_{r^{\prime}-1}
as free variables, unless otherwise stated. As it is easier to handle, we consider

B(pT)=[C(pT)(x+Tf_{r^{\prime}}(T))]_{T}^{(r^{\prime}-1)},

which is obtained by plugging in
pT
to the equation in (
3.0.1
), and this identity can be described by the following identity of column vectors:

(3.1.3)

\begin{bmatrix}p^{r^{\prime}-1}B_{r^{\prime}-1}\\
p^{r^{\prime}-2}B_{r^{\prime}-2}\\
\vdots\\
pB_{1}\\
B_{0}\end{bmatrix}=M_{r^{\prime},x}\begin{bmatrix}p^{r^{\prime}-1}C_{r^{\prime}-1}\\
p^{r^{\prime}-2}C_{r^{\prime}-2}\\
\vdots\\
pC_{1}\\
C_{0}\end{bmatrix}

where
M_{r^{\prime},x}
is the
(r^{\prime}\times r^{\prime})
-matrix described by

\displaystyle M_{r^{\prime},x}:=\begin{bmatrix}x&1&-\frac{1}{2}&\cdots&\frac{(-1)^{r^{\prime}-3}}{r^{\prime}-2}&\frac{(-1)^{r^{\prime}-2}}{r^{\prime}-1}\\
0&x&1&\cdots&\frac{(-1)^{r^{\prime}-4}}{r^{\prime}-3}&\frac{(-1)^{r^{\prime}-3}}{r^{\prime}-2}\\
0&0&x&\cdots&\frac{(-1)^{r^{\prime}-5}}{r^{\prime}-4}&\frac{(-1)^{r^{\prime}-4}}{r^{\prime}-3}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&0&0&\cdots&x&1\\
0&0&0&\cdots&0&x\end{bmatrix}\in\mathrm{M}_{r^{\prime}\times r^{\prime}}(\mathbf{Z}_{(p)}[x]).

For each integer
k
with
0\leq k\leq r^{\prime}
, we write
M_{r^{\prime},x}
as block matrices

M_{r^{\prime},x}=\begin{bmatrix}A_{k,x}&B_{k,x}\\
C_{k,x}&D_{k,x}\end{bmatrix}

with
A_{k,x}\in\mathrm{M}_{k\times(r^{\prime}-k)}(\mathbf{Z}_{(p)}[x])
,
B_{k,x}\in\mathrm{M}_{k\times k}(\mathbf{Z}_{(p)}[x])
,
C_{k,x}\in\mathrm{M}_{(r^{\prime}-k)\times(r^{\prime}-k)}(\mathbf{Z}_{(p)}[x])
, and
D_{k,x}\in\mathrm{M}_{(r^{\prime}-k)\times k}(\mathbf{Z}_{(p)}[x])
. In particular, we have
M_{r^{\prime},x}=C_{0,x}=B_{r^{\prime},x}
.

The matrix
B_{k,x}
as well as
A_{k,x}
will play important role on describing the generators of the filtration of pseudo-strongly divisible modules, as we will see later. We observe that if
0\leq k\leq m
then

(3.1.4)

B_{k,x}=\begin{bmatrix}\frac{(-1)^{r^{\prime}-k-1}}{r^{\prime}-k}&\frac{(-1)^{r^{\prime}-k}}{r^{\prime}-k+1}&\cdots&\frac{(-1)^{r^{\prime}-2}}{r^{\prime}-1}\\
\frac{(-1)^{r^{\prime}-k-2}}{r^{\prime}-k-1}&\frac{(-1)^{r^{\prime}-k}}{r^{\prime}-k+1}&\cdots&\frac{(-1)^{r^{\prime}-3}}{r^{\prime}-2}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\frac{(-1)^{r^{\prime}-2k}}{r^{\prime}-2k+1}&\frac{(-1)^{r^{\prime}-2k+1}}{r^{\prime}-2k+2}&\cdots&\frac{(-1)^{r^{\prime}-k-1}}{r^{\prime}-k}\end{bmatrix}\in\mathrm{M}_{k\times k}(\mathbf{Z}_{(p)})

which is independent of
x
, and so
B_{k,x}=B_{k,0}
, and if
m+1\leq k\leq r^{\prime}
then

(3.1.5)

B_{k,x}=\begin{bmatrix}\frac{(-1)^{r^{\prime}-k-1}}{r^{\prime}-k}&\cdots&\frac{(-1)^{k-2}}{k-1}&\frac{(-1)^{k-1}}{k}&\cdots&\frac{(-1)^{r^{\prime}-2}}{r^{\prime}-1}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots&\cdots&\vdots\\
1&\cdots&\frac{(-1)^{2k-r^{\prime}-1}}{2k-r^{\prime}}&\frac{(-1)^{2k-r^{\prime}}}{2k-r^{\prime}+1}&\cdots&\frac{(-1)^{k-1}}{k}\\
x&\cdots&\frac{(-1)^{2k-r^{\prime}-2}}{2k-r^{\prime}-1}&\frac{(-1)^{2k-r^{\prime}-1}}{2k-r^{\prime}}&\cdots&\frac{(-1)^{k-2}}{k-1}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&\cdots&x&1&\cdots&\frac{(-1)^{r^{\prime}-k-1}}{r^{\prime}-k}\end{bmatrix}\in\mathrm{M}_{k\times k}(\mathbf{Z}_{(p)}[x]),
