where the number of
x
’s on the matrix is
2k-r^{\prime}
.

We now assume
\mathbf{Case}~(k)
and consider the following

(3.1.6)

\begin{bmatrix}\frac{p^{r^{\prime}-1}}{\Theta}D_{r^{\prime}-1}\\
\vdots\\
\frac{p^{k}}{\Theta}D_{k}\\
p^{r^{\prime}-1}D_{k-1}\\
\vdots\\
p^{r^{\prime}-k}D_{0}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}p^{r^{\prime}-1}C_{r^{\prime}-1}\\
\vdots\\
p^{k}C_{k}\\
p^{r^{\prime}-1}B_{r^{\prime}-1}\\
\vdots\\
p^{r^{\prime}-k}B_{r^{\prime}-k}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}I_{r^{\prime}-k}&0_{r^{\prime}-k,k}\\
A_{k,x}&B_{k,x}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}p^{r^{\prime}-1}C_{r^{\prime}-1}\\
p^{r^{\prime}-2}C_{r^{\prime}-2}\\
\vdots\\
pC_{1}\\
C_{0}\end{bmatrix}

where the first equality is due to
\mathbf{Case}~(k)
and the second equality follows from (
3.0.1
), which together with (
3.1.3
) implies

(3.1.7)

\begin{bmatrix}p^{r^{\prime}-1}B_{r^{\prime}-1}\\
p^{r^{\prime}-2}B_{r^{\prime}-2}\\
\vdots\\
pB_{1}\\
B_{0}\end{bmatrix}=M_{r^{\prime},x}\begin{bmatrix}p^{r^{\prime}-1}C_{r^{\prime}-1}\\
p^{r^{\prime}-2}C_{r^{\prime}-2}\\
\vdots\\
pC_{1}\\
C_{0}\end{bmatrix}=M_{r^{\prime},x}\begin{bmatrix}I_{r^{\prime}-k}&0_{r^{\prime}-k,k}\\
A_{k,x}&B_{k,x}\end{bmatrix}^{-1}\begin{bmatrix}\frac{p^{r^{\prime}-1}}{\Theta}D_{r^{\prime}-1}\\
\vdots\\
\frac{p^{k}}{\Theta}D_{k}\\
p^{r^{\prime}-1}D_{k-1}\\
\vdots\\
p^{r^{\prime}-k}D_{0}\end{bmatrix}.

For each integer
i
with
0\leq i\leq r^{\prime}-1
, we define
P_{k,i}(T,x)
and
Q_{k,i}(T,x)
in
\mathbf{Q}(x)[T]
as follow:

P_{k,i}(T,x):=\begin{bmatrix}T^{r^{\prime}-1}&T^{r^{\prime}-2}&\cdots&1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}I_{r^{\prime}-k}&0_{r^{\prime}-k,k}\\
A_{k,x}&B_{k,x}\end{bmatrix}^{-1}\mathbf{e}_{r^{\prime}-i}

and

Q_{k,i}(T,x):=\begin{bmatrix}T^{r^{\prime}-1}&T^{r^{\prime}-2}&\cdots&1\end{bmatrix}M_{r^{\prime},x}\begin{bmatrix}I_{r^{\prime}-k}&0_{r^{\prime}-k,k}\\
A_{k,x}&B_{k,x}\end{bmatrix}^{-1}\mathbf{e}_{r^{\prime}-i}

where
\mathbf{e}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{e}_{r^{\prime}}
are the standard Euclidean basis of column vectors.

For a later use, we point out that

(3.1.8)

\begin{bmatrix}I_{r^{\prime}-k}&0_{r^{\prime}-k,k}\\
A_{k,x}&B_{k,x}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{cases}I_{r^{\prime}}&\mbox{if }k=0;\\
B_{r^{\prime},x}=M_{r^{\prime},x}&\mbox{if }k=r^{\prime}.\end{cases}

Moreover, if
1\leq k\leq r^{\prime}-1
then it is easy to see that

(3.1.9)

\begin{bmatrix}I_{r^{\prime}-k}&0_{r^{\prime}-k,k}\\
A_{k,x}&B_{k,x}\end{bmatrix}^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}I_{r^{\prime}-k}&0_{r^{\prime}-k,k}\\
-B_{k,x}^{-1}A_{k,x}&B_{k,x}^{-1}\end{bmatrix}

and

(3.1.10)

M_{r^{\prime},x}\begin{bmatrix}I_{r^{\prime}-k}&0_{r^{\prime}-k,k}\\
A_{k,x}&B_{k,x}\end{bmatrix}^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}0_{k,r^{\prime}-k}&I_{k}\\
C_{k,x}-D_{k,x}B_{k,x}^{-1}A_{k,x}&D_{k,x}B_{k,x}^{-1}\end{bmatrix}.

Lemma 3.1.11
.

For each integer
k
with
0\leq k\leq r^{\prime}
,
\mathbf{Case}~(k)
implies that

C(pT)=\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}p^{i+r^{\prime}-k}D_{i}P_{k,i}(T,x)+\sum_{i=k}^{r^{\prime}-1}\frac{p^{i}}{\Theta}D_{i}P_{k,i}(T,x)

and

B(pT)=\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}p^{i+r^{\prime}-k}D_{i}Q_{k,i}(T,x)+\sum_{i=k}^{r^{\prime}-1}\frac{p^{i}}{\Theta}D_{i}Q_{k,i}(T,x).

Proof.

The identity for
C(pT)
follows immediately from (
3.1.6
). Similarly, the identity for
B(pT)
follows immediately from (
3.1.7
).
∎

When
k=0
and
k=r^{\prime}
,
P_{k,i}(T,x)
and
Q_{k,i}(T,x)
can be written explicitly.

Lemma 3.1.12
.

For
0\leq i\leq r^{\prime}-1
we have

Q_{0,i}(T,x)=[T^{i}(x+Tf_{r^{\prime}}(T))]_{T}^{(r^{\prime}-1)},\quad P_{0,i}(T,x)=T^{i}=Q_{r^{\prime},i}(T,x),

and

P_{r^{\prime},i}(T,x)=x^{-r^{\prime}}\sum_{s=i}^{r^{\prime}-1}x^{s}(-1)^{r^{\prime}-s-1}\Big[T^{i+r^{\prime}-s-1}f_{r^{\prime}}(T)^{r^{\prime}-s-1}\Big]_{T}^{(r^{\prime}-1)}.

In particular,
P_{k,i}(T,x)
and
Q_{k,i}(T,x)
belong to
\mathbf{Z}_{(p)}[x,\frac{1}{x},T]
if
k=0
or
k=r^{\prime}
, and

Q_{k,i}(T,x)=[(x+Tf_{r^{\prime}}(T))P_{k,i}(T,x)]_{T}^{(r^{\prime}-1)}

for
k\in\{0,r^{\prime}\}
and for all
0\leq i\leq r^{\prime}-1
.

For
r^{\prime}=1
the above lemma yields
Q_{0,0}(T,x)=x
,
P_{0,0}(T,x)=1=Q_{1,0}(T,x)
, and
P_{1,0}(T,x)=x^{-1}
, where we use the convention
f_{1}(T)=0
and
0^{0}=1
for the last one.

Proof.

From (
3.1.8
), it is immediate that
P_{0,i}(T,x)=T^{i}=Q_{r^{\prime},i}(T,x)
for all
0\leq i\leq r^{\prime}-1
.
Moreover, it is also easy to see that

Q_{0,i}(T,x)=\begin{bmatrix}T^{r^{\prime}-1}&T^{r^{\prime}-2}&\cdots&1\end{bmatrix}M_{r^{\prime},x}\mathbf{e}_{r^{\prime}-i}=[T^{i}(x+Tf_{r^{\prime}}(T))]_{T}^{(r^{\prime}-1)}.

We now check the identity for
P_{r^{\prime},i}(T,x)
. It is easy to see that

(3.1.13)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\big(x+Tf_{r^{\prime}}(T)\big)\sum_{s=0}^{r^{\prime}-1}x^{s}\big(-Tf_{r^{\prime}}(T)\big)^{r^{\prime}-s-1}=x^{r^{\prime}}-(-1)^{r^{\prime}}\big(Tf_{r^{\prime}}(T)\big)^{r^{\prime}}\equiv x^{r^{\prime}}\pmod{T^{r^{\prime}}},\end{split}

which implies that

\displaystyle C(pT)

\displaystyle\equiv C(pT)(x+Tf_{r^{\prime}}(T))\sum_{s=0}^{r^{\prime}-1}x^{s}\big(-Tf_{r^{\prime}}(T)\big)^{r^{\prime}-s-1}\cdot x^{-r^{\prime}}\pmod{T^{r^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle\equiv B(pT)\sum_{s=0}^{r^{\prime}-1}x^{s-r^{\prime}}\big(-Tf_{r^{\prime}}(T)\big)^{r^{\prime}-s-1}\pmod{T^{r^{\prime}}}.

Hence, the polynomial
\big[\sum_{s=0}^{r^{\prime}-1}x^{s-r^{\prime}}\big(-Tf_{r^{\prime}}(T)\big)^{r^{\prime}-s-1}\big]^{(r^{\prime}-1)}_{T}
determines the matrix
M_{r^{\prime},x}^{-1}
by (
3.1.3
), so that we have

\displaystyle P_{r^{\prime},i}(T,x)=\begin{bmatrix}T^{r^{\prime}-1}&T^{r^{\prime}-2}&\cdots&1\end{bmatrix}M_{r^{\prime},x}^{-1}\mathbf{e}_{r^{\prime}-i}=\bigg[T^{i}\sum_{s=0}^{r^{\prime}-1}x^{s-r^{\prime}}\big(-Tf_{r^{\prime}}(T)\big)^{r^{\prime}-s-1}\bigg]^{(r^{\prime}-1)}_{T}.

Finally,
P_{k,i}(T,x)
and
Q_{k,i}(T,x)
obviously belong to
\mathbf{Z}_{(p)}[x,\frac{1}{x},T]
as
r^{\prime}<p-1
, and the last part is also clear from the statement.
∎

When
0<k<r^{\prime}
and
x
is specialized in
E
, we can also describe the degrees of
P_{k,i}(T,x)
and
Q_{k,i}(T,x)
in
T
.

Lemma 3.1.14
.

Let
1\leq k\leq r^{\prime}-1
, and assume that
x\in E
with
\det(B_{k,x})\neq 0
. Then for each
0\leq i\leq r^{\prime}-1
,

\big(P_{k,i}(T,x),Q_{k,i}(T,x)\big)

is the unique pair of polynomials in
T
satisfying the following properties:

(i)

Q_{k,i}(T,x)=[(x+Tf_{r^{\prime}}(T))P_{k,i}(T,x)]_{T}^{(r^{\prime}-1)}
;

(ii)

if
0\leq i\leq k-1
, then
\deg_{T}P_{k,i}(T,x)\leq k-1
and
\deg_{T}\big(Q_{k,i}(T,x)-T^{i+r^{\prime}-k}\big)\leq r^{\prime}-k-1
;

(iii)

if
k\leq i\leq r^{\prime}-1
, then
\deg_{T}\big(P_{k,i}(T,x)-T^{i}\big)\leq k-1
and
\deg_{T}Q_{k,i}(T,x)\leq r^{\prime}-k-1
.

For
1\leq k\leq r^{\prime}
we write
a_{k}(x)
for the coefficient of
T^{k-1}
in the polynomial
P_{k,0}(T,x)
, where
k-1
is the maximal possible degree of
P_{k,0}(T,x)
by Lemma
3.1.14
. We often write
a_{k}
for
a_{k}(0)
for simplicity. Note that
a_{r^{\prime}}(0)
is not defined, because
a_{r^{\prime}}(x)
is a non-constant polynomial of
x^{-1}
(see Lemma
3.1.12
).

Proof.

We first check that the pair
(P_{k,i}(T,x),Q_{k,i}(T,x))
satisfies those properties. For (i), it is immediate from the definition of
P_{k,i}(T,x)
that

\displaystyle\big[(x+Tf_{r^{\prime}}(T))P_{k,i}(T,x)\big]_{T}^{(r^{\prime}-1)}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}T^{r^{\prime}-1}&T^{r^{\prime}-2}&\cdots&1\end{bmatrix}M_{r^{\prime},x}\begin{bmatrix}I_{r^{\prime}-k}&0_{r^{\prime}-k,k}\\
A_{k,x}&B_{k,x}\end{bmatrix}^{-1}\mathbf{e}_{r^{\prime}-i}

\displaystyle=Q_{k,i}(T,x).

For (ii), let
0\leq i\leq k-1
and let
\mathbf{e}^{\prime}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{e}^{\prime}_{k}
be the standard basis of
\mathbf{Q}^{k}
of collum vectors. Then by (
3.1.9
) we have

\displaystyle P_{k,i}(T,x)

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}T^{r^{\prime}-1}&T^{r^{\prime}-2}&\cdots&1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}I_{r^{\prime}-k}&0_{r^{\prime}-k,k}\\
-B_{k,x}^{-1}A_{k,x}&B_{k,x}^{-1}\end{bmatrix}\mathbf{e}_{r^{\prime}-i}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}T^{r^{\prime}-1}&T^{r^{\prime}-2}&\cdots&1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}0_{r^{\prime}-k,k}\\
B_{k,x}^{-1}\end{bmatrix}\mathbf{e}^{\prime}_{k-i}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}T^{k-1}&T^{k-2}&\cdots&1\end{bmatrix}B_{k,x}^{-1}\mathbf{e}^{\prime}_{k-i},

and by (
3.1.10
) we have

\displaystyle Q_{k,i}(T,x)

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}T^{r^{\prime}-1}&T^{r^{\prime}-2}&\cdots&1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}0_{k,r^{\prime}-k}&I_{k}\\
C_{k,x}-D_{k,x}B_{k,x}^{-1}A_{k,x}&D_{k,x}B_{k,x}^{-1}\end{bmatrix}\mathbf{e}_{r^{\prime}-i}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}T^{r^{\prime}-1}&T^{r^{\prime}-2}&\cdots&1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}I_{k}\\
D_{k,x}B_{k,x}^{-1}\end{bmatrix}\mathbf{e}^{\prime}_{k-i}

\displaystyle=T^{i+r^{\prime}-k}+\begin{bmatrix}T^{k-1}&T^{k-2}&\cdots&1\end{bmatrix}D_{k,x}B_{k,x}^{-1}\mathbf{e}^{\prime}_{k-i}.

For (iii), let
k\leq i\leq r^{\prime}-1
and let
\mathbf{e}^{\prime\prime}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{e}^{\prime\prime}_{r^{\prime}-k}
be the standard basis of
\mathbf{Q}^{r^{\prime}-k}
of collum vectors. Then by the same idea as (ii), we get

P_{k,i}(T,x)=T^{i}-\begin{bmatrix}T^{k-1}&T^{k-2}&\cdots&1\end{bmatrix}B_{k,x}^{-1}A_{k,x}\mathbf{e}^{\prime\prime}_{r^{\prime}-i}

and

Q_{k,i}(T,x)=\begin{bmatrix}T^{r^{\prime}-k-1}&T^{r^{\prime}-k-2}&\cdots&1\end{bmatrix}(C_{k,x}-D_{k,x}B_{k,x}^{-1}A_{k,x})\mathbf{e}^{\prime\prime}_{r^{\prime}-i}.

Hence, we proved that the pair satisfies those properties.

The rest of the proof is devoted to the uniqueness. For given
k
and
i
, let
(p(T),q(T))
be a pair of polynomials in
E[T]
satisfying those properties. If
0\leq i\leq k-1
then by the property (ii) we may write

p(T)=p_{0}+p_{1}T+\cdots+p_{k-1}T^{k-1}\,\,\mbox{and}\,\,q(T)=q_{0}+q_{1}T+\cdots+q_{r^{\prime}-k-1}T^{r^{\prime}-k-1}+T^{i+r^{\prime}-k},

and if
k\leq i\leq r^{\prime}-1
then by the property (iii) we may write

p(T)=p_{0}+p_{1}T+\cdots+p_{k-1}T^{k-1}+T^{i}\,\,\mbox{and}\,\,q(T)=q_{0}+q_{1}T+\cdots+q_{r^{\prime}-k-1}T^{r^{\prime}-k-1}.

Then from the property (i) we have

\displaystyle\left[\sum_{s=0}^{k-1}p_{s}T^{s}(x+Tf_{r^{\prime}}(T))\right]_{T}^{(r^{\prime}-1)}-\sum_{s=0}^{r^{\prime}-k-1}q_{s}T^{s}=\begin{cases}T^{i+r^{\prime}-k}&\mbox{if }0\leq i<k;\\
[T^{i}(x+Tf_{r^{\prime}}(T))]_{T}^{(r^{\prime}-1)}&\mbox{if }k\leq i<r^{\prime}.\end{cases}

We now write the left hand side of the identity as matrix multiplication.

\displaystyle[

\displaystyle(p_{0}+\cdots+p_{k-1}T^{k-1})(x+Tf_{r^{\prime}}(T))]_{T}^{(r^{\prime}-1)}-(q_{0}+\cdots+q_{r^{\prime}-k-1}T^{r^{\prime}-k-1})

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}T^{r^{\prime}-1}&\cdots&1\end{bmatrix}\left(M_{r^{\prime},x}\begin{bmatrix}0_{r^{\prime}-k,1}\\
\mathbf{p}\end{bmatrix}-\begin{bmatrix}0_{k,1}\\
\mathbf{q}\end{bmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}T^{r^{\prime}-1}&\cdots&1\end{bmatrix}\left(\begin{bmatrix}A_{k,x}&B_{k,x}\\
C_{k,x}&D_{k,x}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}0_{r^{\prime}-k,k}&0_{r^{\prime}-k,r^{\prime}-k}\\
I_{k}&0_{k,r^{\prime}-k}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{p}\\
\mathbf{q}\end{bmatrix}-\begin{bmatrix}0_{k,k}&0_{k,r^{\prime}-k}\\
0_{r^{\prime}-k,k}&I_{r^{\prime}-k}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{p}\\
\mathbf{q}\end{bmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}T^{r^{\prime}-1}&\cdots&1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}B_{k,x}&0_{k,r^{\prime}-k}\\
D_{k,x}&-I_{r^{\prime}-k}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{p}\\
\mathbf{q}\end{bmatrix}

where
\mathbf{p}=\begin{bmatrix}p_{k-1}&\cdots&p_{0}\end{bmatrix}^{t}
and

\mathbf{q}=\begin{bmatrix}q_{r^{\prime}-k-1}&\cdots&p_{0}\end{bmatrix}^{t}
. Therefore, the uniqueness follows from the non-singularity

\det\left(\begin{bmatrix}B_{k,x}&0_{k,r^{\prime}-k}\\
D_{k,x}&-I_{r^{\prime}-k}\end{bmatrix}\right)=(-1)^{r^{\prime}-k}\det(B_{k,x})\neq 0,

where the last inequality is due to our assumption.
∎

We further study the quotient
Q_{k,i}(T,x)/P_{k,i}(T,x)
for
i\in\{0,k\}
which will be used to define the coefficients of our strongly divisible modules when we specialize
x
in
E
.

We recall the definition of Padé approximation.

Definition 3.1.15

(Padé approximation)
.

Let
R[[T]]
be the power series ring over a commutative ring
R
. For
f(T)\in R[[T]]
and for a pair of integers
(m,n)
with
m\geq 0
and
n\geq 0
, the
[m/n]
-Padé approximant of
f(T)
is a rational function

\frac{Q(T)}{P(T)}\equiv f(T)\pmod{T^{m+n+1}}

where
P(T)\in R[T]
(resp.
Q(T)\in R[T]
) is a polynomial with
\deg P(T)\leq n
(resp.
\deg Q(T)\leq m
).

Note that if such a pair
(P(T),Q(T))
exists, then the quotient
P(T)/Q(T)
is unique, although the pair
(P(T),Q(T))
may not be unique up to scalar multiple in general.

Lemma 3.1.16
.

Let
0\leq k\leq r^{\prime}
, and assume that
x\in E
with
\det(B_{k,x})\neq 0
.

(i)

For each
1\leq k\leq r^{\prime}
,
Q_{k,0}(T,x)
is monic and
Q_{k,0}(T,x)/P_{k,0}(T,x)
is the
[(r^{\prime}-k)/(k-1)]
-Padé approximation of
x+\log(1+T)
.

(ii)

For each
0\leq k\leq r^{\prime}-1
,
P_{k,k}(T,x)
is monic and
Q_{k,k}(T,x)/P_{k,k}(T,x)
is the
[(r^{\prime}-k-1)/k]
-Padé approximation of
x+\log(1+T)
.

(iii)

For
1\leq k\leq r^{\prime}
, we have

P_{k-1,k-1}(T,x)=\frac{1}{a_{k}(x)}P_{k,0}(T,x)\quad\mbox{and}\quad Q_{k-1,k-1}(T,x)=\frac{1}{a_{k}(x)}Q_{k,0}(T,x).

Note that
a_{k}(x)
is defined right after Lemma
3.1.14
.

Proof.

(i) and (ii) follows immediately from Lemma
3.1.14
together with Lemma
3.1.12
. (iii) follows from the uniqueness in Lemma
3.1.14
if
k<r^{\prime}
and from the explicit formula in Lemma
3.1.12
if
k=r^{\prime}
.
∎

3.2.
The polynomials
P_{k,i}(T,x)
and
Q_{k,i}(T,x)

In this subsection, we study the necessary properties of
P_{k,i}(T,x)
and
Q_{k,i}(T,x)
as polynomials in
x
. Later, we will specialize
x
in
E
and so it is necessary to investigate the form of those polynomials as polynomials in
x
, to keep track the
p
-adic valuation of the coefficients of those polynomials. We keep the notation of §
3.1
.

3.2.1.
When
0\leq k\leq m

In this section of paragraph, we treat the case
0\leq k\leq m
. We first show that the coefficients of
P_{k,i}(T,x)
and
Q_{k,i}(T,x)
are
p
-adically integral if take
x=0
.

Lemma 3.2.1
.

Let
1\leq k\leq m
. Then

(i)

B_{k,x}=B_{k,0}
and
v_{p}(\det(B_{k,0}))=0
;

(ii)

P_{k,i}(T,0),Q_{k,i}(T,0)\in\mathbf{Z}_{(p)}[T]
for all
0\leq i\leq r^{\prime}-1
;

(iii)

a_{k}\in\mathbf{Z}_{(p)}^{\times}
and
P_{k,0}(T,x)=P_{k,0}(T,0)
.

Proof.

We first point out that if
1\leq k\leq m
then none of the entries of
B_{k,x}
is
x
(see (
3.1.4
)), which proves the first identity of (i). The second identity of (i) follows from the proof of
[
GP19
, Lemma 4.1.1]
.

For (ii), we have
B_{k,0}^{-1}\in\mathrm{M}_{k\times k}(\mathbf{Z}_{(p)})
and
D_{k,0}B_{k,0}^{-1}\in\mathrm{M}_{(r^{\prime}-k)\times k}(\mathbf{Z}_{(p)})
, as
\det(B_{k,0})\in\mathbf{Z}_{(p)}^{\times}
and
D_{k,x}\in\mathrm{M}_{(r^{\prime}-k)\times k}(\mathbf{Z}_{(p)})
. Now, (
3.1.9
) and (
3.1.10
) completes the proof of (ii).

For (iii), as
B_{k,x}^{-1}=B_{k,0}^{-1}\in\mathrm{M}_{k\times k}(\mathbf{Z}_{(p)})
, (
3.1.9
) implies that
P_{k,0}(T,x)=P_{k,0}(T,0)
. Moreover, we see that the
(1,k)
-entry of
B_{k,x}^{-1}
is
a_{k}(x)=a_{k}
by definition. On the other hand, the
(1,k)
-entry of
B_{k,x}^{-1}
can be also written as
\frac{(-1)^{1+k}\det(B_{k-1,x})}{\det(B_{k,x})}
, where we let
\det(B_{0,x})=1
by convention. Hence, by (i) we conclude that
v_{p}(a_{k})=0
, which completes the proof.
∎

For
m+1\leq k\leq r^{\prime}-1
, this statement does not hold in general. For example, if
r^{\prime}=6
,
k=4
and
p=19
, then

\det B_{4,0}=-\frac{19}{720},

and so
v_{19}(\det B_{4,0})\neq 0
.

We further study the necessary properties of
P_{k,i}(T,x)
and
Q_{k,i}(T,x)
as polynomials in
x
. Recall that if
0\leq k\leq m
, then the matrix
B_{k,x}
is of the form (
3.1.4
), so that we have
B_{k,x}=B_{k,0}
.

Lemma 3.2.2
.

Let
0\leq k\leq m
.

(i)

if
0\leq i\leq r^{\prime}-k-1
then

P_{k,i}(T,x)=P_{k,i}(T,0)\quad\mbox{ and }\quad Q_{k,i}(T,x)=Q_{k,i}(T,0)+xP_{k,i}(T,0);

(ii)

if
r^{\prime}-k\leq i\leq r^{\prime}-1
then

P_{k,i}(T,x)=P_{k,i}(T,0)-xP_{k,i-r^{\prime}+k}(T,0)

and

Q_{k,i}(T,x)=Q_{k,i}(T,0)+xP_{k,i}(T,0)-x\big(Q_{k,i-r^{\prime}+k}(T,0)+xP_{k,i-r^{\prime}+k}(T,0)\big).

Proof.

As
0\leq k\leq m
, we may write

A_{k,x}=A_{k,0}+x\begin{bmatrix}I_{k}&0_{k,r^{\prime}-2k}\end{bmatrix}

and so by (
3.1.9
)

\begin{bmatrix}I_{r^{\prime}-k}&0_{r^{\prime}-k,k}\\
A_{k,x}&B_{k,x}\end{bmatrix}^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}I_{r^{\prime}-k}&0_{r^{\prime}-k,k}\\
-B_{k,0}^{-1}A_{k,0}&B_{k,0}^{-1}\end{bmatrix}-x\begin{bmatrix}0_{r^{\prime}-k,k}&0_{r^{\prime}-k,r^{\prime}-k}\\
B_{k,0}^{-1}&0_{k,r^{\prime}-k}\end{bmatrix}.

Hence, from the definition of
P_{k,i}(T,x)
we have

\displaystyle P_{k,i}(T,x)

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}T^{r^{\prime}-1}&\cdots&1\end{bmatrix}\left(\begin{bmatrix}I_{r^{\prime}-k}&0_{r^{\prime}-k,k}\\
-B_{k,0}^{-1}A_{k,0}&B_{k,0}^{-1}\end{bmatrix}-x\begin{bmatrix}0_{r^{\prime}-k,k}&0_{r^{\prime}-k,r^{\prime}-k}\\
B_{k,0}^{-1}&0_{k,r^{\prime}-k}\end{bmatrix}\right)\mathbf{e}_{r^{\prime}-i}

\displaystyle=P_{k,i}(T,0)-x\begin{bmatrix}T^{r^{\prime}-1}&\cdots&1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}0_{r^{\prime}-k,k}&0_{r^{\prime}-k,r^{\prime}-k}\\
B_{k,0}^{-1}&0_{k,r^{\prime}-k}\end{bmatrix}\mathbf{e}_{r^{\prime}-i}

If
0\leq i\leq r^{\prime}-k-1
, then the
(r^{\prime}-i)
-th column of the matrix in the last term is
0
, and so we have
P_{k,i}(T,x)=P_{k,i}(T,0)
. If
r^{\prime}-k\leq i\leq r^{\prime}
, then we observe that

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}T^{r^{\prime}-1}&\cdots&1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}0_{r^{\prime}-k,k}&0_{r^{\prime}-k,r^{\prime}-k}\\
B_{k,x}^{-1}&0_{k,r^{\prime}-k}\end{bmatrix}\mathbf{e}_{r^{\prime}-i}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}T^{k-1}&\cdots&1\end{bmatrix}B_{k,x}^{-1}\mathbf{e}^{\prime}_{k-(i-r^{\prime}+k)}

\displaystyle=P_{k,i-r^{\prime}+k}(T,0),

providing
P_{k,i}(T,x)=P_{k,i}(T,0)-xP_{k,i-r^{\prime}+k}(T,0)
.

We now compute for
Q_{k,i}(T,x)
. Recall from Lemma
3.1.12
and Lemma
3.1.14
that

Q_{k,i}(T,x)=\big[(x+Tf_{r^{\prime}}(T))P_{k,i}(T,x)\big]_{T}^{(r^{\prime}-1)}=\big[Tf_{r^{\prime}}(T)P_{k,i}(T,x)\big]_{T}^{(r^{\prime}-1)}+xP_{k,i}(T,x).

If
0\leq i\leq r^{\prime}-k-1
, then we have

\displaystyle Q_{k,i}(T,x)

\displaystyle=\big[Tf_{r^{\prime}}(T)P_{k,i}(T,0)\big]_{T}^{(r^{\prime}-1)}+xP_{k,i}(T,0)

\displaystyle=Q_{k,i}(T,0)+xP_{k,i}(T,0).

If
r^{\prime}-k\leq i\leq r^{\prime}-1
, then we have

\displaystyle Q_{k,i}(T,x)

\displaystyle=\big[Tf_{r^{\prime}}(T)\big(P_{k,i}(T,0)-xP_{k,i-r^{\prime}+k}(T,0)\big)\big]_{T}^{(r^{\prime}-1)}

\displaystyle\hskip 70.0001pt+x\big(P_{k,i}(T,0)-xP_{k,i-r^{\prime}+k}(T,0)\big)

\displaystyle=\big[Tf_{r^{\prime}}(T)P_{k,i}(T,0)\big]_{T}^{(r^{\prime}-1)}-x\big[Tf_{r^{\prime}}(T)P_{k,i-r^{\prime}+k}(T,0)\big]_{T}^{(r^{\prime}-1)}

\displaystyle\hskip 80.00012pt+xP_{k,i}(T,0)-x^{2}P_{k,i-r^{\prime}+k}(T,0)

\displaystyle=Q_{k,i}(T,0)-xQ_{k,i-r^{\prime}+k}(T,0)+xP_{k,i}(T,0)-x^{2}P_{k,i-r^{\prime}+k}(T,0).

This completes the proof.
∎

3.2.2.
When
m+1\leq k\leq r^{\prime}

In this section of paragraph, we treat the case
m+1\leq k\leq r^{\prime}
. In this case, the matrix
B_{k,x}
is of the form (
3.1.5
), so that it is easy to see that

\mathrm{adj}(B_{k,x})=\det(B_{k,x})B_{k,x}^{-1}\in\mathrm{M}_{k\times k}(\mathbf{Z}_{(p)}[x]).

Moreover, we see that

\begin{bmatrix}I_{r^{\prime}-k}&0_{r^{\prime}-k,k}\\
A_{k,x}&B_{k,x}\end{bmatrix}^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}I_{r^{\prime}-k}&0_{r^{\prime}-k,k}\\
-B_{k,x}^{-1}A_{k,x}&B_{k,x}^{-1}\end{bmatrix}\in\mathrm{M}_{k\times k}(\mathbf{Q}_{p}(x)).

To normalize
P_{k,i}(T,x)
and
Q_{k,i}(T,x)
, we set

d_{k}(x):=(-1)^{r^{\prime}(k-1)}\frac{\det(B_{k,x})}{\det(B_{r^{\prime}-k,0})}.

Here we set
\det(B_{0,0})=1
for
k=r^{\prime}
. Note that
B_{r^{\prime}-k,x}=B_{r^{\prime}-k,0}
and
\det(B_{r^{\prime}-k,0})
is a constant in
\mathbf{Z}_{(p)}^{\times}
. We also note that
d_{k}(x)
is a monic polynomial in
x
of degree
2k-r^{\prime}
(see Lemma
3.2.5
).
We now define

\begin{cases}\tilde{P}_{k,i}(T,x):=d_{k}(x)P_{k,i}(T,x);\\
\tilde{Q}_{k,i}(T,x):=d_{k}(x)Q_{k,i}(T,x),\end{cases}

both of which belong to
\mathbf{Z}_{(p)}[x,T]
.

We also set

s_{k,i}:=\deg_{x}\tilde{P}_{k,i}(T,x)\quad\mbox{ and }\quad t_{k,i}:=\deg_{x}\tilde{Q}_{k,i}(T,x),

and write

\tilde{P}_{k,i}(T,x)=\sum_{s=0}^{s_{k,i}}x^{s}P_{k,i,s}(T)\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\tilde{Q}_{k,i}(T,x)=\sum_{s=0}^{t_{k,i}}x^{s}Q_{k,i,s}(T),

with
P_{k,i,s}(T),\,Q_{k,i,s}(T)\in\mathbf{Z}_{(p)}[T].
It will be useful to use
\tilde{P}_{k,i}(T,x)
and
\tilde{Q}_{k,i}(T,x)
to describe the valuations of the coefficients in practice.

Lemma 3.2.3
.

Let
m+1\leq k\leq r^{\prime}
. Then

t_{k,i}-1=s_{k,i}=\begin{cases}2k-r^{\prime}-1&\mbox{if }0\leq i\leq 2k-r^{\prime}-1;\\
2k-r^{\prime}&\mbox{if }2k-r^{\prime}\leq i\leq k-1;\\
2k-r^{\prime}+1&\mbox{if }k\leq i\leq r^{\prime}-1,\end{cases}

and

Q_{k,i,t_{k,i}}(T)=P_{k,i,s_{k,i}}(T)=\begin{cases}P_{r^{\prime}-k,i+r^{\prime}-k}(T,0)&\mbox{if }0\leq i\leq 2k-r^{\prime}-1;\\
P_{r^{\prime}-k,i+r^{\prime}-2k}(T,0)&\mbox{if }2k-r^{\prime}\leq i\leq k-1;\\
-P_{r^{\prime}-k,i-k}(T,0)&\mbox{if }k\leq i\leq r^{\prime}-1.\end{cases}

We need a little preparation to prove this Lemma. It is easy to see that the matrix
B_{k,x}
is a low-rank update of
B_{k,0}
if
m+1\leq k<r^{\prime}
. More precisely, if we set
t=2k-r^{\prime}
and define

U_{t}=\begin{bmatrix}0_{k-t,t}\\
I_{t}\end{bmatrix}\in\mathrm{M}_{k\times t}(\mathbf{Q})\quad\mbox{ and }\quad V_{t}=\begin{bmatrix}I_{t}&0_{t,k-t}\end{bmatrix}\in\mathrm{M}_{t\times k}(\mathbf{Q}),

then we can write

B_{k,x}=B_{k,0}+xU_{t}V_{t},

and we have

(3.2.4)

B_{k,x}^{-1}=B_{k,0}^{-1}-xB_{k,0}^{-1}U_{t}(I_{t}+xV_{t}B_{k,0}^{-1}U_{t})^{-1}V_{t}B_{k,0}^{-1},

which is the Sherman-Morrison-Woodbury Formula.

We write the matrix
B_{k,0}^{-1}
in block matrices as

B_{k,0}^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}A_{k}&A^{\prime}_{k}\\
A^{\prime\prime}_{k}&A^{\prime\prime\prime}_{k}\end{bmatrix},

where
A_{k}\in\mathrm{M}_{t\times(k-t)}(\mathbf{Q})
,
A^{\prime}_{k}\in\mathrm{M}_{t\times t}(\mathbf{Q})
,
A^{\prime\prime}_{k}\in\mathrm{M}_{(k-t)\times(k-t)}(\mathbf{Q})
, and
A^{\prime\prime\prime}_{k}\in\mathrm{M}_{(k-t)\times t}(\mathbf{Q})
.

Lemma 3.2.5
.

Let
m+1\leq k<r^{\prime}
. Then

(i)

A^{\prime}_{k}
is invertible and
(A^{\prime}_{k})^{-1}\in\mathrm{M}_{t\times t}(\mathbf{Z}_{(p)})
;

(ii)

B_{r^{\prime}-k,0}^{-1}=A^{\prime\prime}_{k}-A^{\prime\prime\prime}_{k}(A^{\prime}_{k})^{-1}A_{k}
;

(iii)

d_{k}(x)=\det(xI_{t}+(A^{\prime}_{k})^{-1})\in\mathbf{Z}_{(p)}[x]
, which is monic of degree
t
.

Proof.

Write

\displaystyle B_{k,0}=\begin{bmatrix}B&B^{\prime}\\
B^{\prime\prime}&B^{\prime\prime\prime}\end{bmatrix}

where
B\in\mathrm{M}_{(k-t)\times t}(\mathbf{Q})
,
B^{\prime}\in\mathrm{M}_{(k-t)\times(k-t)}(\mathbf{Q})
,
B^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{M}_{t\times t}(\mathbf{Q})
, and
B^{\prime\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{M}_{t\times(k-t)}(\mathbf{Q})
. Note that
B^{\prime}=B_{k-t,0}=B_{r^{\prime}-k,0}
as
k-t=r^{\prime}-k
.

Computing
B_{k,0}B_{k,0}^{-1}=I_{k}
blockwisely, we get 4 equations:

\begin{bmatrix}BA_{k}+B^{\prime}A^{\prime\prime}_{k}&BA^{\prime}_{k}+B^{\prime}A^{\prime\prime\prime}_{k}\\
B^{\prime\prime}A_{k}+B^{\prime\prime\prime}A^{\prime\prime}_{k}&B^{\prime\prime}A^{\prime}_{k}+B^{\prime\prime\prime}A^{\prime\prime\prime}_{k}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}I_{k-t}&0_{k-t,t}\\
0_{t,k-t}&I_{t}\end{bmatrix}.

As
B^{\prime}=B_{r^{\prime}-k,0}
is invertible, we can solve the equation in the (1,2)-entry for
A^{\prime\prime\prime}_{k}
to get

A^{\prime\prime\prime}_{k}=-(B^{\prime})^{-1}BA^{\prime}_{k}.

Plugging it into the equation in the (2,2)-entry, we get

I_{t}=B^{\prime\prime}A^{\prime}_{k}-B^{\prime\prime\prime}(B^{\prime})^{-1}BA^{\prime}_{k}=(B^{\prime\prime}-B^{\prime\prime\prime}(B^{\prime})^{-1}B)A^{\prime}_{k}.

Therefore,
A^{\prime}_{k}
is invertible, and

(A^{\prime}_{k})^{-1}=B^{\prime\prime}-B^{\prime\prime\prime}(B^{\prime})^{-1}B\in\mathrm{M}_{t\times t}(\mathbf{Z}_{(p)}),

which proves (i).

For (ii), we solve the equation in the (1,2)-entry for
B
to get

B=-B^{\prime}A^{\prime\prime\prime}_{k}(A^{\prime}_{k})^{-1}.

Plugging it into the equation in the (1,1)-entry, we get

I_{k-t}=-B^{\prime}A^{\prime\prime\prime}_{k}(A^{\prime}_{k})^{-1}A_{k}+B^{\prime}A^{\prime\prime}_{k}=B^{\prime}(A^{\prime\prime}_{k}-A^{\prime\prime\prime}_{k}(A^{\prime}_{k})^{-1}A_{k}),

which proves (ii).

For (iii), we observe that

\displaystyle B_{k,x}=\begin{bmatrix}B&B^{\prime}\\
xI_{t}+B^{\prime\prime}&B^{\prime\prime\prime}\end{bmatrix}.

From the identity

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}(B^{\prime})^{-1}&0_{k-t,t}\\
-B^{\prime\prime\prime}(B^{\prime})^{-1}&I_{t}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}B&B^{\prime}\\
xI_{t}+B^{\prime\prime}&B^{\prime\prime\prime}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}(B^{\prime})^{-1}B&I_{k-t}\\
xI_{t}+B^{\prime\prime}-B^{\prime\prime\prime}(B^{\prime})^{-1}B&0_{t,k-t}\end{bmatrix}

we get

\det(B^{\prime})^{-1}\det B_{k,x}=(-1)^{r^{\prime}(k-1)}\det(xI_{t}+B^{\prime\prime}-B^{\prime\prime\prime}(B^{\prime})^{-1}B)

where the sign of right-hand side comes from consecutive cofactor expansions at the rightmost rows. Also, from
B^{\prime}=B_{r^{\prime}-k,0}
and
B^{\prime\prime}-B^{\prime\prime\prime}(B^{\prime})^{-1}B=(A^{\prime}_{k})^{-1}
, one has

\displaystyle d_{k}(x)=(-1)^{r^{\prime}(k-1)}\frac{\det(B_{k,x})}{\det(B_{r^{\prime}-k,0})}=\det(xI_{t}+(A^{\prime}_{k})^{-1}).

This is a monic polynomial of
x
with degree
t
defined over
\mathbf{Z}_{(p)}
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
3.2.3
.

Let
t=2k-r^{\prime}
, and we first consider the case
k=r^{\prime}
. But this case immediately follows from Lemma
3.1.12
.

From now on, we assume that
m+1\leq k\leq r^{\prime}-1
. We have the following identities:

B_{k,0}^{-1}U_{t}=\begin{bmatrix}A^{\prime}_{k}\\
A^{\prime\prime\prime}_{k}\end{bmatrix},\qquad V_{t}B_{k,0}^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}A_{k}&A^{\prime}_{k}\end{bmatrix},\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad V_{t}B_{k,0}^{-1}U_{t}=A^{\prime}_{k}.

We consider
y=x^{-1}
to compute the terms with highest power of
x
. As
A^{\prime}_{k}
is invertible by Lemma
3.2.5
(i), we observe that

\displaystyle(I_{t}+xV_{t}B_{k,0}^{-1}U_{t})^{-1}=(I_{t}+xA^{\prime}_{k})^{-1}=y(A^{\prime}_{k})^{-1}(y(A^{\prime}_{k})^{-1}+I_{t})^{-1}.

On
\mathbf{Q}[[y]]
, we have

\displaystyle(y(A^{\prime}_{k})^{-1}+I_{t})^{-1}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(y(A^{\prime}_{k})^{-1})^{n}\in\mathrm{M}_{t\times t}\big(\mathbf{Q}[[y]]\big),

which gives

\displaystyle(I_{t}+xA^{\prime}_{k})^{-1}\equiv y(A^{\prime}_{k})^{-1}+y^{2}((A^{\prime}_{k})^{-1})^{2}\pmod{y^{3}}.

Combining this to (
3.2.4
) gives

\displaystyle B_{k,x}^{-1}

\displaystyle=B_{k,0}^{-1}-x\begin{bmatrix}A^{\prime}_{k}\\
A^{\prime\prime\prime}_{k}\end{bmatrix}(I_{t}+xA^{\prime}_{k})^{-1}\begin{bmatrix}A_{k}&A^{\prime}_{k}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle\equiv B_{k,0}^{-1}-\begin{bmatrix}A^{\prime}_{k}\\
A^{\prime\prime\prime}_{k}\end{bmatrix}\big((A^{\prime}_{k})^{-1}+y((A^{\prime}_{k})^{-1})^{2}\big)\begin{bmatrix}A_{k}&A^{\prime}_{k}\end{bmatrix}\pmod{y^{2}}.

We set

\displaystyle\tilde{A}:=\begin{bmatrix}A^{\prime}_{k}\\
A^{\prime\prime\prime}_{k}\end{bmatrix}(A^{\prime}_{k})^{-1}\begin{bmatrix}A_{k}&A^{\prime}_{k}\end{bmatrix}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\tilde{A}^{\prime}:=\begin{bmatrix}A^{\prime}_{k}\\
A^{\prime\prime\prime}_{k}\end{bmatrix}((A^{\prime}_{k})^{-1})^{2}\begin{bmatrix}A_{k}&A^{\prime}_{k}\end{bmatrix}

so that we have
B_{k,x}^{-1}\equiv B_{k,0}^{-1}-\tilde{A}-y\tilde{A}^{\prime}\pmod{y^{2}}
. Then we have

\displaystyle\tilde{A}=\begin{bmatrix}A_{k}&A^{\prime}_{k}\\
A^{\prime\prime\prime}_{k}(A^{\prime}_{k})^{-1}A_{k}&A^{\prime\prime\prime}_{k}\end{bmatrix}.

By Lemma
3.2.5
(ii),

(3.2.6)

B_{k,0}^{-1}-\tilde{A}=\begin{bmatrix}0_{t,k-t}&0_{t,t}\\
A^{\prime\prime}_{k}-A^{\prime\prime\prime}_{k}(A^{\prime}_{k})^{-1}A_{k}&0_{k-t,t}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}0_{t,k-t}&0_{t,t}\\
B_{k-t,0}^{-1}&0_{k-t,t}\end{bmatrix}.

For
\tilde{A}^{\prime}
, we observe that

(3.2.7)

\tilde{A}^{\prime}=B_{k,0}^{-1}U_{t}((A^{\prime}_{k})^{-1})^{2}V_{t}B_{k,0}^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}(A^{\prime}_{k})^{-1}A_{k}&I_{t}\\
A^{\prime\prime\prime}_{k}((A^{\prime}_{k})^{-1})^{2}A_{k}&A^{\prime\prime\prime}_{k}(A^{\prime}_{k})^{-1}\end{bmatrix}.

Then it is easy to see that

\displaystyle M_{r^{\prime},0}\begin{bmatrix}0_{r^{\prime}-k,t}\\
\tilde{A}^{\prime}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}A_{k,0}&B_{k,0}\\
C_{k,0}&D_{k,0}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}0_{r^{\prime}-k,t}\\
\tilde{A}^{\prime}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}B_{k,0}\tilde{A}^{\prime}\\
D_{k,0}\tilde{A}^{\prime}\end{bmatrix}.

On the upper block, from the first equality of (
3.2.7
) we observe

\displaystyle B_{k,0}\tilde{A}^{\prime}=\begin{bmatrix}0_{k-t,t}\\
I_{t}\end{bmatrix}((A^{\prime}_{k})^{-1})^{2}V_{t}B_{k,0}^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}0_{k-t,k}\\
((A^{\prime}_{k})^{-1})^{2}V_{t}B_{k,0}^{-1}\end{bmatrix}.

Now we compute
P_{k,i,s_{k,i}}(T)
for
0\leq i\leq k-1
. Recall that
\mathbf{e}^{\prime}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{e}^{\prime}_{k}
be the standard basis of
\mathbf{Q}^{k}
, and that we have

\displaystyle P_{k,i}(T,x)=\begin{bmatrix}T^{k-1}&\cdots&1\end{bmatrix}B_{k,x}^{-1}\mathbf{e}^{\prime}_{k-i}.

From Lemma
3.2.5
(iii), we have

\displaystyle d_{k}(x)=\det(y^{-1}I_{t}+(A^{\prime}_{k})^{-1})\equiv y^{-t}+y^{-t+1}\mathrm{Tr}((A^{\prime}_{k})^{-1})\pmod{y^{-t+2}},

and so

\displaystyle\tilde{P}_{k,i}(T,x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=0}^{s_{k,i}}y^{-s}P_{k,i,s}(T)=d_{k}(x)P_{k,i}(T,x)

\displaystyle\equiv\big(y^{-t}+y^{-t+1}\mathrm{Tr}((A^{\prime}_{k})^{-1})\big)\begin{bmatrix}T^{k-1}&\cdots&1\end{bmatrix}(B_{k,0}^{-1}-\tilde{A}-y\tilde{A}^{\prime})\mathbf{e}^{\prime}_{k-i}\pmod{y^{-t+2}}

Hence, we observe that
s_{k,i}\leq t=2k-r^{\prime}
.

We let

\displaystyle g_{k,i}(T)

\displaystyle:=\begin{bmatrix}T^{k-1}&\cdots&1\end{bmatrix}(B_{k,0}^{-1}-\tilde{A})\mathbf{e}^{\prime}_{k-i};

\displaystyle h_{k,i}(T)

\displaystyle:=\begin{bmatrix}T^{k-1}&\cdots&1\end{bmatrix}\tilde{A}^{\prime}\mathbf{e}^{\prime}_{k-i},

so that we have

(3.2.8)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tilde{P}_{k,i}(T,x)&\equiv y^{-t}\big(1+y\mathrm{Tr}((A^{\prime}_{k})^{-1})\big)\big(g_{k,i}(T)-yh_{k,i}(T)\big)\\
&\equiv y^{-t}g_{k,i}(T)+y^{-t+1}\big(\mathrm{Tr}((A^{\prime}_{k})^{-1})g_{k,i}(T)-h_{k,i}(T)\big)\pmod{y^{-t+2}}.\end{split}

Consider first the case
0\leq i\leq t-1
. We see that
g_{k,i}(T)=0
as the
(k-i)
-th column of
B_{k,0}^{-1}-\tilde{A}
is zero (see (
3.2.6
)). As
(k-i)
-th column of
\tilde{A}^{\prime}
is not zero,
h_{k,i}(T)\neq 0
. So, we get
s_{k,i}=t-1
(from (
3.2.8
)) and

\displaystyle P_{k,i,t-1}(T)=h_{k,i}(T).

We further characterize
h_{k,i}(T)
. Observe that, from the definition of
h_{k,i}(T)
together with (
3.2.7
), we can easily see that

\displaystyle\deg_{T}\big(h_{k,i}(T)-T^{i+k-t}\big)\leq k-t-1.

Also, we see that

\displaystyle[Tf_{r^{\prime}}(T)h_{k,i}(T)]_{T}^{(r^{\prime}-1)}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}T^{k-1}&\cdots&1\end{bmatrix}M_{r^{\prime},0}\begin{bmatrix}0_{r^{\prime}-k,t}\\
\tilde{A}^{\prime}\end{bmatrix}\mathbf{e}^{\prime}_{k-i}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}T^{k-1}&\cdots&1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}B_{k,0}\tilde{A}^{\prime}\\
D_{k,0}\tilde{A}^{\prime}\end{bmatrix}\mathbf{e}^{\prime}_{k-i}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}T^{k-1}&\cdots&1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}0_{k-t,k}\\
((A^{\prime}_{k})^{-1})^{2}V_{t}B_{k,0}^{-1}\\
D_{k,0}\tilde{A}^{\prime}\end{bmatrix}\mathbf{e}^{\prime}_{k-i}

and so

\displaystyle\deg_{T}[Tf_{r^{\prime}}(T)h_{k,j}(T)]_{T}^{(r^{\prime}-1)}\leq(r^{\prime}-1)-(k-t).

Noting that
k-t=r^{\prime}-k
, we observe that

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\deg_{T}(h_{k,i}(T)-T^{i+r^{\prime}-k})\leq r^{\prime}-k-1,\\
\deg_{T}[Tf_{r^{\prime}}(T)h_{k,i}(T)]_{T}^{(r^{\prime}-1)}\leq k-1.\end{cases}

According to Lemma
3.1.14
,
P_{r^{\prime}-k,i+r^{\prime}-k}(T,0)
is the unique polynomial satisfying such properties.
Therefore, we have

\displaystyle P_{k,i,t-1}(T)=h_{k,i}(T)=P_{r^{\prime}-k,i+r^{\prime}-k}(T,0).

We now consider the case
t\leq i\leq k-1
. From the definition of
g_{k,i}(T)
together with (
3.2.6
), we see that
g_{k,i}(T)\neq 0
and
s_{k,i}=t
. Moreover, from (
3.2.8
) we have

\displaystyle P_{k,i,t}(T)=g_{k,i}(T).

Setting
\mathbf{e}^{\prime\prime}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{e}^{\prime\prime}_{k-t}
be the standard basis of
\mathbf{Q}^{k-t}
, we have

\displaystyle g_{k,i}(T)=\begin{bmatrix}T^{k-t-1}&\cdots&1\end{bmatrix}B_{k-t,0}^{-1}\mathbf{e}^{\prime\prime}_{k-i}=P_{k-t,i-t}(T,0).

Noting that
k-t=r^{\prime}-k
, we get

\displaystyle P_{k,i,t}(T)=P_{r^{\prime}-k,i+r^{\prime}-2k}(T,0).

We finally consider the case
k\leq i\leq r^{\prime}-1
. For
k\leq i\leq r^{\prime}-1
, we have

\displaystyle P_{k,i}(T,x)

\displaystyle=T^{i}-\begin{bmatrix}T^{k-1}&\cdots&1\end{bmatrix}B_{k,x}^{-1}A_{k,x}\mathbf{e}^{\prime\prime}_{r^{\prime}-i}.

Set
y=x^{-1}
as before, then

\displaystyle A_{k,x}\mathbf{e}^{\prime\prime}_{r^{\prime}-i}

\displaystyle=x\begin{bmatrix}I_{k-t}\\
0_{t,k-t}\end{bmatrix}\mathbf{e}^{\prime\prime}_{r^{\prime}-i}+A_{k,0}\mathbf{e}^{\prime\prime}_{r^{\prime}-i}=y^{-1}\mathbf{e}^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}-i}+A_{k,0}\mathbf{e}^{\prime\prime}_{r^{\prime}-i}

\displaystyle\equiv y^{-1}\mathbf{e}^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}-i}\pmod{y^{0}}.

Using
B_{k,x}^{-1}\in\mathrm{M}_{k\times k}(\mathbf{Q}[[y]])
, one get
P_{k,i-r^{\prime}+k}(T,x)\in(\mathbf{Q}[[y]])[T]
, and

\displaystyle P_{k,i}(T,x)

\displaystyle\equiv-\begin{bmatrix}T^{k-1}&\cdots&1\end{bmatrix}B_{k,x}^{-1}(y^{-1}\mathbf{e}^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}-i})

\displaystyle\equiv-y^{-1}\begin{bmatrix}T^{k-1}&\cdots&1\end{bmatrix}B_{k,x}^{-1}\mathbf{e}^{\prime}_{k-(i-r^{\prime}+k)}

\displaystyle\equiv-y^{-1}P_{k,i-r^{\prime}+k}(T,x)\pmod{y^{0}}.

So this gives

\displaystyle\frac{\tilde{P}_{k,i}(T,x)}{d_{k}(x)}\equiv-y^{-1}\frac{\tilde{P}_{k,i-r^{\prime}+k}(T,x)}{d_{k}(x)}\pmod{y^{0}}.

As
d_{k}(x)
is monic of degree
t
, one has

\displaystyle d_{k}(x)\equiv y^{-t}\pmod{y^{-t+1}}.

Also, as
t\leq i-r^{\prime}+k\leq k-1
, one has

\displaystyle\tilde{P}_{k,i-r^{\prime}+k}(T,x)\equiv y^{-t}P_{r^{\prime}-k,i-k}(T,0)\pmod{y^{-t+1}}.

Combining these gives

\displaystyle\tilde{P}_{k,i}(T,x)

\displaystyle\equiv-y^{-1}\tilde{P}_{k,i-r^{\prime}+k}(T,x)

\displaystyle\equiv-y^{-t-1}P_{r^{\prime}-k,i-k}(T,0)\pmod{y^{-t}}.

Therefore, we get
s_{k,i}=t+1
and

\displaystyle P_{k,i,s_{k,i}}(T)=-P_{r^{\prime}-k,i-k}(T,0)

for
k\leq i\leq r^{\prime}-1
.

Lastly, from
\tilde{Q}_{k,i}(T,x)=\big[\tilde{P}_{k,i}(T,x)(x+Tf_{r^{\prime}}(T))\big]_{T}^{(r^{\prime}-1)}
we have

\tilde{Q}_{k,i}(T,x)=x^{s_{k,i}+1}P_{k,i,s_{k,i}}(T)\\
+\sum_{s=1}^{s_{k,i}}x^{s}\big[P_{k,i,s}(T)Tf_{r^{\prime}}(T)+P_{k,i,s-1}(T)\big]_{T}^{(r^{\prime}-1)}+\big[P_{k,i,0}(T)Tf_{r^{\prime}}(T)\big]_{T}^{(r^{\prime}-1)},

and so we see that
t_{k,i}=s_{k,i}+1
and
Q_{k,i,t_{k,i}}(T)=P_{k,i,s_{k,i}}(T)
.
∎

We close this subsection by proving the following lemma, which will be needed to compute the mod-
p
reduction of our strongly divisible modules.

Lemma 3.2.9
.

Let
m+1\leq k\leq r^{\prime}
. Then

(i)

if
k\leq i\leq r^{\prime}-1
then
P_{k,i,2k-r^{\prime}}(T)
is monic of degree
i
;

(ii)

if
2k-r^{\prime}\leq i\leq k-1
then
Q_{k,i,2k-r^{\prime}}(T)
is monic of degree
i+r^{\prime}-k
;

(iii)

if
m+2\leq k\leq r^{\prime}
then

P_{k,0,2k-r^{\prime}-2}(T)=-\frac{1}{a_{r^{\prime}-k+1}}P_{k-1,k-1,2k-r^{\prime}-2}(T)

whose degree is
k-1
and whose leading coefficient is
-1/a_{r^{\prime}-k+1}\in\mathbf{Z}_{(p)}^{\times}
;

(iv)

if
k=m+1
and
r^{\prime}=2m
then

P_{m+1,0,0}(T)=-\frac{1}{a_{m}}P_{m,m}(T,0)

whose degree is
m
and whose leading coefficient is
-1/a_{m}\in\mathbf{Z}_{(p)}^{\times}
;

(v)

if
k=m+1
and
r^{\prime}=2m+1>1
then

\frac{1}{a_{m+1}}=d_{m+1}(0)=2H_{m}\in\mathbf{Z}_{(p)}.

Note that we exclude
r^{\prime}=1
in (v) because
a_{1}(x)=x^{-1}
and so
a_{1}(0)
cannot be defined.

Proof.

(i) For
k\leq i\leq r^{\prime}-1
, consider

\displaystyle d_{k}(x)P_{k,i}(T,x)=\sum_{s=0}^{2k-r^{\prime}+1}x^{s}P_{k,i,s}(T).

As
P_{k,i}(T,x)
is monic of degree
i
in
T
by Lemma
3.1.14
and
d_{k}(x)
is monic of degree
2k-r^{\prime}
in
x
, the coefficient of
x^{2k-r^{\prime}}T^{i}
on the left-hand side is
1
. On the right-hand side, the term
x^{2k-r^{\prime}}T^{i}
comes only from
x^{2k-r^{\prime}}P_{k,i,2k-r^{\prime}}(T)
. Therefore,
P_{k,i,2k-r^{\prime}}(T)
should be monic of degree
i
.

(ii) For
2k-r^{\prime}-1\leq i\leq k-1
, we have
t_{k,i}-1=2k-r^{\prime}
. Consider

\displaystyle d_{k}(x)Q_{k,i}(T,x)=\sum_{s=0}^{t_{k,i}}x^{s}Q_{k,i,s}(T).

As
Q_{k,i}(T,x)
is monic of degree
i+r^{\prime}-k
in
T
by Lemma
3.1.14
and
d_{k}(x)
is monic of degree
2k-r^{\prime}
in
x
, the coefficient of
x^{2k-r^{\prime}}T^{i+r^{\prime}-k}
on the left-hand side is
1
. On the right-hand side, the term
x^{2k-r^{\prime}}T^{i+r^{\prime}-k}
comes only from
x^{2k-r^{\prime}}Q_{k,i,2k-r^{\prime}}(T)
. Therefore,
Q_{k,i,2k-r^{\prime}}(T)
should be monic of degree
i+r^{\prime}-k
.

(iii) We compute
a_{k}(x)
in Lemma
3.1.16
(iii). From

P_{k,0}(T,x)=\begin{bmatrix}T^{k-1}&\cdots&1\end{bmatrix}B_{k,x}^{-1}\mathbf{e}^{\prime}_{k}

one gets

\displaystyle a_{k}(x)=(\mathbf{e}^{\prime}_{1})^{T}B_{k,x}^{-1}\mathbf{e}^{\prime}_{k}=\frac{1}{\det(B_{k,x})}(\mathbf{e}^{\prime}_{1})^{T}\mathrm{adj}(B_{k,x})\mathbf{e}^{\prime}_{k}=(-1)^{1+k}\frac{\det(B_{k-1,x})}{\det(B_{k,x})}

because the
(k,1)
-minor of
B_{k,x}
is
\det(B_{k-1,x})
. Hence, by Lemma
3.1.16
(iii) we have

\displaystyle\det(B_{k,x})P_{k,0}(T,x)=(-1)^{k+1}\det(B_{k-1,x})P_{k-1,k-1}(T,x).

Equivalently,

\displaystyle\det(B_{r^{\prime}-k,0})\tilde{P}_{k,0}(T,x)=(-1)^{r^{\prime}-k+1}\det(B_{r^{\prime}-k+1,0})\tilde{P}_{k-1,k-1}(T,x).

Noting that
s_{k-1,k-1}=2(k-1)-r^{\prime}+1=2k-r^{\prime}-1
and
s_{k,0}=2k-r^{\prime}-1
, we can write

\displaystyle\sum_{s=0}^{2k-r^{\prime}-1}x^{s}P_{k,0,s}(T)=\sum_{s=0}^{2k-r^{\prime}-1}x^{s}\frac{(-1)^{r^{\prime}-k+1}\det(B_{r^{\prime}-k+1,0})}{\det(B_{r^{\prime}-k,0})}P_{k-1,k-1,s}(T),

and so we have

(3.2.10)

\displaystyle P_{k,0,s}(T)=\frac{(-1)^{r^{\prime}-k+1}\det(B_{r^{\prime}-k+1,0})}{\det(B_{r^{\prime}-k,0})}P_{k-1,k-1,s}(T).

If
s=2k-r^{\prime}-1
, by Lemma
3.2.3
and Lemma
3.1.16
(iii) one has

\displaystyle P_{k,0,2k-r^{\prime}-1}(T)

\displaystyle=P_{r^{\prime}-k,r^{\prime}-k}(T,0)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{a_{r^{\prime}-k+1}}P_{r^{\prime}-k+1,0}(T,0)=-\frac{1}{a_{r^{\prime}-k+1}}P_{k-1,k-1,2k-r^{\prime}-1}(T).

Therefore, we have

(3.2.11)

\displaystyle\frac{(-1)^{r^{\prime}-k+1}\det(B_{r^{\prime}-k+1,0})}{\det(B_{r^{\prime}-k,0})}=-\frac{1}{a_{r^{\prime}-k+1}}

and

\displaystyle P_{k-1,k-1,2k-r^{\prime}-2}(T)=-\frac{1}{a_{r^{\prime}-k+1}}P_{k,0,2k-r^{\prime}-2}(T).

(iv) Now we let
r^{\prime}=2m
and
k=m+1
. As before, we get

\displaystyle\det(B_{m+1,x})P_{m+1,0}(T,x)=(-1)^{m+2}\det(B_{m,x})P_{m,m}(T,x).

Applying Lemma
3.2.2
and using
\det(B_{m,x})=\det(B_{m,0})
, one has

\displaystyle(-1)^{r^{\prime}(m-1)}\det(B_{m-1,0})\tilde{P}_{m+1,0}(T,x)=(-1)^{m+2}\det(B_{m,0})\big(P_{m,m}(T,0)-xP_{m,0}(T,0)\big)

and so

\displaystyle P_{m+1,0,0}(T)+xP_{m+1,0,1}(T)=\frac{(-1)^{m}\det(B_{m,0})}{\det(B_{m-1,0})}\big(P_{m,m}(T,0)-xP_{m,0}(T,0)\big)

As

\displaystyle P_{m+1,0,1}(T)=P_{m-1,m-1}(T,0)=\frac{1}{a_{m}}P_{m,0}(T,0),

we can rewrite the equation as

\displaystyle P_{m+1,0,0}(T)+xP_{m+1,0,1}(T)=-\frac{1}{a_{m}}\big(P_{m,m}(T,0)-xP_{m,0}(T,0)\big),

proving the assertion.

(v) Let
r^{\prime}=2m+1>1
and
k=m+1
. By Lemma
3.1.16
(iii) and by Lemma
3.2.3
we have

P_{m+1,0,0}(T)=P_{m.m}(T,0)=\frac{1}{a_{m+1}}P_{m+1,0}(T,0).

On the other hand, by definition we have

P_{m+1,0,0}(T)=d_{m+1}(0)P_{m+1,0}(T,0).

By Lemma
3.1.14

P_{m,m}(T,0)
is a monic polynomial of degree
m
, and so
a_{m+1}d_{m+1}(0)=1
in
\mathbf{Q}
.

We now compute
d_{m+1}(0)
. We set the
(m+1)\times(m+1)
-matrix
M
as follows:

\displaystyle M=\begin{bmatrix}\frac{1}{m}&\frac{1}{m+1}&\cdots&\frac{1}{2m-1}&\frac{1}{2m}\\
\frac{1}{m-1}&\frac{1}{m}&\cdots&\frac{1}{2m-2}&\frac{1}{2m-1}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
1&\frac{1}{2}&\cdots&\frac{1}{m}&\frac{1}{m+1}\\
0&1&\cdots&\frac{1}{m-1}&\frac{1}{m}\end{bmatrix}.

We also let
M^{\prime}
(resp.
M^{\prime\prime}
) be the matrix obtained from
M
by replacing the
(m+1,1)
-entry of
M
with
2H_{m}
(resp. by deleting the first column and the last row of
M
). As we have

\displaystyle B_{m+1,0}=\mathrm{Diag}((-1)^{m-1},\cdots,(-1)^{0},(-1)^{-1})\cdot M\cdot\mathrm{Diag}((-1)^{0},\cdots,(-1)^{m})

and

\displaystyle B_{m,0}=\mathrm{Diag}((-1)^{m},\cdots,(-1)^{2},(-1)^{1})\cdot M^{\prime\prime}\cdot\mathrm{Dig}((-1)^{0},\cdots,(-1)^{m-1}),

we have

d_{m+1}(0)=(-1)^{rm}\frac{\det B_{m+1,0}}{\det B_{m,0}}=(-1)^{m+1}\frac{\det M}{\det M^{\prime\prime}}.

By definition of the determinant, it is easy to see that

(-1)^{m+1}\det M^{\prime\prime}\left(d_{m+1}(0)-2H_{m}\right)=\det M^{\prime}.

We now claim that
\det M^{\prime}=0
, and we prove it by induction on
m
. By subtracting the first row of
M^{\prime}
from each other row, we get

\displaystyle D(m):=\det M^{\prime}=\begin{vmatrix}\frac{1}{m}&\frac{1}{m+1}&\cdots&\frac{1}{2m-1}&\frac{1}{2m}\\
\frac{1}{m-1}-\frac{1}{m}&\frac{1}{m}-\frac{1}{m+1}&\cdots&\frac{1}{2m-2}-\frac{1}{2m-1}&\frac{1}{2m-1}-\frac{1}{2m}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
1-\frac{1}{m}&\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{m+1}&\cdots&\frac{1}{m}-\frac{1}{2m-1}&\frac{1}{m+1}-\frac{1}{2m}\\
2H_{m}-\frac{1}{m}&1-\frac{1}{m+1}&\cdots&\frac{1}{m-1}-\frac{1}{2m-1}&\frac{1}{m}-\frac{1}{2m}\end{vmatrix}

which can be rewritten as

\displaystyle D(m)=\begin{vmatrix}\frac{1}{m}&\frac{1}{m+1}&\cdots&\frac{1}{2m-1}&\frac{1}{2m}\\
\frac{1}{(m-1)m}&\frac{1}{m(m+1)}&\cdots&\frac{1}{(2m-2)(2m-1)}&\frac{1}{(2m-1)2m}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
\frac{m-1}{1\cdot m}&\frac{m-1}{2(m+1)}&\cdots&\frac{m-1}{m(2m-1)}&\frac{m-1}{(m+1)2m}\\
H_{m}+H_{m-1}&\frac{m}{1(m+1)}&\cdots&\frac{m}{(m-1)(2m-1)}&\frac{m}{m\cdot 2m}\end{vmatrix}.

Factoring out the common factors from each column and each row, we have

\displaystyle D(m)=\frac{m!}{m(m+1)\cdots 2m}\begin{vmatrix}1&1&\cdots&1&1\\
\frac{1}{m-1}&\frac{1}{m}&\cdots&\frac{1}{2m-2}&\frac{1}{2m-1}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
1&\frac{1}{2}&\cdots&\frac{1}{m}&\frac{1}{m+1}\\
H_{m}+H_{m-1}&1&\cdots&\frac{1}{m-1}&\frac{1}{m}\end{vmatrix}.

For the last determinant, we subtract the last column from each other column, to get

\displaystyle\frac{(2m)!}{m!(m-1)!}D(m)

\displaystyle=\begin{vmatrix}0&0&\cdots&0&1\\
\frac{1}{m-1}-\frac{1}{2m-1}&\frac{1}{m}-\frac{1}{2m-1}&\cdots&\frac{1}{2m-2}-\frac{1}{2m-1}&\frac{1}{2m-1}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
1-\frac{1}{m+1}&\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{m+1}&\cdots&\frac{1}{m}-\frac{1}{m+1}&\frac{1}{m+1}\\
H_{m}+H_{m-1}-\frac{1}{m}&1-\frac{1}{m}&\cdots&\frac{1}{m-1}-\frac{1}{m}&\frac{1}{m}\end{vmatrix}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{m}\begin{vmatrix}\frac{m}{(m-1)(2m-1)}&\frac{m-1}{m(2m-1)}&\cdots&\frac{1}{(2m-2)(2m-1)}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\frac{m}{1(m+1)}&\frac{m-1}{2(m+1)}&\cdots&\frac{1}{m(m+1)}\\
2H_{m-1}&\frac{m-1}{1\cdot m}&\cdots&\frac{1}{(m-1)m}\end{vmatrix}

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{m}m!}{m(m+1)\cdots(2m-1)}\begin{vmatrix}\frac{1}{m-1}&\frac{1}{m}&\cdots&\frac{1}{2m-2}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
1&\frac{1}{2}&\cdots&\frac{1}{m}\\
2H_{m-1}&1&\cdots&\frac{1}{m-1}\end{vmatrix}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{m}\frac{m!(m-1)!}{(2m-1)!}D(m-1).

It is clear that
D(1)=0
by direct computation, and so by induction on
m
we conclude that
D(m)=0
for all
m\geq 1
. Hence, we have
d_{m+1}(0)=2H_{m}
.
∎

3.3.
The elements
\delta_{k}\in S

In this subsection, we define
\delta_{k}(T,x)
as well as
\delta_{k}(T)
for
1\leq k\leq m+1
, which will appear as the coefficients of our strongly divisible modules. Recall that by
r^{\prime}
we mean a positive integer with
1\leq r^{\prime}<p-1
and that
m=\lfloor r^{\prime}/2\rfloor
.

Definition 3.3.1
.

Set
\delta_{0}(T,x):=-x
. For
1\leq k\leq m+1
, we set

\delta_{k}(T,x):=\frac{Q_{k,0}(T,x)}{P_{k,0}(T,x)}-x\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\delta_{k}(T):=\delta_{k}(T,0).

Note that by Lemma
3.1.16
(iii) if
k\geq 1
we have

\delta_{k}(T,x)=\frac{Q_{k-1,k-1}(T,x)}{P_{k-1,k-1}(T,x)}-x,

so in particular
\delta_{1}(T,x)=0
if
r^{\prime}=1
. Moreover, unless either
r^{\prime}=2m
and
k=m+1
or
k=0
we have

\delta_{k}(T,x)=\delta_{k}(T)

by Lemma
3.2.2
for
1\leq k\leq m
and by Lemma
3.2.3
for
k=m+1
and
r^{\prime}=2m+1
, and if
r^{\prime}=2m
and
k=m+1
then

\delta_{m+1}(T,x)=\frac{Q_{m,m}(T,0)-xQ_{m,0}(T,0)}{P_{m,m}(T,0)-xP_{m,0}(T,0)}=\frac{[\tilde{P}_{m+1,0}(T,x)Tf_{r^{\prime}}(T)]_{T}^{(r^{\prime}-1)}}{\tilde{P}_{m+1,0}(T,x)}

where the first equality follows from Lemma
3.2.2
and the second equality follows from Lemma
3.1.14
(i) if
m+1<r^{\prime}
and from Lemma
3.1.12
if
m+1=r^{\prime}
.

For the rest of this subsection, we assume
r^{\prime}>1
and investigate some necessary properties of the coefficients. In particular, we compute the values of
\delta_{k}(-1)
,
\dot{\delta}_{k}(-1)
and their differences by series of lemmas.

Lemma 3.3.2
.

Assume that
r^{\prime}\geq 2
.

(i)

If
1\leq k\leq m
, then

P_{k,0}(-1,0)=(-1)^{r^{\prime}}\frac{(r^{\prime}-k)!}{(r^{\prime}-2k)!(k-1)!}\in\mathbf{Z}_{(p)}^{\times}.

In particular,
P_{k,0}(T,0)\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}[[T+1]]^{\times}
, and so

P_{k,0}(c,0)\in(S^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}})^{\times}
and

\delta_{k}(T)=\frac{Q_{k,0}(T,0)}{P_{k,0}(T,0)}\in\mathcal{O}[[T+1]].

(ii)

If
2\leq k<r^{\prime}/2+1
, then

P_{k-1,k-1}(-1,0)=\frac{P_{k,0}(-1,0)}{a_{k}}=\frac{(r^{\prime}-2k+1)!(k-1)!}{(r^{\prime}-k)!}\in\mathbf{Z}_{(p)}^{\times}.

In particular,
P_{k-1,k-1}(T,0)\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}[[T+1]]^{\times}
and so
P_{k-1,k-1}(c,0)\in(S^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}})^{\times}
.

(iii)

If
1\leq k\leq r^{\prime}-1
, then we have

\displaystyle P_{k,k}(T,x)Q_{k,0}(T,x)-P_{k,0}(T,x)Q_{k,k}(T,x)=T^{r^{\prime}}.

Proof.

Let
1\leq k<r^{\prime}/2+1
, and consider the linear equation

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}y_{k-1}&y_{k-2}&\cdots&y_{0}\end{bmatrix}B_{k,0}=\begin{bmatrix}(-1)^{k-1}&(-1)^{k-2}&\cdots&1\end{bmatrix}.

Solving this equation for
y_{0}
gives

\displaystyle y_{0}=\begin{bmatrix}(-1)^{k-1}&(-1)^{k-2}&\cdots&1\end{bmatrix}B_{k,0}^{-1}\mathbf{e}^{\prime}_{k}=P_{k,0}(-1,0).

On the other hand, using Cramér’s rule we have

y_{0}=\frac{1}{\det(B_{k,0})}\det(B_{k,0}^{\prime})

where

\displaystyle B_{k,0}^{\prime}:=\begin{bmatrix}\frac{(-1)^{r^{\prime}-k-1}}{r^{\prime}-k}&\frac{(-1)^{r^{\prime}-k}}{r^{\prime}-k+1}&\cdots&\frac{(-1)^{r^{\prime}-3}}{r^{\prime}-2}&\frac{(-1)^{r^{\prime}-2}}{r^{\prime}-1}\\
\frac{(-1)^{r^{\prime}-k-2}}{r^{\prime}-k-1}&\frac{(-1)^{r^{\prime}-k-1}}{r^{\prime}-k}&\cdots&\frac{(-1)^{r^{\prime}-4}}{r^{\prime}-3}&\frac{(-1)^{r^{\prime}-3}}{r^{\prime}-2}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
\frac{(-1)^{r^{\prime}-2k+1}}{r^{\prime}-2k+2}&\frac{(-1)^{r^{\prime}-2k+2}}{r^{\prime}-2k+3}&\cdots&\frac{(-1)^{r^{\prime}-k-1}}{r^{\prime}-k}&\frac{(-1)^{r^{\prime}-k}}{r^{\prime}-k+1}\\
(-1)^{k-1}&(-1)^{k-2}&\cdots&(-1)&1\end{bmatrix}.

Let

M=\begin{bmatrix}\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-k}&\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-k+1}&\cdots&\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-2}&\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-1}\\
\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-k-1}&\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-k}&\cdots&\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-3}&\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-2}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-2k+2}&\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-2k+3}&\cdots&\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-k}&\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-k+1}\\
M_{k,1}&\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-2k+2}&\cdots&\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-k-1}&\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-k}\end{bmatrix},

where
M_{k,1}
is
\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-2k+1}
if
r^{\prime}-2k+1>0
and
0
otherwise,
and let
M^{\prime}
be the square matrix of size
k\times k
obtained from
M
by replacing the last row with

\begin{bmatrix}1&1&\cdots&1\end{bmatrix}.

Noting that

\displaystyle B_{k,0}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Diag}((-1)^{r^{\prime}-k-1},\cdots,(-1)^{r^{\prime}-2k+1},(-1)^{r^{\prime}-2k})\cdot M\cdot\mathrm{Diag}((-1)^{0},\cdots,(-1)^{k-1})

and

\displaystyle B_{k,0}^{\prime}=\mathrm{Diag}((-1)^{r^{\prime}-k-1},\cdots,(-1)^{r^{\prime}-2k+1},(-1)^{k-1})\cdot M^{\prime}\cdot\mathrm{Dig}((-1)^{0},\cdots,(-1)^{k-1}),

we have

(3.3.3)

P_{k,0}(-1,0)=y_{0}=(-1)^{r^{\prime}-k-1}\frac{\det M^{\prime}}{\det M}.

(i) We now compute
\frac{\det M^{\prime}}{\det M}
for
1\leq k\leq m
as follows: subtracting the
k
-th row from each row of
M
, we have

\displaystyle\det M=\begin{vmatrix}\frac{-(k-1)}{(r^{\prime}-k)(r^{\prime}-2k+1)}&\frac{-(k-1)}{(r^{\prime}-k+1)(r^{\prime}-2k+2)}&\cdots&\frac{-(k-1)}{(r^{\prime}-2)(r^{\prime}-k-1)}&\frac{-(k-1)}{(r^{\prime}-1)(r^{\prime}-k)}\\
\frac{-(k-2)}{(r^{\prime}-k-1)(r^{\prime}-2k+1)}&\frac{-(k-2)}{(r^{\prime}-k)(r^{\prime}-2k+2)}&\cdots&\frac{-(k-2)}{(r^{\prime}-3)(r^{\prime}-k-1)}&\frac{-(k-2)}{(r^{\prime}-2)(r^{\prime}-k)}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
\frac{-1}{(r^{\prime}-2k+2)(r^{\prime}-2k+1)}&\frac{-1}{(r^{\prime}-2k+3)(r^{\prime}-2k+2)}&\cdots&\frac{-1}{(r^{\prime}-k)(r^{\prime}-k-1)}&\frac{-1}{(r^{\prime}-k+1)(r^{\prime}-k)}\\
\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-2k+1}&\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-2k+2}&\cdots&\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-k-1}&\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-k}\end{vmatrix}.

Factoring out
-(k-i)
from each
i
-th row and
\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-2k+j}
from each
j
-th column, we get

\displaystyle\det M

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{k-1}(k-1)!}{(r^{\prime}-2k+1)(r^{\prime}-2k+2)\cdots(r^{\prime}-k)}\begin{vmatrix}\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-k}&\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-k+1}&\cdots&\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-2}&\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-1}\\
\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-k-1}&\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-k}&\cdots&\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-3}&\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-2}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-2k+2}&\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-2k+3}&\cdots&\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-k}&\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-k+1}\\
1&1&\cdots&1&1\end{vmatrix}

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{k-1}(r^{\prime}-2k)!(k-1)!}{(r^{\prime}-k)!}\det M^{\prime}.

Therefore, this together with (
3.3.3
) gives the desired identity of (i).

(ii) From
P_{k,0}(T,0)=\begin{bmatrix}T^{k-1}&\cdots&1\end{bmatrix}B_{k,0}^{-1}\mathbf{e}^{\prime}_{k}
, we have

\displaystyle a_{k}=(\mathbf{e}^{\prime}_{1})^{T}B_{k,0}^{-1}\mathbf{e}^{\prime}_{k}=(-1)^{k+1}\frac{\det(B_{k-1,0})}{\det(B_{k,0})}.

If we let
M^{\prime\prime}
be the square matrix of size
(k-1)\times(k-1)
obtained from
M
by deleting the first column and the last row, then it is immediate that

\displaystyle B_{k-1,0}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Diag}((-1)^{r^{\prime}-k},\cdots,(-1)^{r^{\prime}-2k+2})\cdot M^{\prime\prime}\cdot\mathrm{Diag}(1,(-1),\cdots,(-1)^{k-2}),

and so we have

\displaystyle a_{k}=(-1)^{k+1}\frac{(-1)^{r^{\prime}}\det M^{\prime\prime}}{\det M}=(-1)^{r^{\prime}+k+1}\frac{\det M^{\prime\prime}}{\det M},

which together with (
3.3.3
) and Lemma
3.1.16
(iii) implies

\displaystyle P_{k-1,k-1}(-1,0)=\frac{P_{k,0}(-1,0)}{a_{k}}=\frac{(-1)^{r^{\prime}-k-1}\frac{\det M^{\prime}}{\det M}}{(-1)^{r^{\prime}+k+1}\frac{\det M^{\prime\prime}}{\det M}}=\frac{\det M^{\prime}}{\det M^{\prime\prime}}.

We now compute this quotient as follows: subtracting the first column from each column of
M^{\prime}
, we have

\displaystyle\det M^{\prime}=\begin{vmatrix}\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-k}&\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-k+1}-\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-k}&\cdots&\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-2}-\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-k}&\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-1}-\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-k}\\
\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-k-1}&\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-k}-\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-k-1}&\cdots&\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-3}-\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-k-1}&\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-2}-\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-k-1}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-2k+2}&\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-2k+3}-\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-2k+2}&\cdots&\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-k}-\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-2k+2}&\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-k+1}-\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-2k+2}\\
1&0&\cdots&0&0\end{vmatrix}

which can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\det M^{\prime}=(-1)^{k+1}\begin{vmatrix}\frac{-1}{(r^{\prime}-k+1)(r^{\prime}-k)}&\cdots&\frac{-(k-2)}{(r^{\prime}-2)(r^{\prime}-k)}&\frac{-(k-1)}{(r^{\prime}-1)(r^{\prime}-k)}\\
\frac{-1}{(r^{\prime}-k)(r^{\prime}-k-1)}&\cdots&\frac{-(k-2)}{(r^{\prime}-3)(r^{\prime}-k-1)}&\frac{-(k-1)}{(r^{\prime}-2)(r^{\prime}-k-1)}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
\frac{-1}{(r^{\prime}-2k+3)(r^{\prime}-2k+2)}&\cdots&\frac{-(k-2)}{(r^{\prime}-k)(r^{\prime}-2k+2)}&\frac{-(k-1)}{(r^{\prime}-k+1)(r^{\prime}-2k+2)}\end{vmatrix}.

Factoring out
\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-k+1-i}
from each
i
-th row and
-j
from each
j
-th column, we get

\displaystyle\det M^{\prime}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{k+1}\frac{(-1)^{k-1}(k-1)!}{(r^{\prime}-k)(r^{\prime}-k-1)\cdots(r^{\prime}-2k+2)}\begin{vmatrix}\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-k+1}&\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-k+2}&\cdots&\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-1}\\
\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-k}&\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-k+1}&\cdots&\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-2}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-2k+3}&\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-2k+4}&\cdots&\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-k+1}\end{vmatrix}

\displaystyle=\frac{(r^{\prime}-2k+1)!(k-1)!}{(r^{\prime}-k)!}\det M^{\prime\prime}.

Therefore, we have the desired identity of (ii).

(iii) By Lemma
3.1.14
, we have

\displaystyle\deg_{T}P_{k,k}(T,x)Q_{k,0}(T,x)=r^{\prime}\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\deg_{T}P_{k,0}(T,x)Q_{k,k}(T,x)=r^{\prime}-2.

As
P_{k,k}(T,x)
and
Q_{k,0}(T,x)
are monic, we have

\displaystyle P_{k,k}(T,x)Q_{k,0}(T,x)-P_{k,0}(T,x)Q_{k,k}(T,x)

\displaystyle\hskip 30.00005pt=T^{r^{\prime}}+\big[P_{k,k}(T,x)Q_{k,0}(T,x)-P_{k,0}(T,x)Q_{k,k}(T,x)\big]_{T}^{(r^{\prime}-1)}.

On the other hand, by Lemma
3.1.14
(i) we have

\displaystyle P_{k,k}(T,x)Q_{k,0}(T,x)-P_{k,0}(T,x)Q_{k,k}(T,x)

\displaystyle\equiv P_{k,k}(T,x)P_{k,0}(T,x)(x+\log(1+T))-P_{k,0}(T,x)P_{k,k}(T,x)(x+\log(1+T))

\displaystyle\equiv 0\pmod{T^{r^{\prime}}}.

Therefore, we get the desired identity of (iii).
∎

Lemma 3.3.4
.

Assume that
r^{\prime}\geq 2
.

(i)

If
1\leq k<r^{\prime}/2
, then we have

\displaystyle\delta_{k+1}(-1)-\delta_{k}(-1)=\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-k}-\frac{1}{k}.

In particular,
\delta_{k+1}(c)-\delta_{k}(c)\in(S^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}})^{\times}
.

(ii)

If
1\leq k<r^{\prime}/2+1
, then we have

\displaystyle\delta_{k}(-1)=-H_{r^{\prime}-k}-H_{k-1}.

In particular, if
r^{\prime}=2m+1
then
Q_{m+1,0}(-1,0)=-1
.

(iii)

We have

\displaystyle\delta_{m+1}(T,x)-\delta_{m}(T)=-\frac{T^{r^{\prime}}}{P_{m,m}(T,x)P_{m,0}(T,0)}.

In particular, if
r^{\prime}=2m
and
x\in E
with
v_{p}(x)<0
, then

\displaystyle\delta_{m+1}(T,x)-\delta_{m}(T)\equiv\frac{T^{r^{\prime}}}{xP_{m,0}(T,0)^{2}}\pmod{x^{-1}\mathfrak{m}}

and so
\delta_{m+1}(-1,x)-\delta_{m-1}(-1)\equiv\delta_{m}(-1)-\delta_{m-1}(-1)\pmod{\mathfrak{m}}
.

Proof.

(i) By Lemma
3.3.2
(iii), for
1\leq k\leq m
we have

\displaystyle\delta_{k+1}(-1)-\delta_{k}(-1)

\displaystyle=\frac{Q_{k,k}(-1,0)}{P_{k,k}(-1,0)}-\frac{Q_{k,0}(-1,0)}{P_{k,0}(-1,0)}

\displaystyle=-\frac{P_{k,k}(-1,0)Q_{k,0}(-1,0)-P_{k,0}(-1,0)Q_{k,k}(-1,0)}{P_{k,0}(-1,0)P_{k,k}(-1,0)}

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{r^{\prime}+1}}{P_{k,0}(-1,0)P_{k,k}(-1,0)}.

Now, by Lemma
3.3.2
, if
1\leq k<r^{\prime}/2
, then we have

\displaystyle\delta_{k+1}(-1)-\delta_{k}(-1)

\displaystyle=-\frac{(r^{\prime}-2k)!(k-1)!}{(r^{\prime}-k)!}\frac{(r^{\prime}-k-1)!}{(r^{\prime}-2k-1)!k!}

\displaystyle=-\frac{r^{\prime}-2k}{(r^{\prime}-k)k}=\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-k}-\frac{1}{k}.

The last part of (i) follows immediately.

(ii) By Lemma
3.1.12
, we have

\displaystyle\delta_{1}(T)=\frac{Q_{0,0}(T,0)}{P_{0,0}(T,0)}=Q_{0,0}(T,0)=\sum_{n=1}^{r^{\prime}-1}\frac{(-1)^{n-1}}{n}T^{n}

and so

\displaystyle\delta_{1}(-1)=-H_{r^{\prime}-1}.

Hence, for each
1\leq k<r^{\prime}/2+1
, we have

\displaystyle\delta_{k}(-1)=-H_{r^{\prime}-1}+\sum_{n=1}^{k-1}\bigg(\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-n}-\frac{1}{n}\bigg)=-H_{r^{\prime}-k}-H_{k-1}

by applying (i) inductively.

For the second part, from the definition together with Lemma
3.1.16
(iii), we have

Q_{m+1,0}(-1,0)=P_{m+1,0}(-1,0)\delta_{m+1}(-1)=P_{m,m}(-1,0)a_{m+1}\delta_{m+1}(-1).

By Lemma
3.3.2
(ii) we have
P_{m,m}(-1,0)=1
, and by the first part of (ii) we have
\delta_{m+1}(-1)=-2H_{m}=-\frac{1}{a_{m+1}}
, where the last equality is due to Lemma
3.2.9
(v). Therefore, we have
Q_{m+1,0}(-1)=-1
.

(iii) By definition together with Lemma
3.2.2
(i), we have

\displaystyle\delta_{m+1}(T,x)-\delta_{m}(T)

\displaystyle=\frac{Q_{m,m}(T,x)}{P_{m,m}(T,x)}-x-\frac{Q_{m,0}(T,0)}{P_{m,0}(T,0)}

\displaystyle=\frac{Q_{m,m}(T,x)}{P_{m,m}(T,x)}-x-\frac{Q_{m,0}(T,x)-xP_{m,0}(T,0)}{P_{m,0}(T,0)}.

Now, by Lemma
3.3.2
(iii), we conclude the first part

\displaystyle\delta_{m+1}(T,x)-\delta_{m}(T)

\displaystyle=-\frac{P_{m,m}(T,x)Q_{m,0}(T,x)-P_{m,0}(T,x)Q_{m,m}(T,x)}{P_{m,m}(T,x)P_{m,0}(T,x)}

\displaystyle=-\frac{T^{r^{\prime}}}{P_{m,m}(T,x)P_{m,0}(T,x)}.

For the second part, we have

\displaystyle\delta_{m+1}(T,x)-\delta_{m}(T)=-\frac{T^{r^{\prime}}}{(P_{m,m}(T,0)-xP_{m,0}(T,0))P_{m,0}(T,0)}

by Lemma
3.2.2
. By Lemma
3.3.2
(i), we have
P_{m,0}(T,0)\in\mathbf{Z}_{p}[[T+1]]^{\times}
, and so we conclude that

\displaystyle\delta_{m+1}(T,x)-\delta_{m}(T)

\displaystyle=\frac{x^{-1}T^{r^{\prime}}}{P_{m,0}(T,0)(P_{m,0}(T,0)-x^{-1}P_{m,m}(T,0))}

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{x^{-1}T^{r^{\prime}}}{P_{m,0}(T,0)^{2}}\pmod{x^{-1}\mathfrak{m}},

which completes the proof.
∎

We need to compute
P_{m,m}(-1,0)
when
r^{\prime}=2m
, which is not covered by Lemma
3.3.2
.

Lemma 3.3.5
.

Let
r^{\prime}=2m\geq 2
. Then we have

\displaystyle P_{m,m}(-1,0)=Q_{m,0}(-1,0)=-m(H_{m}+H_{m-1}).

Proof.

During the proof, we write
P_{r^{\prime};k,j}(T,x)
and
Q_{r^{\prime};k,j}(T,x)
for
P_{k,j}(T,x)
and
Q_{k,j}(T,x)
, respectively, as we need to indicate
r^{\prime}
. We also write
\delta_{r^{\prime};k}
for
\delta_{k}
, to emphasize
r^{\prime}
.

We first claim that the following identity:

Q_{r^{\prime}-1;m,0}(T,0)\big(P_{r^{\prime};m,0}(T,0)+(1+T)\dot{P}_{r^{\prime};m,m}(T,0)\big)-mT^{r^{\prime}-1}\\
=P_{r^{\prime}-1;m,0}(T,0)\big(Q_{r^{\prime};m,0}(T,0)-P_{r^{\prime};m,m}(T,0)+(1+T)\dot{Q}_{r^{\prime};m,m}(T,0)\big).

By Lemma
3.1.14
, we have

\displaystyle P_{r^{\prime};m,m}(T,0)\log(1+T)\equiv Q_{r^{\prime};m,m}(T,0)\pmod{T^{r^{\prime}}}.

Differentiating both sides with respect to
T
, we have

\displaystyle\dot{P}_{r^{\prime};m,m}(T,0)\log(1+T)+P_{r^{\prime};m,m}(T,0)(1+T)^{-1}\equiv\dot{Q}_{r^{\prime};m,m}(T,0)\pmod{T^{r^{\prime}-1}},

which can be rewritten as

\displaystyle(1+T)\dot{P}_{r^{\prime};m,m}(T,0)\log(1+T)\equiv-P_{r^{\prime};m,m}(T,0)+(1+T)\dot{Q}_{r^{\prime};m,m}(T,0)\pmod{T^{r^{\prime}-1}}.

Hence, we have

(3.3.6)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&Q_{r^{\prime}-1;m,0}(T,0)\big(P_{r^{\prime};m,0}(T,0)+(1+T)\dot{P}_{r^{\prime};m,m}(T,0)\big)\\
&\equiv P_{r^{\prime}-1;m,0}(T,0)\big(P_{r^{\prime};m,0}(T,0)+(1+T)\dot{P}_{r^{\prime};m,m}(T,0)\big)\log(1+T)\\
&\equiv P_{r^{\prime}-1;m,0}(T,0)\big(Q_{r^{\prime};m,0}(T,0)-P_{r^{\prime};m,m}(T,0)+(1+T)\dot{Q}_{r^{\prime};m,m}(T,0)\big)\end{split}

modulo
(T^{r^{\prime}-1})
.

As
Q_{r^{\prime}-1;m,0}(T,0)
is a monic polynomial of degree
m-1
by Lemma
3.1.14
,
P_{r^{\prime};m,0}(T,0)
has degree
m-1
by Lemma
3.2.1
, and
P_{r^{\prime};m,m}(T,0)
is a monic polynomial of degree
m
by Lemma
3.1.14
, we conclude that the polynomial
(1+T)\dot{P}_{r^{\prime};m,m}(T,0)
has degree
m
and the leading coefficient
m
, and so the degree of the most-left side of (
3.3.6
) satisfies

\displaystyle\deg_{T}\Big(Q_{r^{\prime}-1;m,0}(T,0)\big(P_{r^{\prime};m,0}(T,0)+(1+T)\dot{P}_{r^{\prime};m,m}(T,0)\big)-mT^{r^{\prime}-1}\Big)\leq r^{\prime}-2.

On the other hand, we also have the following:

\circ

P_{r^{\prime}-1;m,0}(T,0)
has degree
m-1
by Lemma
3.1.14
and Lemma
3.2.1
;

\circ

both
Q_{r^{\prime};m,0}(T,0)
and
P_{r^{\prime};m,m}(T,0)
are monic polynomials of degree
m
by Lemma
3.1.14
, so that we have

\deg\big(Q_{r^{\prime};m,0}(T,0)-P_{r^{\prime};m,m}(T,0)\big)\leq m-1
.

\circ

Q_{r^{\prime};m,m}(T,0)
has degree less than equal to
m-1
by Lemma
3.1.14
, and so does
(1+T)\dot{Q}_{r^{\prime};m,m}(T,0)
.

Hence, we conclude that the degree of the most-right side of (
3.3.6
) satisfies

\displaystyle\deg_{T}P_{r^{\prime}-1;m,0}(T,0)\big(Q_{r^{\prime};m,0}(T,0)-P_{r^{\prime};m,m}(T,0)+(1+T)\dot{Q}_{r^{\prime};m,m}(T,0)\big)\leq r^{\prime}-2.

Therefore, the congruence (
3.3.6
) together with these degree bounds gives rise to the identity in the claim.

We now evaluate the identity in the claim at
T=-1
. Then we have

\displaystyle Q_{r^{\prime}-1;m,0}(-1,0)P_{r^{\prime};m,0}(-1,0)+m=P_{r^{\prime}-1;m,0}(-1,0)\big(Q_{r^{\prime};m,0}(-1,0)-P_{r^{\prime};m,m}(-1,0)\big).

As
Q_{r^{\prime}-1;m,0}(-1,0)=-1
by Lemma
3.3.4
(ii) and
P_{r^{\prime};m,0}(-1,0)=m
by Lemma
3.3.2
(i), the left-hand side is
0
.
Moreover, from

-1=Q_{r^{\prime}-1;m,0}(-1,0)=P_{r^{\prime}-1;m,0}(-1,0)\delta_{r^{\prime}-1;m}(-1)

we see that
P_{r^{\prime}-1;m,0}(-1,0)\not=0
, so that by Lemma
3.3.4
(ii), we conclude that

\displaystyle P_{r^{\prime};m,m}(-1,0)=Q_{r^{\prime};m,0}(-1,0)=P_{r^{\prime};m,0}(-1,0)\delta_{r^{\prime};m}(-1)=-m(H_{m}+H_{m-1}).

This completes the proof.
∎

We further compute
\dot{\delta}_{k}(-1)
. By
\dot{P}_{k,i}(-1,0)
we mean
\left.\frac{dP_{k,i}(T,0)}{dT}\right|_{T=-1}
.

Lemma 3.3.7
.

Assume that
r^{\prime}\geq 2
.

(i)

If
1\leq k<r^{\prime}/2+1
, then we have

\displaystyle\dot{\delta}_{k}(-1)=2\frac{\dot{P}_{k,0}(-1,0)}{P_{k,0}(-1,0)}+(r^{\prime}-1)=2\frac{\dot{P}_{k-1,k-1}(-1,0)}{P_{k-1,k-1}(-1,0)}+(r^{\prime}-1).

(ii)

If
1\leq k<r^{\prime}/2
, then we have

\frac{\dot{\delta}_{k+1}(-1)}{r^{\prime}(2k+1)-2k(k+1)}-\frac{\dot{\delta}_{k}(-1)}{r^{\prime}(2k-1)-2k(k-1)}\\
=-\frac{1}{r^{\prime}(2k+1)-2k(k+1)}+\frac{1}{r^{\prime}(2k-1)-2k(k-1)}.

(iii)

If
1\leq k<r^{\prime}/2+1
, then we have

\displaystyle\dot{\delta}_{k}(-1)=r^{\prime}(2k-1)-2k(k-1)-1.

(iv)

If
x\in E
with
v_{p}(x)<0
then
\delta_{m+1}(c,x)\in S^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}}
and

\dot{\delta}_{r^{\prime}-m}(-1)\equiv\dot{\delta}_{m+1}(-1,x)\pmod{\mathfrak{m}}.

Proof.

(i) It suffices to prove the equation for
P_{k,0}(-1,0)
by Lemma
3.1.16
. For brevity, let
P(T)=P_{k,0}(T,0)
and
Q(T)=Q_{k,0}(T,0)
. The condition

Q(T)\equiv P(T)\log(1+T)\pmod{T^{r^{\prime}}}
in Lemma
3.1.14
yields

\displaystyle\dot{P}(T)

\displaystyle Q(T)-P(T)\dot{Q}(T)

\displaystyle\equiv\dot{P}(T)P(T)\log(1+T)-P(T)\bigg(\dot{P}(T)\log(1+T)+P(T)\frac{1}{1+T}\bigg)

\displaystyle\equiv-\frac{P(T)^{2}}{1+T}\pmod{T^{r^{\prime}-1}},

and so

\displaystyle(1+T)\big(\dot{P}(T)Q(T)-P(T)\dot{Q}(T)\big)\equiv-P(T)^{2}\pmod{T^{r^{\prime}-1}}.

Noting that
\deg P=k-1
and
\deg Q=r^{\prime}-k
, we have

\displaystyle\deg(1+T)\big(\dot{P}(T)Q(T)-P(T)\dot{Q}(T)\big)\leq r^{\prime}-1;

\displaystyle\deg P(T)^{2}=2(k-1)\leq r^{\prime}-1,

so we may write

\displaystyle(1+T)\big(\dot{P}(T)Q(T)-P(T)\dot{Q}(T)\big)=AT^{r^{\prime}-1}-P(T)^{2}

for some suitable constant
A
. Plugging
T=-1
in, one has
A=(-1)^{r^{\prime}-1}P(-1)^{2}
, and thus

\displaystyle\dot{\delta}_{k}(T)=-\frac{\dot{P}(T)Q(T)-P(T)\dot{Q}(T)}{P(T)^{2}}=\frac{P(T)^{2}-(-1)^{r^{\prime}-1}P(-1)^{2}T^{r^{\prime}-1}}{(1+T)P(T)^{2}}.

Hence, we have

\displaystyle\dot{\delta}_{k}(-1)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{P(-1)^{2}}\bigg(\bigg.\frac{P(T)^{2}-(-1)^{r^{\prime}-1}P(-1)^{2}T^{r^{\prime}-1}}{1+T}\bigg|_{T=-1}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{P(-1)^{2}}\bigg(\bigg.\frac{d}{dT}\bigg|_{T=-1}\big(P(T)^{2}-(-1)^{r^{\prime}-1}P(-1)^{2}T^{r^{\prime}-1}\big)\bigg)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{P(-1)^{2}}\big(2P(-1)\dot{P}(-1)+(r^{\prime}-1)P(-1)^{2}\big)

\displaystyle=2\frac{\dot{P}(-1)}{P(-1)}+(r^{\prime}-1),

as desired.

(ii) By Lemma
3.3.2
(iii) together with Lemma
3.2.2
(i), we have

(3.3.8)

\delta_{k+1}(T)-\delta_{k}(T)=-\frac{T^{r^{\prime}}}{P_{k,0}(T,0)P_{k,k}(T,0)}.

Then by a direct computation we have

\displaystyle\dot{\delta}_{k+1}(T)-\dot{\delta}_{k}(T)

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dT}\bigg(-\frac{T^{r^{\prime}}}{P_{k,0}(T,0)P_{k,k}(T,0)}\bigg)

\displaystyle=-\frac{r^{\prime}T^{r^{\prime}-1}}{P_{k,0}(T,0)P_{k,k}(T,0)}+\frac{T^{r^{\prime}}}{P_{k,0}(T,0)P_{k,k}(T,0)}\bigg(\frac{\dot{P}_{k,k}(T,0)}{P_{k,k}(T,0)}+\frac{\dot{P}_{k,0}(T,0)}{P_{k,0}(T,0)}\bigg).

Now, by (i) we have

\displaystyle\dot{\delta}_{k+1}(-1)-\dot{\delta}_{k}(-1)

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{r^{\prime}}}{P_{k,0}(-1,0)P_{k,k}(-1,0)}\bigg(r^{\prime}+\frac{1}{2}\big(\dot{\delta}_{k+1}(-1)-(r^{\prime}-1)\big)+\frac{1}{2}\big(\dot{\delta}_{k}(-1)-(r^{\prime}-1)\big)\bigg)

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{r^{\prime}}}{2P_{k,0}(-1,0)P_{k,k}(-1,0)}\big(\dot{\delta}_{k+1}(-1)+\dot{\delta}_{k}(-1)+2\big).

By Lemma
3.3.4
(i) together with (
3.3.8
), we have

\displaystyle\frac{(-1)^{r^{\prime}}}{P_{k,0}(-1,0)P_{k,k}(-1,0)}=\frac{r^{\prime}-2k}{k(r^{\prime}-k)},

and so we get

\displaystyle\frac{r^{\prime}(2k-1)-2k(k-1)}{2k(r^{\prime}-k)}\dot{\delta}_{k+1}(-1)-\frac{r^{\prime}(2k+1)-2k(k+1)}{2k(r^{\prime}-k)}\dot{\delta}_{k}(-1)=\frac{r^{\prime}-2k}{k(r^{\prime}-k)}.

Noting

\displaystyle\big(r^{\prime}(2k+1)-2k(k+1)\big)-\big(r^{\prime}(2k-1)-2k(k-1)\big)=2(r^{\prime}-2k)

completes the proof.

(iii) For brevity, we set
b_{k}=r^{\prime}(2k-1)-2k(k-1)
. By (ii) we have

\displaystyle\frac{\dot{\delta}_{k+1}(-1)}{b_{k+1}}-\frac{\dot{\delta}_{k}(-1)}{b_{k}}=-\frac{1}{b_{k+1}}+\frac{1}{b_{k}}

for
1\leq k<r^{\prime}/2
. Now, noting that
b_{1}=r^{\prime}
and
\delta^{\prime}_{1}(-1)=r^{\prime}-1
by (i), we have
\frac{\delta^{\prime}_{1}(-1)}{b_{1}}=1-\frac{1}{b_{1}}
. Hence, for
1\leq k<r^{\prime}/2+1
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\dot{\delta}_{k}(-1)}{b_{k}}=1-\frac{1}{b_{1}}+\sum_{n=1}^{k-1}\bigg(-\frac{1}{b_{n+1}}+\frac{1}{b_{n}}\bigg)=1-\frac{1}{b_{k}},

which gives the desired result.

(iv) By Lemma
3.2.3
, we have

(3.3.9)

\tilde{P}_{m+1,0}(T,x)\equiv x^{-1}P_{r^{\prime}-m-1,r^{\prime}-m-1}(T,0)\pmod{x^{-1}\mathfrak{m}}

as
v_{p}(x)<0
, and so we have

\delta_{m+1}(T,x)=\frac{\tilde{Q}_{m+1,0}(T,x)}{\tilde{P}_{m+1,0}(T,x)}-x\equiv\frac{Q_{m+1,0,t-1}(T)}{P_{r^{\prime}-m-1,r^{\prime}-m-1}(T,0)}\pmod{\mathfrak{m}}.

Hence, we have
\delta_{m+1}(c,x)\in S^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}}
by Lemma
3.3.2
(ii).

By definition together with Lemma
3.1.14
, it is clear that

[P_{m+1,0}(T,x)Tf_{r^{\prime}}(T)]_{T}^{(r^{\prime}-1)}-P_{m+1,0}(T,x)\delta_{m+1}(T,x)=0.

On the other hand, by (
3.3.9
) we have

[P_{r^{\prime}-m-1,r^{\prime}-m-1}(T,0)Tf_{r^{\prime}}(T)]_{T}^{(r^{\prime}-1)}-P_{r^{\prime}-m-1,r^{\prime}-m-1}(T,0)\delta_{m+1}(T,x)\equiv 0\pmod{\mathfrak{m}},

which immediately gives rise to

(3.3.10)

P_{r^{\prime}-m-1,r^{\prime}-m-1}(T-1,0)\big(\delta_{r^{\prime}-m}(T-1)-\delta_{m+1}(T-1,x)\big)\equiv 0\pmod{\mathfrak{m}}

by Lemma
3.3.2
(ii). In particular, we have
\delta_{r^{\prime}-m}(-1)\equiv\delta_{m+1}(-1,x)\pmod{\mathfrak{m}}
. Moreover, by differentiating (
3.3.10
), we conclude the desired result.
∎

We close this subsection by computing the following: for
r^{\prime}=2m+1
, define

b_{r^{\prime},m+2}(T):=\frac{[P_{m+2,0,1}(T)Tf_{r^{\prime}}(T)]_{T}^{(r^{\prime}-1)}-P_{m+2,0,1}(T)\delta_{m}(T)}{P_{m-1,m-1}(T)},

which is the same as

b_{r^{\prime},m+2}(T)=-\frac{[P_{m+1,m+1,1}(T)Tf_{r^{\prime}}(T)]_{T}^{(r^{\prime}-1)}-P_{m+1,m+1,1}(T)\delta_{m}(T)}{P_{m,0}(T,0)}

by (
3.2.10
), (
3.2.11
), and Lemma
3.1.16
(iii).
Note that
b_{2m+1,m+2}
will appear in Proposition
4.3.7
and Proposition
4.3.10
.

Lemma 3.3.11
.

Let
r^{\prime}=2m+1\geq 3
. Then the coefficient of
T^{m}
in
Q_{m,0}(T,0)
is
-m(m+1)
, i.e.

\displaystyle Q_{m,0}(T,0)=T^{m+1}-m(m+1)T^{m}+[Q_{m,0}(T,0)]_{T}^{(m-1)}.

Moreover, we have

\displaystyle b_{r^{\prime},m+2}(T)=\frac{T^{r^{\prime}+1}-2m(m+1)T^{r^{\prime}}}{P_{m,0}(T,0)^{2}}.

In particular,
b_{r^{\prime},m+2}(-1)=-\frac{2m^{2}+2m+1}{m^{2}(m+1)^{2}}
.

Proof.

We start to prove the first part of the lemma. From
Q_{m,0}(T,0)=T^{m+1}+\begin{bmatrix}T^{m}&T^{m-1}&\cdots&1\end{bmatrix}D_{m,0}B_{m,0}^{-1}\mathbf{e}^{\prime}_{m}
,
the coefficient of
T^{m}
is given by

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}1&0&\cdots&0\end{bmatrix}D_{m,0}B_{m,0}^{-1}\mathbf{e}^{\prime}_{m}=\begin{bmatrix}1&-\frac{1}{2}&\cdots&\frac{(-1)^{m-1}}{m}\end{bmatrix}B_{m,0}^{-1}\mathbf{e}^{\prime}_{m}.

Using Cramér’s rule as in the proof of Lemma
3.3.2
(i), this is equal to

\frac{1}{\det(B_{m,0})}\det(\tilde{M}^{\prime})

where

\displaystyle\tilde{M}^{\prime}=\begin{bmatrix}\frac{(-1)^{m}}{m+1}&\frac{(-1)^{m+1}}{m+2}&\cdots&\frac{(-1)^{r^{\prime}-3}}{r^{\prime}-2}&\frac{(-1)^{r^{\prime}-2}}{r^{\prime}-1}\\
\frac{(-1)^{m-1}}{m}&\frac{(-1)^{m}}{m+1}&\cdots&\frac{(-1)^{r^{\prime}-4}}{r^{\prime}-3}&\frac{(-1)^{r^{\prime}-3}}{r^{\prime}-2}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
\frac{1}{3}&-\frac{1}{4}&\cdots&\frac{(-1)^{m}}{m+1}&\frac{(-1)^{m+1}}{m+2}\\
1&-\frac{1}{2}&\cdots&\frac{(-1)^{m-2}}{m-1}&\frac{(-1)^{m-1}}{m}\end{bmatrix}.

Let
M
,
M^{\prime}
be as in the proof of Lemma
3.3.2
(i) with
r^{\prime}=2m+1
and
k=m
, and let
\tilde{M}
be the matrix given by

\displaystyle\tilde{M}=\begin{bmatrix}\frac{1}{m+1}&\frac{1}{m+2}&\cdots&\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-2}&\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-1}\\
\frac{1}{m}&\frac{1}{m+1}&\cdots&\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-3}&\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-2}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
\frac{1}{3}&\frac{1}{4}&\cdots&\frac{1}{m+1}&\frac{1}{m+2}\\
1&\frac{1}{2}&\cdots&\frac{1}{m-1}&\frac{1}{m}\end{bmatrix}.

Then we have

\displaystyle\tilde{M}^{\prime}=\mathrm{Diag}((-1)^{m},\cdots,(-1)^{2},1)\cdot\tilde{M}\cdot\mathrm{Diag}(1,\cdots,(-1)^{m-2},(-1)^{m-1}),

and we already know that

\displaystyle B_{m,0}=\mathrm{Diag}((-1)^{m},\cdots,(-1)^{2},(-1))\cdot M\cdot\mathrm{Diag}(1,\cdots,(-1)^{m-2},(-1)^{m-1}).

Hence, the coefficient of
T^{m}
now becomes
-\frac{\det\tilde{M}}{\det M}
.

We now compute
\det\tilde{M}
: subtracting the
m
-th row from each row gives rise to

\displaystyle\det\tilde{M}=\begin{vmatrix}\frac{-m}{m+1}&\frac{-m}{(m+2)2}&\cdots&\frac{-m}{(r^{\prime}-2)(m-1)}&\frac{-m}{(r^{\prime}-1)m}\\
\frac{-(m-1)}{m}&\frac{-(m-1)}{(m+1)2}&\cdots&\frac{-(m-1)}{(r^{\prime}-3)(m-1)}&\frac{-(k-2)}{(r^{\prime}-2)m}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
\frac{-2}{3}&\frac{-2}{4\cdot 2}&\cdots&\frac{-2}{(m+1)(m-1)}&\frac{-2}{(m+2)m}\\
1&\frac{1}{2}&\cdots&\frac{1}{m-1}&\frac{1}{m}\end{vmatrix}.

Factoring out
-(m-i+1)
from each
i
-th row and
\frac{1}{j}
from each
j
-th column, we get

\displaystyle\det\tilde{M}

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{m-1}m!}{m!}\begin{vmatrix}\frac{1}{m+1}&\frac{1}{m+2}&\cdots&\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-2}&\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-1}\\
\frac{1}{m}&\frac{1}{m+1}&\cdots&\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-3}&\frac{1}{r^{\prime}-2}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
\frac{1}{3}&\frac{1}{4}&\cdots&\frac{1}{m+1}&\frac{1}{m+2}\\
1&1&\cdots&1&1\end{vmatrix}=(-1)^{m-1}\det M^{\prime}

On the other hand, we know from the proof of Lemma
3.3.2
(i) that

\displaystyle\det M=\frac{(-1)^{m-1}(r^{\prime}-2m)!(m-1)!}{(r^{\prime}-m)!}\det M^{\prime}=\frac{(-1)^{m-1}}{m(m+1)}\det M^{\prime}.

Combining these two gives

\displaystyle-\frac{\det\tilde{M}}{\det M}=-(-1)^{m-1}\frac{\det M^{\prime}}{\det M}=-m(m+1).

We now treat the second part. Note first that

(3.3.12)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&P_{m-1,m-1}(T,0)^{2}b_{r^{\prime},m+2}(T)\\
&=P_{m-1,m-1}(T,0)[P_{m+2,0,1}(T)Tf_{r^{\prime}}(T)]_{T}^{(r^{\prime}-1)}-Q_{m-1,m-1}(T,0)P_{m+2,0,1}(T).\end{split}

It is easy to see from Lemma
3.1.14
that we further have

P_{m-1,m-1}(T,0)^{2}b_{r^{\prime},m+2}(T)\equiv P_{m-1,m-1}(T,0)P_{m+2,0,1}(T)\log(1+T)\\
-P_{m-1,m-1}(T,0)\log(1+T)P_{m+2,0,1}(T)\pmod{T^{r^{\prime}}},

and so we conclude that

(3.3.13)

[P_{m-1,m-1}(T,0)^{2}b_{r^{\prime},m+2}(T)]_{T}^{(r^{\prime}-1)}=0.

We now claim that

P_{m+2,0,1}(T)=Q_{m+2,0,2}(T)-Q_{m-1,m-1}(T,0).

Indeed, looking at the coefficients of
x^{2}
in
\tilde{Q}_{m+2,0}(T,x)=\big[\tilde{P}_{m+2,0}(T,x)(x+Tf_{r^{\prime}}(T))\big]_{T}^{(r^{\prime}-1)}
from Lemma
3.1.14
(i), it easy to see that

Q_{m+2,0,2}(T)=[P_{m+2,0,2}(T)Tf_{r^{\prime}}(T)]_{T}^{(r^{\prime}-1)}+P_{m+2,0,1}(T).

By Lemma
3.2.3
,
P_{m+2,0,2}(T)=P_{m-1,m-1}(T,0)
, and so we have

[P_{m+2,0,2}(T)Tf_{r^{\prime}}(T)]_{T}^{(r^{\prime}-1)}=Q_{m-1,m-1}(T,0)

by Lemma
3.1.14
(i), which completes the proof of the claim.

By the same argument as the claim above, we have

[P_{m+2,0,1}(T)Tf_{r^{\prime}}(T)]_{T}^{(r^{\prime}-1)}=Q_{m+2,0,1}(T)-P_{m+2,0,0}(T),

which together with the claim immediately implies that from (
3.3.12
) we have

(3.3.14)

P_{m-1,m-1}(T,0)^{2}b_{r^{\prime},m+2}(T)=P_{m-1,m-1}(T,0)\big(Q_{m+2,0,1}(T)-P_{m+2,0,0}(T)\big)\\
-Q_{m-1,m-1}(T,0)\big(Q_{m+2,0,2}(T)-Q_{m-1,m-1}(T,0)\big).

On the other hand, it is easy to see from Lemma
3.1.14
that each term on the right hand side of (
3.3.14
), except the term
Q_{m-1,m-1}(T,0)^{2}
, has degree less than
r^{\prime}
, so that we have the following identity

[P_{m-1,m-1}(T,0)^{2}b_{r^{\prime},m+2}(T)]_{T}^{(r^{\prime}-1)}=P_{m-1,m-1}(T,0)\big(Q_{m+2,0,1}(T)-P_{m+2,0,0}(T)\big)\\
-Q_{m-1,m-1}(T,0)Q_{m+2,0,2}(T)+[Q_{m-1,m-1}(T,0)^{2}]_{T}^{(r^{\prime}-1)},

which together with (
3.3.13
) gives rise to

P_{m-1,m-1}(T,0)\big(Q_{m+2,0,1}(T)-P_{m+2,0,0}(T)\big)-Q_{m-1,m-1}(T,0)Q_{m+2,0,2}(T)\\
=-[Q_{m-1,m-1}(T,0)^{2}]_{T}^{(r^{\prime}-1)}.

Hence, from (
3.3.14
) we may write

\displaystyle P_{m-1,m-1}(T,0)^{2}b_{r^{\prime},m+2}(T)=Q_{m-1,m-1}(T,0)^{2}-[Q_{m-1,m-1}(T,0)^{2}]_{T}^{(r^{\prime}-1)},

which is the same as

\displaystyle P_{m-1,m-1}(T,0)^{2}b_{r^{\prime},m+2}(T)=\frac{1}{a_{m}^{2}}\big(Q_{m,0}(T,0)^{2}-[Q_{m,0}(T,0)^{2}]_{T}^{(r^{\prime}-1)}\big)

by Lemma
3.1.16
(iii). Finally, by (i) we have

\displaystyle b_{r^{\prime},m+2}(T)=\frac{T^{r^{\prime}+1}-2m(m+1)T^{r^{\prime}}}{a_{m}^{2}P_{m-1,m-1}(T,0)^{2}}=\frac{T^{r^{\prime}+1}-2m(m+1)T^{r^{\prime}}}{P_{m,0}(T,0)^{2}},

as desired. Evaluating
b_{r^{\prime},m+2}(T)
at
T=-1
, we have
b_{r^{\prime},m+2}(-1)=-\frac{2m^{2}+2m+1}{m^{2}(m+1)^{2}}
because
P_{m,0}(-1,0)=-m(m+1)
by Lemma
3.3.2
(i).
∎

4.
Pseudo-strongly divisible modules

In this section, we introduce the pseudo-strongly divisible modules, which are the main objects to be studied in this paper, and deduce the necessary conditions to become an isotypic component of the strongly divisible modules.

We keep the notation of §
3
, but we specialize
x
in
E
. In particular, we recall from §
3.1
the polynomials
P_{k,i}(T,x)
and
Q_{k,i}(T,x)
in
\mathbf{Case}~(k)
for
0\leq k\leq r^{\prime}
.

4.1.
Definitions and Summaries

In this subsection, we define the pseudo-strongly divisible modules of rank
2
and introduce various terminology that will be used throughout the paper. Moreover, we also introduce the statement of the main results on pseudo-strongly divisible modules. Throughout this subsection, we fix a pair of integers
(r,r^{\prime})
with
0<r^{\prime}\leq r<p-1
. We also define an
\mathcal{O}
-submodule of
E((T+1))

E(T)_{\Delta}:=E(T)\cap(E+\mathcal{O}[[T+1]]).

Let
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}:=S^{\prime}_{E}(E^{\prime}_{1},E^{\prime}_{2})
be the free
S^{\prime}_{E}
-module of rank
2
whose basis is denoted by
E_{1}^{\prime},E_{2}^{\prime}
, and let
k
be an integer with
0\leq k\leq r^{\prime}
and
(x,\Theta)\in E\times E^{\times}
. For all
0\leq i\leq r^{\prime}-1
, we define certain elements in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}

(4.1.1)

F^{\prime}_{k,i}:=\begin{cases}p^{i+r^{\prime}-k}\Theta P_{k,i}\Big(\dfrac{v}{p},x\Big)E^{\prime}_{1}+p^{i+r^{\prime}-k}Q_{k,i}\Big(\dfrac{v}{p},x\Big)E^{\prime}_{2}&\mbox{if }0\leq i\leq k-1;\\
p^{i}P_{k,i}\Big(\dfrac{v}{p},x\Big)E^{\prime}_{1}+\dfrac{p^{i}}{\Theta}Q_{k,i}\Big(\dfrac{v}{p},x\Big)E^{\prime}_{2}&\mbox{if }k\leq i\leq r^{\prime}-1.\end{cases}

In particular, if
r^{\prime}=1
then
F^{\prime}_{0,0}=E^{\prime}_{1}+\frac{x}{\Theta}E^{\prime}_{2}
and
F^{\prime}_{1,0}=\frac{\Theta}{x}E^{\prime}_{1}+E^{\prime}_{2}
.

Definition 4.1.2
.

Fix
k\in([0,r^{\prime}]\cap\mathbf{Z})\cup\{\infty\}
. For given
(\Lambda,\Theta,\Omega,x)\in(E^{\times})^{3}\times E
and
\Delta(T),\tilde{\Delta}(T)\in E(T)_{\Delta}
, a
pseudo-strongly divisible module in
\mathbf{Case}~(k)
consists of quadruple

(\mathfrak{M}^{\prime},\,\mathrm{Fil}^{r;r^{\prime}}_{k}\mathfrak{M}^{\prime},\,\phi^{\prime},\,N^{\prime})

where

\circ

\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}:=S^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}}(E^{\prime}_{1},E^{\prime}_{2})
is the
S^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}}
-submodule of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
generated by
E_{1}^{\prime},E_{2}^{\prime}
,

\circ

\mathrm{Fil}^{r;r^{\prime}}_{k}\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
is an
S^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}}
-submodule of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
defined by

\mathrm{Fil}^{r;r^{\prime}}_{k}\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}:=[\mathrm{Fil}^{r:r^{\prime}}_{k}\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}]^{(r-1)}+\mathrm{Fil}^{r}S^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}}\cdot\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}

where
[\mathrm{Fil}^{r:r^{\prime}}_{k}\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}]^{(r-1)}
is a
S^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}}
-submodule of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
generated by

(4.1.3)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\{v^{r-r^{\prime}}F^{\prime}_{k,i}\mid 0\leq i\leq r^{\prime}-1\}&\hbox{if $k\in[0,r^{\prime}]\cap\mathbf{Z}$;}\\
\{v^{r-r^{\prime}}E^{\prime}_{2}\}&\hbox{if $k=\infty$,}\end{array}\right.

\circ

\phi^{\prime}:\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
is a continuous
\phi
-semilinear map induced by

(4.1.4)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\phi^{\prime}(E^{\prime}_{1})&:=p\Lambda E^{\prime}_{1}-\frac{\Lambda}{\Omega}\big(x+\Delta(\gamma-1)\big)E^{\prime}_{2}+\frac{\Lambda}{p\Omega}\big(\tilde{\Delta}(\varphi(\gamma)-1)-\tilde{\Delta}(-1)\big)E^{\prime}_{2};\\
\phi^{\prime}(E^{\prime}_{2})&:=\frac{\Lambda\Theta}{\Omega}E^{\prime}_{2},\end{split}

\circ

N^{\prime}:\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
is a continuous
\mathcal{O}
-linear derivation defined by

(4.1.5)

\displaystyle\begin{split}N^{\prime}(E^{\prime}_{1})&:=\frac{1}{\Theta}\bigg(1+\frac{N(\gamma)}{p}\dot{\tilde{\Delta}}(\gamma-1)\bigg)E^{\prime}_{2};\\
N^{\prime}(E^{\prime}_{2})&:=0.\end{split}

We write
\phi^{\prime}_{r}:=\frac{1}{p^{r}}\phi^{\prime}:\mathrm{Fil}^{r;r^{\prime}}_{k}\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
.

Remark 4.1.6
.

\circ

We point out that once
r,r^{\prime},k
are fixed, the pseudo-strongly divisible modules in
\mathbf{Case}~(k)
are parameterized by
(\Lambda,\Theta,\Omega,x)\in(E^{\times})^{3}\times E
.

\circ

If
0\leq k\leq r^{\prime}
then the choice of the generators in the filtration
\mathrm{Fil}^{r;r^{\prime}}_{k}\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
in
\mathbf{Case}~(k)
is induced from the choice of the generators for
\mathbb{M}
in
\mathbf{Case}~(k)
(see (
3.1.1
)), as we will see in §
5.1
. Hence, it is harmless to use the notation
\mathbf{Case}~(k)
for both of them.

\circ

The choice of the generators in the filtration
\mathrm{Fil}^{r;r^{\prime}}_{k}\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
in
\mathbf{Case}~(k)
for
k=\infty
is induced from the case
\mathfrak{L}_{j}=\infty
, as we will see in §
5.1
.

We wish that a pseudo-strongly divisible module is an isotypic component of the strongly divisible modules, and so it is quite natural to require that the images of
\phi^{\prime}
and
N^{\prime}
are contained in
\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
.

Lemma 4.1.7
.

(i)

\phi^{\prime}(\mathfrak{M}^{\prime})
and
N^{\prime}(\mathfrak{M}^{\prime})
are contained in
\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
if and only if

(4.1.8)

\begin{cases}v_{p}(\Lambda)\geq-1;\\
v_{p}(\Lambda)+v_{p}(x+\Delta(-1))\geq v_{p}(\Omega);\\
v_{p}(\Lambda)+v_{p}(\Theta)\geq v_{p}(\Omega);\\
v_{p}(\Theta)\leq 0.\end{cases}

(ii)

N^{\prime}(E_{1}^{\prime})\in\mathfrak{m}\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
if and only if
v_{p}(\Theta)<0
.

Proof.

The proof is straightforward from the definition, as we have

(4.1.9)

p^{p-1}\mid\big(\tilde{\Delta}(\phi(\gamma)-1)-\tilde{\Delta}(-1)\big)

together with
\Delta(T),\tilde{\Delta}(T)\in E(T)_{\Delta}
.
∎

We will also determine the precise conditions under which
\mathrm{Fil}^{r;r^{\prime}}_{k}\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
becomes a
S^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}}
-submodule of
\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
, which is the necessary property to become an isotypic component of strongly divisible modules.

Theorem 4.1.10
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
be a pseudo-strongly divisible module in
\mathbf{Case}~(k)
for
k\in([0,r^{\prime}]\cap\mathbf{Z})\cup\{\infty\}
. Assume that
v_{p}(\Theta)\leq 0
, and further that
v_{p}(x)<0
for
m+1\leq k\leq r^{\prime}-1
. Then
\mathrm{Fil}_{k}^{r;r^{\prime}}\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
is a
S^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}}
-submodule of
\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
if and only if the inequalities

\begin{cases}v_{p}(\Theta)\leq v_{p}(x),\,\,v_{p}(\Theta)\leq-r^{\prime}+1&\mbox{if }k=0;\\
v_{p}(\Theta)\leq v_{p}(x),\,\,-r^{\prime}+2k-1\leq v_{p}(\Theta)\leq-r^{\prime}+2k+1&\mbox{if }1\leq k<\frac{r^{\prime}}{2};\\
v_{p}(\Theta)-2v_{p}(x)\leq 1,\,\,-1\leq v_{p}(\Theta)&\mbox{if }r^{\prime}=2m=2k;\\
v_{p}(x)\leq v_{p}(\Theta),\,\,2k-r^{\prime}-1\leq v_{p}(\Theta)-2v_{p}(x)\leq 2k-r^{\prime}+1&\mbox{if }m+1\leq k\leq r^{\prime}-1;\\
v_{p}(x)\leq v_{p}(\Theta),\,\,v_{p}(\Theta)-2v_{p}(x)\geq r^{\prime}-1&\mbox{if }k=r^{\prime};\\
\mbox{none}&\mbox{if }k=\infty\end{cases}

hold, in which case we have
